BT · BT.A-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 16 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: BT

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 46,567 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces that act on BT's cash flows improved more in the last three months than at any point in the last 18, while the external force that acts on BT's valuation worsened. At the value-chain level, each of the three external threats the September memo named moved in BT's favour: Ofcom's final decision imposed no margin constraint; the scaled nexfibre-Netomnia wholesale challenger is provisionally blocked and VMO2 has stopped building; and TalkTalk, the largest defection risk, is now inside BT. Line losses are falling (175k in Q2 FY27). The end of the legacy-voice drag in January 2027 is the one dated demand inflection, and it supports a flat-to-slightly-positive service revenue path from FY28 that consensus barely credits. Against this, retail competition is intensifying (mobile virtual network operators, fixed wireless, alt-net Black Friday pricing, Starlink at £32), the risk of a scaled independent wholesaler survives in a different form (CityFibre-Netomnia), labour and energy inputs inflate, and the discount rate (gilts about 5.3%) is the force that sets the multiple for a leveraged, pension-carrying yield stock. For the second set: model a lower Openreach line-loss path than the 800k guide (a defensible reduction from FY28 as TalkTalk's deliberate migration off Openreach stops), Consumer broadband ARPU flat to slightly down, voice drag ending in Q4 FY27, a small energy headwind in FY28, and the multiple against a high-yield regime rather than a falling one.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0258C-0546C-0734C-0963C-3548C-3963
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

B+585th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is diverging by layer. At wholesale, where most of BT's value sits, intensity has eased materially in the last three months; at retail, it is intensifying. Because Openreach earns roughly half of group EBITDA at an infrastructure margin, the net change is positive for the case, but it is not a one-way improvement and the wholesale threat has been delayed and reshaped rather than removed.

  • Wholesale, easing (the more valuable layer).
  • The CMA provisionally found the nexfibre-Netomnia deal would substantially lessen competition; nexfibre declined to offer remedies; the statutory deadline is 15 December 2026. The scaled 8m-premise (13m with VMO2's cable) challenger the memo feared is now likely blocked.
  • VMO2 (leverage 5.7x, bonds below par, £0.8bn cost reset by 2029) has halted its fibre upgrade beyond existing commitments (footprint 9.4m in Q3 2026) and is discussing wholesaling Openreach in rural areas, turning a would-be wholesale rival into a possible customer.
  • TalkTalk, the most likely large defector, is now owned by BT.
  • Alt-net construction velocity is down at least 40%; toob's equity was written to zero; G.Network went into administration; CityFibre cut 18% of staff and sought £900m.
  • Result: Openreach broadband line losses fell to 175k in Q2 FY27 (consensus 198k), from 192k in Q1 and 203k in Q4 FY26.
  • But the wholesale risk is reshaped, not gone. The CMA's counterfactual is that CityFibre would have bought Netomnia's parent, and a report of 3 October 2026 speaks of CityFibre's acquisition of Netomnia being approved. A CityFibre-Netomnia combination (about 4.7m plus about 3.2m to 3.4m premises) would create a scaled independent wholesaler without VMO2's anchor volumes. Sky continues to multi-source over CityFibre (2.5Gbps at £35, 5Gbps at £45). The 1.0m third-party lines on PlatformX may now prefer a non-BT wholesaler.
  • Retail, intensifying. Black Friday discounting by Grain and Brsk on symmetric gigabit; VodafoneThree marketing 5G home broadband and targeting a doubling of its base to over 4m; mobile virtual network operators pricing aggressively (AO Mobile £12 for 500GB; Sky Mobile entry tariff £1; Monzo up to 30% discounts; Lebara from £5); Starlink cut its 100Mbps UK service to £32 a month; Fibrus offering termination-fee credits to poach TalkTalk customers. BT's Consumer broadband net additions turned to -7k in H1 FY27 and Consumer broadband ARPU fell 3% to £40.8.
  • Mobile. VodafoneThree is complete (about 29m customers, an £11bn network plan, CMA wholesale-access remedies that open cheap mobile routes); EE nonetheless added 86k postpaid customers in H1, its best first half in four years, with mobile ARPU +3% to £19.8.

For the model: a lower wholesale line-loss path than the 800k guide is now defensible (see C-06 and C-33), while Consumer broadband ARPU should be modelled flat to slightly down rather than rising with the annual price rise.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C-4618th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a mild net headwind in FY27 to FY28, because labour (the dominant input) is inflating and energy hedges roll off, partly offset by the mechanical deflation that comes with passing the build peak. Input availability is not a constraint; supplier concentration is a standing but stable risk.

  • Labour dominates. Gross staff costs are about 70% of EBITDAaL, so a 5% pay rise consumes up to 4% of EBITDA. Employer National Insurance and living-wage rises cost about £100m a year (already in the base since April 2025, about 70% in operating cost); UK minimum wage rises of about 10% are cited by an expert; wage settlements have been agreed with the unions (Morgan Stanley), giving near-term visibility. 77% of UK employees are represented by the Communication Workers Union or Prospect.
  • Energy. About £500m a year (Jefferies estimated about £450m after power purchase agreements); 88% of UK network electricity hedged for FY27 and 52% for FY28; the aim was 95% for FY27. Network energy use fell 7% to 8%. With UK energy prices forecast higher and oil volatile, the FY28 hedge roll-off is a plausible £20m to £50m headwind (our judgement; not quantified in the files).
  • Build inputs easing. Fibre unit cost held within £250 to £350 per premise; management now says just above the midpoint of that range, up from "about £300, bottom half" earlier in 2026, which is a small adverse drift as the build moves to harder rural and multi-dwelling premises. Openreach engineering headcount is moving from a peak of 38,000 toward about 30,000; contractor rates are moderating; large operators get priority fibre-cable allocation (a channel check names Corning prioritising larger providers).
  • Concentration. Nokia and Ericsson are the dual radio and core suppliers; the Huawei swap is about £100m a year of incremental capital expenditure over six years; handset supply is Apple and Samsung. Stable.
  • Working capital lever on suppliers. A £350m bills-of-exchange facility (£215m used) and a separate £237m early-payment programme; the risks report treats their growth as a 'false friend' in cash flow.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3441st of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the macro force is the discount rate, and it bites hardest at the company level through BT's leverage and pension, then at the sector level through the market's treatment of telecoms as a yield group. The real-economy channel (consumer squeeze) is secondary. A further point for the model: BT's switch from inflation-linked to fixed money-amount price rises makes its retail pricing less protected if UK inflation re-accelerates.

  • Rates and the discount rate. UK 10-year gilt about 5.34% (28 October 2026); a Bank of England rise to 4.00% reported on 5 November 2026; US 10-year near 5%. The Industry Digest: telecom shares trade as yield stocks; the group's 24-month-forward multiple fell 12% from May to September 2026 while 2027 estimates held; telecoms read the lowest grade of relative strength on 13 November 2026; the Fitness for Fighting digest holds Telecoms at Underweight with recovery tied to a US 10-year under 4.5%.
  • Company-level transmission. Net debt £21.0bn including leases; net interest about £1.0bn (adjusted net finance expense +11% to £1,060m in FY26 as cheap debt was refinanced); 90% of sterling debt fixed; maturities £372m FY27, £1,013m FY28, £1,296m FY29. A 5% fall in enterprise value is about a 10% fall in equity value (Morgan Stanley). Higher gilt yields reduced the pension's actuarial deficit (to £2.7bn), but the scheme is over-hedged on an IAS 19 basis and exposed to collateral calls in a violent gilt move.
  • Real economy. Inflation 3.5% (September 2026), Morgan Stanley sees a 4.9% peak in Q2 2027; 2027 UK growth cut to 1.0%; the energy price cap forecast to rise about 16% in January. Telecom demand is low-cyclicality; spin-down is the risk.
  • Pricing nuance. Openreach copper and anchor products remain CPI-indexed (3.6% in FY27), so wholesale is inflation-protected; retail moved to fixed pounds-and-pence rises, which are durable (the Sector Digest calls a flat money rise "the durable form") but lose real value when inflation rises, as it now is.
  • Currency. Neutral: about 89% of revenue was sterling in FY25 and International is now discontinued.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–63

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the dominant other dynamic is competitor balance-sheet distress, and it is a net positive for BT. TalkTalk's collapse handed BT its largest wholesale customer; VMO2's debt stress has halted its fibre build; the alt-net sector's £9bn to £10bn of debt against about £500m of revenue is forcing retrenchment.

  • Positive. TalkTalk (leverage over 20x, CCC-, 2028 maturity wall) went into administration and BT bought it; VMO2 (about £22bn of debt; leverage 5.7x; revenue -7.9% in Q2 2026) is cutting capital expenditure and may rent Openreach; alt-net administrations (G.Network, Trooli), toob's equity at zero, Truespeed-Freedom Fibre merger. Vodafone's new largest shareholder (Vega, 19.9%) pushes for cost cuts, reinforcing sector cost discipline.
  • Negative. A distressed alt-net emerging debt-free could price below Openreach's regulated tariffs (Rothschild & Co Redburn); competitors are actively targeting TalkTalk customers; the AI-agent narrative and the satellite question cap sector multiples; strategic holders (Bharti 24.95%, Deutsche Telekom 12.06%) are a potential supply overhang.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D4826th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: entry is active in all three forms but the threat to BT's earnings in the next 18 months is moderate; the most serious is the adjacent entrant (a consolidated alt-net or cable operator acting as a national wholesaler).

  • Adjacent entrants, moderate-to-high threat over three years. VMO2's fixed wholesale unit and nexfibre (now constrained); CityFibre as a consolidator; mobile operators entering home broadband by fixed wireless (VodafoneThree to over 4m customers targeted); aggregators routing traffic across alt-nets.
  • Standard entrants, low-to-moderate threat. Mobile virtual network operators mandated by the VodafoneThree remedies (Monzo, Revolut, Octopus, AO, Lebara; Tesco in discussion for white-label broadband); regional alt-net mergers.
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants, low threat near term. Starlink at £32 a month and qualifying for the universal service obligation; Amazon Kuiper; SpaceX's possible mobile route through the VodafoneThree wholesale remedy (Morgan Stanley, 12 October 2026); Digi's 51% of WhyFibre at £25 for 10Gbps.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

A631st of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: a support. Terms eased as the build passed its peak, wages were settled with the unions, energy is largely hedged for FY27, and BT's scale secures materials that smaller rivals struggle to obtain; concentration in Nokia and Ericsson is the standing risk.

  • Fibre build cost within £250 to £350 per premise; contractor rates moderating; Openreach uses contractors for easy builds and its own engineers for hard rural builds.
  • Long-term software partnerships with Ericsson (dual-mode 5G core) and Nokia; Huawei swap about £100m a year of capital expenditure.
  • Energy 88% to 95% hedged for FY27, 52% for FY28; long-term power purchase agreements into the 2030s.
  • Wage agreements reached with the Communication Workers Union and Prospect.
  • Supply-chain finance (£350m facility, £215m used) and early-payment programmes (£237m) give working-capital flexibility.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0146C-0356C-0456C-0852C-3256C-3757C-4332
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F4633rd of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: aggregate UK demand for connectivity is flat and highly visible; what is changing is the mix inside it, and the one dated demand inflection that matters for BT's reported revenue is the end of the legacy-voice drag at the PSTN switch-off on 31 January 2027. There is no demand explosion and no cyclical swing with two-plus years of visibility; there is a structural, contract-backed migration that BT partly captures and partly loses.

  • Flat volume. The UK retail broadband market has been flat at 28.6m active lines for four years (to FY26). European telecom revenue grows about 1.5% a year, below inflation. Mobile data traffic grows about 20% a year but is not monetised as volume.
  • Mix shift is the demand change. Copper to fibre: Openreach take-up rose to 41% (H1 FY27) from 38% (FY26), 10m premises connected; Consumer full-fibre base 58% of its broadband base; Business full-fibre connections +29% to 356k. Fixed-mobile convergence 27.1% of Consumer households (+1.2 points). Legacy voice to IP: 1.1m PSTN migrations in H1 FY27, about 1m still to go before 31 January 2027; units on legacy networks down 69% to 0.9m.
  • The voice drag ends, and that is the revenue hinge. Legacy voice has taken £150m to £200m a year out of service revenue (about one percentage point; H1 FY27 service revenue -1% was "entirely" voice; Business service revenue was +1% excluding voice). When it stops, UK service revenue moves from about -1% to about flat or slightly positive, which is what underpins management's "growth from FY28". Consensus has FY28 revenue +0.1% and FY29 +0.9%, so the market gives little credit for it.
  • Cyclicality is low but not zero. Bernstein: connectivity ranks high among household priorities, but cost-of-living pressure drives "spin-down" to cheaper tiers. UK inflation was 3.5% in September 2026 with Morgan Stanley expecting a peak near 4.9% in Q2 2027; real disposable income is flat to slightly negative. Enterprise IT spending is cautious, though BT Business reports high-double-digit order-book growth and public-sector wins (Ministry of Justice, Royal Mail, Scottish Water).
  • Visibility is high because the base is subscription and contract revenue with low churn (1.1% broadband, 1.0% postpaid mobile a month) and the switch-off date is fixed.

For the model: treat aggregate volume as flat; build service revenue from (a) voice drag of £150m to £200m in FY27, falling to near zero from FY28, (b) Openreach ARPU growth, (c) line losses, (d) Consumer ARPU and convergence. The risk to the switch-off date (1m lines to migrate in about two months, including vulnerable users) is a timing risk to when the drag ends, not to whether.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C5616th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the disruption forces acting on demand (satellite, fixed wireless, artificial-intelligence switching agents) are strengthening at the edges but are not yet material to BT's numbers, while the disruption force acting on cost (AI and automation) is strengthening and acts in BT's favour. Net, disruption is a mild positive for the next 18 months and a terminal-value question beyond it.

  • Satellite. Starlink cut its UK 100Mbps service to £32 a month (Deutsche Bank, 6 November 2026), meets Ofcom's universal service obligation thresholds and is "highly unhelpful for Openreach" in rural areas. Against that, Bernstein (24 September 2026) judges low-Earth-orbit broadband a limited threat in dense European markets; BT partners with Starlink for remote premises, which relieves Openreach of costly rural build. Effect: a rural edge loss, partly a cost saving.
  • Fixed wireless. VodafoneThree has extended 5G fixed wireless to a further 3.7m premises and targets over 4m broadband customers. Management argues UK fibre pricing leaves no price umbrella for fixed wireless, unlike the United States. Effect: pressure on entry-tier and suburban price-sensitive households.
  • AI switching agents. The sector narrative (Barclays, 20 October 2026: 4% to 7% of revenue and 8% to 13% of EBITDAaL at risk from agents and satellites) is now a cap on any telecom re-rating. BT's exposure is moderated by low churn and convergence, but a premium-priced incumbent has more revenue at risk than a discounter.
  • AI on cost (positive). The labour target was cut to 65,000 to 70,000 by FY30; total labour resource fell 11% to 90,000 in H1 FY27; an internal AI agent cut bid preparation from 14 man-weeks to 3 man-days; the virtual assistant handles 60,000 conversations a week; 5,000 developers use AI coding tools. Morgan Stanley (11 November 2026) says AI will drive deep opex savings ahead of consensus.
  • Network technology. Symmetric XGS-PON alt-nets out-speed BT's GPON at the top tier (5Gbps to 10Gbps); BT is trialling XGS-PON. The first UK 5G standalone network slicing service ('Fast Lane') is a small monetisation option.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C+5614th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: regulation moved from the low point of July 2026 to a broadly supportive settlement in September and October, and a new regulatory risk (the CMA's public interest review of the TalkTalk purchase) has replaced the old one. The framework to 2031 is stable and lets Openreach earn above its cost of capital; the regulator's stance toward Openreach's pricing tactics is restrictive; the competition authority is, for now, protecting the incumbent's wholesale position by blocking challenger consolidation.

  • Ofcom, supportive in framework, restrictive in tactics. The Telecoms Access Review 2026-31 keeps the 'fair bet' and no cost-based controls in 86% of premises; the final decision of 28 September 2026 ordered withdrawal of the 'Incremental New to Openreach' offer (up to £9.50 a month for 30 months) but approved the Frontbook ARPU Share offer (£19.32 cap) and the £50 geographic bounty in VMO2 areas, and set no margin-squeeze cap (the memo's invalidation trigger did not fire). The anchor product was re-based to 80Mbps at £17.17 (from £22.37), costing about £100m of FY27 working capital and depressing H1 cash. Copper pricing may rise from 1 April 2029 in exchanges where 90% of premises have ultrafast coverage (threshold lowered from 100%). The notice period for commercial offers rose from 90 to 120 days.
  • CMA, two cases pulling opposite ways. nexfibre-Netomnia: provisional finding against (helpful). TalkTalk: public interest review of BT's purchase; competitors object to BT's retail broadband share rising from about 31% to about 36% (Deutsche Bank, calculated); VMO2's chief executive has demanded divestment of TalkTalk's wholesale operations. Possible outcomes range from clearance to behavioural undertakings to a forced sale of PlatformX.
  • Government. Full expensing of capital allowances is permanent (cash tax deferral; the zero UK cash tax position ends from FY28 as capital expenditure falls); Project Gigabit's up to £1.2bn rural contract to Openreach lowers net capital cost; the employer National Insurance increase (in the base since April 2025) and minimum wage rises raise labour cost; the government caps Bharti at 24.95%; business rates of about £500m a year, two thirds on Openreach, are a standing political exposure.
  • Long horizon. Bernstein projects Openreach's regulated return could peak above 15% around 2031 before re-basing toward 8% to 10% by 2035 under future charge controls; this is a terminal-value question, not an 18-month one.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F5232nd of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: no industry driver is accelerating BT's aggregate demand; three are accelerating the value of its existing demand. The fibre migration, convergence and the forced PSTN and copper retirement raise revenue per line and lower cost per line on a flat base.

  • Fibre migration. Record FTTP net additions (574k in Q1 FY27; take-up 41% by Q2), Openreach broadband ARPU £17.8 (+7%) on CPI indexation and higher-speed mix. Stop-sell of copper applies in 1,572 exchanges covering 15.4m premises, which forces migration. Each fibre migration lowers fault rates to about one third of copper's.
  • Convergence. 27.1% of Consumer households, rising about 1 point a year; converged customers churn less.
  • Copper and PSTN retirement. Removes legacy voice revenue (a drag) but also dual-running cost and frees exchanges (5,500 toward about 1,000) and copper for sale (forward sales of £105m and £99m; up to 200,000 tonnes through the 2030s).
  • Business digitalisation, sovereign cloud and AI connectivity. Real but small against a £5bn Business revenue line; UK ICT sector value added +8.1% year on year in mid-2026 (Morgan Stanley).
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5626th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports the cash thesis (stable retail base, record fibre take-up, strong mobile additions) and does not support a revenue-growth thesis; the support is moderately robust.

  • Supporting. FTTP take-up 41%; 10m Openreach premises connected; Openreach ARPU +7%; postpaid mobile +86k in H1 (best in four years) with ARPU +3%; convergence 27.1%; churn stable; Business order book high-double-digit growth with public-sector wins; Group NPS 33.4.
  • Not supporting. Consumer broadband net additions -7k (connection charges); Consumer broadband ARPU -3%; retail price-sensitivity rising (an expert: decisions now driven by price and reliability, not brand); consumer real incomes squeezed.
  • Fragility. Low on the base (contracts, convergence, low churn); moderate on ARPU (front-book competition; the AI-agent narrative of easier switching).
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C+5713th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: on balance they help more than they did in July, through a settled wholesale framework, a competition authority blocking challenger consolidation, full expensing and rural subsidy; the fragility is concentrated in the CMA's TalkTalk review and in the regulator's restrictive stance on Openreach pricing tactics.

  • Helping. The 'fair bet' to 2031; no cost-based controls in 86% of premises; copper price freedom from April 2029 at 90% coverage; final decision of 28 September 2026 with no margin cap; the CMA's provisional finding against nexfibre-Netomnia; permanent full expensing; Project Gigabit up to £1.2bn; Starlink accepted for the universal service obligation (relieves rural build).
  • Hurting. The anchor product at £17.17 (about £100m of FY27 working capital); the blocked £9.50 offer and 120-day notice period; the TalkTalk review; labour taxes; business rates; the cap on Bharti.
  • Fragility. Moderate: a decision against BT on TalkTalk would partly unwind the counterparty de-risking; a post-2031 review could re-base returns.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F3240th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the addressable market is mature and BT already holds a third of it; it allows nominal revenue growth of about 0% to 2% a year over the mid term, not more. Growth must come from price and mix, not volume.

  • UK broadband 28.6m lines, flat for four years; BT about 36% of retail after TalkTalk and about 80% of wholesale lines.
  • European telecom revenue growth about 1.5% a year, below inflation; Bernstein and Barclays debate whether long-run sector growth is 0.5% or 1% to 2%.
  • Adjacencies (UK sovereign cloud and AI, cyber, IoT, 5G slicing) are real but small against a £17bn base.
  • Openreach can grow revenue faster than the market (ARPU +7%) only while indexation and speed mix outrun line losses.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0662C-1053
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C6216th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: BT's own cycle is turning on two fronts at once: the capital cycle turned down decisively in FY27 (high confidence), and the revenue cycle is at a trough and beginning to turn (moderate confidence), with the first total-revenue growth in two and a half years in H1 FY27, though driven by handsets. The turn in line losses is the most under-appreciated part of it.

  • Capital cycle, turned. Build fell to 1.2m premises in H1 FY27 (4.8m in FY26); footprint 24.1m; the 25m target slipped from December 2026 to March 2027; accrued capital expenditure -5% in H1, cash -1% (capital creditors settled later). Guidance implies a second-half cash capital expenditure of about £1.9bn to £2.0bn against about £2.6bn in H2 FY26 (our arithmetic from H1 FY27 £2,518m and FY26 £5,169m), so the step-down is back-loaded into H2 FY27.
  • Revenue cycle, at the trough. Reported revenue fell in six of the seven years to FY26 (FY20 £22.8bn to FY26 £19.6bn, partly disposals). H1 FY27 continuing revenue +1% (handset-led; Q2 revenue £4,428m beat consensus by 2.5%), service revenue -1%. With voice drag ending in January 2027, service revenue should stabilise in FY28.
  • Line-loss cycle, turning. Openreach losses: 825k FY26; 203k Q4 FY26; 192k Q1 FY27; 175k Q2 FY27. H1 FY27 is 367k, an annualised run-rate of about 735k against an 800k guide that implies a worse H2. With TalkTalk under BT's control since 5 October 2026 (it had driven more than half of line losses), the structural rate should fall further in FY28, though churn of TalkTalk retail customers to alt-net ISPs will continue (competitors are targeting them). Our judgement: FY27 line losses below 800k and FY28 materially lower are more likely than not; this is the main upside lever on Openreach revenue the consensus may not hold.
  • Earnings cycle, flat. EBITDA +1% in H1; Openreach EBITDA +4% on fibre mix and cost; Business -7%; Consumer -1% (management expects Consumer roughly flat for the year).
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D5325th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: BT is the purest example in the sector of the theme the market rewards (capital expenditure falling on an owned network), and it now also benefits from blocked challenger consolidation and competitor distress; but it carries the attributes the market is currently punishing (leverage, a pension, a high valuation in its own range, and a UK discount rate near 5%). The external picture is a tailwind at the value-chain level, mixed at the industry level and a headwind at the sector and market level.

  • Thematic drivers, one by one (from the Sector and Industry Digests of 15 October 2026).
  • Capital expenditure falling on owned networks ('Big'; BT named first): strong tailwind. BT owns Openreach, so the fall is real cash, not cash moved into rent.
  • Closed mergers ('Big'): UK mobile 4-to-3 (VodafoneThree) supports rational mobile pricing but creates a stronger EE rival; UK fixed wholesale consolidation is being blocked, which helps BT. Mildly positive.
  • New owners forcing costs out ('Early'): BT is self-driven rather than owner-forced; the AI labour target is aggressive. Neutral to positive.
  • Price rises written into contracts ('Early, a worry clearing'): BT's pounds-and-pence model is the durable form, though Openreach's front-book is capped and Consumer ARPU fell. Mildly positive.
  • Satellite ('clearing for network operators'): mild headwind in rural UK.
  • Bond yields and the AI-agent cap on re-rating: headwind for the whole group, and BT's leverage makes it the "leveraged defensive that de-rates first" (Wisdom Library, 6 November 2026).
  • At each level. Value chain: tailwind (alt-net and VMO2 distress; TalkTalk internalised; Ofcom settled). Industry: mixed (retail discounting and mobile virtual network operators against wholesale relief). Sector and market: headwind (Telecoms Underweight; relative strength F; the Sector Digest explicitly says a stock high in its own range, "such as BT at the 82nd percentile, needs the cash itself").

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

BT's internal change is broad, deep and resourced, and since the September memo it has moved from executing a plan to actively removing the plan's tail risks. The two new actions, buying TalkTalk to stop the largest wholesale migration and settling the pension triennial early with a distribution framework, show a management that adapts when a lever is blocked (Ofcom stopped defence by price, so it defended by ownership). The new chief financial officer's first half already shows a capital-allocation agenda (distribution headroom up to £1.5bn in FY27, up to £2.0bn by FY30), and the labour target was cut by about 10,000. What has not changed is the nature of the change: it lands in cost, capital expenditure and cash, not in revenue or guidance, and the near-term cash print was weak (H1 normalised free cash flow £236m; net debt +£1.04bn). For the second set: model the cost programme as delivering (£3.5bn gross by FY30, labour to 65,000 to 70,000), net of wage inflation and an own-work-capitalisation reversal; treat the FY27 £2.0bn cash target as achievable but back-loaded and without buffer once TalkTalk's £400m is counted; model TalkTalk consolidation explicitly; and model the pension cash under both readings until the mechanics are confirmed, because the difference is several hundred million pounds a year of shareholder cash from FY28.

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement38C-1465C-8637C-5952C-8138D-0355
Group judgement

Risks

F3831st of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

At 216p the risk in BT is mainly in the price, not the business. The fundamental risks to the cash case fell in the last three months (TalkTalk internalised, the pension triennial settled, Ofcom's final decision without a margin cap, the challenger merger provisionally blocked), the growth engine is not decelerating, and the sudden fundamental risks that remain (a CMA remedy on TalkTalk, a gilt shock to the pension, a large wholesale defection, the PSTN switch-off, a cyber event) are each possible rather than probable. But the stock trades at the 91st percentile of its five-year 24-month-forward multiple in a sector the market is de-rating as a leveraged yield group; consensus earnings per share are being trimmed; six of eleven visible brokers are sellers; and a reversion to its own median multiple is about -25% with earnings unchanged, with a fair trough of about 120p to 140p. For the second set: the forecast risk is moderate and skewed to timing (H2 FY27 cash, TalkTalk losses, pension mechanics), while the valuation risk is high; the third and fourth sets should weigh how much of the de-risking the price already holds.

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

F3832nd of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: five sudden fundamental risks stand out; none is likely in the next 18 months, but each could impair the cash case in one step without an earnings warning first. Ranked by our judgement of plausibility times size:

  • A CMA remedy on TalkTalk (plausibility: possible; size: medium). A forced sale of PlatformX or behavioural undertakings would partly unwind the counterparty de-risking and leave the £400m spent with less benefit; a tougher outcome could require divesting retail customers. Decision timing not stated in the files.
  • A gilt or pension shock (possible; large). The scheme is over-hedged on an IAS 19 basis and exposed to collateral calls in a violent gilt move; notional swaps of £1,047m on credit support; a solvency deficit estimated at £7.6bn without the covenant (June 2025); about 30% to 50% of assets in less liquid holdings; only 45% of longevity hedged; mitigated by £2.6bn of money-market liquidity, the new £2.7bn actuarial deficit and the bond-based funding.
  • A large wholesale defection (possible; medium to large). Sky migrating a material share of its about 6m customers to a consolidated CityFibre-Netomnia; or the PlatformX third-party ISPs leaving. Each 100k lines is about £19m of EBITDA.
  • A PSTN switch-off failure (low; medium). About 1m lines to migrate before 31 January 2027, including about 350k business premises and vulnerable users; a serious incident could bring political intervention, delay and cost.
  • A cyber or network outage at critical-infrastructure scale (low; medium to large). BT blocks over 4m attacks a day; an outage at EE or Openreach would carry fines, compensation and reputational cost.
  • Lesser: reclassification of supply-chain finance and factoring as debt (over £500m added to net debt; Deutsche Bank); industrial action against the 65,000-to-70,000 target; a further distressed alt-net emerging debt-free and pricing below Openreach.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D553rd of 5

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: concentrated, and more so for cash than for earnings. One driver, capital expenditure, produces more than the whole of the rise in free cash flow over the next 18 months and about two thirds of it over four years. Three drivers (core EBITDA, TalkTalk and depreciation) produce all of the rise in earnings per share. What makes this concentration tolerable for predictability is its nature: the dominant driver is a spending schedule in management's own hands on a build that is physically ending, not a market outcome. What makes it uncomfortable is that the two earnings drivers that are not mechanical (group EBITDA growth and TalkTalk) are the two with no record behind them.

  • Against the last six years, what is different. From FY20 to FY26: revenue fell about 1% a year on the continuing base; EBITDA was flat; depreciation rose from £4.3bn to £4.85bn; interest rose £260m; capital expenditure rose from £4.1bn to £5.2bn; adjusted earnings per share stayed at 18p to 19p; normalised free cash flow stayed at £1.3bn to £1.6bn. In the forecast: revenue +0.7% to +1%; core EBITDA +1% to +1.3%; depreciation flat to slightly down; interest flat; capital expenditure down to £3.95bn (FY28) and £3.5bn (FY31). Every one of the differences except revenue is a line that stops going the wrong way. That is a single underlying cause, the end of the build, expressed in several lines.
  • The next 18 months (FY27 to FY28).
  • Free cash flow, +£277m to about £2.2bn. Capital expenditure +£400m; EBITDA +£92m; working capital +£50m; against cash tax -£160m, leases -£40m, interest -£15m and the absence of FY27's copper proceeds -£50m. Capital expenditure is 144% of the net change. If FY28 capital expenditure were £200m higher than assumed, the rise in free cash flow would be about £75m, not £277m.
  • Earnings per share, +0.7p to about 19.0p. Core EBITDA +0.6p; the depreciation line +0.4p (EE and little else); TalkTalk -0.1p; interest, tax and share count -0.2p. Two drivers.
  • Four years (FY27 to FY31).
  • Free cash flow, +£1.3bn. Capital expenditure about +£0.85bn (65%); EBITDA about +£0.56bn (43%); working capital +£0.15bn; cash tax -£0.21bn.
  • Earnings per share, about +5p after my change. Core EBITDA about +2.9p (57%); TalkTalk about +1.2p (23%); core depreciation about +1.1p (21%); interest +0.1p; tax -0.2p; share count about nil. The second set had depreciation at about 2.5p of a 7p gain; it is now the smallest of the three.
  • How the concentration splits by kind of driver.
  • Mechanical and in management's hands (high predictability): capital expenditure; the EE roll-off; restructuring cash falling; the end of the voice drag on a fixed date. These carry all of the 18-month change in cash and about half of the 18-month change in earnings.
  • Operational, with a partial record (moderate predictability): core EBITDA growth of about 1% a year, which is itself a net of several offsetting flows (gross savings of about £450m to £500m a year against wage inflation, decapitalisation, voice and Business decline). Each 1% of EBITDA growth a year is about 0.65p of earnings per share a year. The driver is single in the model but diffuse underneath, and a net of large gross flows is where small percentage errors become large absolute ones: a £100m shortfall is 1.2% of EBITDA and about 4% of earnings per share.
  • Discretionary and external (low predictability): TalkTalk, which is a single decision by a regulator and a minister followed by an integration; it is 23% of the four-year earnings change and nothing in the next 18 months except a small drag.
  • What the concentration means if one driver fails.
  • Capital expenditure £300m a year higher than planned (Deutsche Bank's case that savings must be spent on faster fibre technology, mobile or the costlier premises beyond 25m, 40% of which are in multi-dwelling buildings): FY30 free cash flow about £2.75bn, not £3.05bn; the FY30 target missed; earnings per share barely affected at first.
  • Core EBITDA flat instead of +1% a year: FY31 earnings per share about 2.9p lower (about 20.6p); free cash flow about £0.4bn lower.
  • TalkTalk: FY31 earnings per share about 0.6p lower if it is sold (before any effect on line losses), and about 1.5p lower if it is kept and still loses money.
  • These are largely independent, which narrows the range of the total, but the first two share one root: if labour does not leave as the build ends, both capitalised cost and operating cost stay.
  • Peers. Royal KPN's and Chorus's post-build cases were just as concentrated in capital expenditure, and both delivered the cash; KPN's premium rests, Bernstein says, on broadband losses stabilising, which is the same second condition BT has. Morgan Stanley's point that equity is about half of enterprise value (a 5% change in enterprise value is about 10% in equity) means concentration in a few drivers reaches the share price geared.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

D6223rd of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has changed in one material respect since the memo: having been blocked by Ofcom from defending the wholesale base with price, BT chose to defend it with ownership, by buying TalkTalk. The core strategy (UK-only, fibre to 25m then about 30m premises by FY32, cost out, deleverage to BBB+, then distribute) is unchanged, clearly articulated in a six-year transformation, and resourced (capital expenditure falling, a £1.2bn cost-to-achieve budget, about £400m committed to TalkTalk in FY27).

  • The shift. From wholesale neutrality plus commercial offers (Equinox, the blocked £9.50 offer) to vertical consolidation of a distressed retail customer, framed by the chief executive as "an unprecedented measure to protect critical national infrastructure". This makes BT's retail share about 36% and gives it control over whether about 2.5m customers stay on Openreach.
  • Articulation. Clear: "UK-focused strategy", "building trusted digital networks", "simpler and more agile company"; quarterly building blocks for every driver.
  • Resourcing. Capital is redirected entirely to UK networks; International ring-fenced in the Verizon joint venture ($625m equalisation payment at completion, H2 2027); the TalkTalk outlay is excluded from guidance.
  • What is not changed. No growth engine; the ambition is margin and cash on a flat revenue line; the Openreach spin-out remains a conditional option ("would absolutely have to look at options" if the market does not reflect its value).
  • Risk. The TalkTalk move may draw remedies and invites the question of whether Openreach's Equivalence of Inputs regime is compatible with BT owning the two largest Openreach retail customers.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C5214th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible?

Judgement: real at wholesale, nominal and partly given back at retail broadband, modestly real in mobile, and latent in copper from 2029. The retail model change to fixed money-amount rises is durable but no longer generates real retail broadband ARPU growth while front-book discounting, voice loss and new connection charges offset it.

  • Wholesale, real but regulated. Openreach broadband ARPU £17.8, +7% in H1 FY27, from 3.6% CPI indexation plus higher-speed mix; Openreach plans to raise copper prices to force migration; copper price freedom from April 2029 in 90%-fibre exchanges. Capped on the front book (Frontbook ARPU Share at £19.32) and blocked from deep targeted discounts.
  • Retail broadband, nominal and leaking. Annual fixed rises (£3 a month per the Earnings Review; £4.00 per the business foundations report) are 7% to 10% of a £40.8 ARPU, yet Consumer broadband ARPU fell 3%. The causes are legacy voice roll-off, front-book discounting (Plusnet at £22.99) and IFRS 15, which brings the contracted rise forward into the first year. New connection charges pushed broadband net additions to -7k. Morgan Stanley's survey found the rises "stuck" with fewer departures than expected.
  • Mobile, modestly real. Postpaid mobile ARPU £19.8, +3%, with net additions of 86k.
  • Business, weak. Only 15% of managed-services revenue carries indexation.
  • Relative to costs. Wage inflation and energy run near or above CPI; the real pricing gain sits in Openreach, where the cost base is falling.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

B6110th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: one acquisition, defensive, strategically sensible and cheaply bought, with real integration and regulatory risk. TalkTalk protects about £1bn a year of Openreach revenue and the line-loss trajectory for a total FY27 cash cost of about £400m, which is about 2% of BT's market capitalisation; it is a disciplined use of the balance sheet if the CMA does not force remedies and if TalkTalk's losses are closed quickly.

  • Strategic logic. Removes the most likely large migration off Openreach (TalkTalk was pursuing a sale of PlatformX to Octopus Investments, whose affiliate Fern Trading owns alt-nets; Deutsche Bank called a change of control "a material unpriced risk event"). Morgan Stanley: "substantially reduces Openreach wholesale line losses".
  • Discipline. Bought out of administration, described as debt-free (TalkTalk had £1.3bn of restructured debt maturing in 2028); the £400m includes consideration, working capital and expected trading losses (the earnings call also mentions "debt assumptions"; the two statements are not reconciled in the files).
  • Integration. About 2.5m customers on low ARPU; a CRM migration to the Kraken platform was under way at TalkTalk; Clive Selley leads; competitors are targeting the base with termination-fee credits; dual running costs.
  • Regulatory. CMA public interest review; retail share about 31% to 36%; VMO2 urges forced divestment of PlatformX; Deutsche Bank warns the benefits could be unwound.
  • Unknown for the model. TalkTalk's revenue, EBITDA (likely small or negative), synergy plan and the intra-group elimination of Openreach revenue are not in the files.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D5227th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: shareholder returns are not yet stepping up, but for the first time there is a written framework for how much can be distributed (up to £1.5bn in FY27, up to £2.0bn by FY30), and the pension agreement may release several hundred million pounds a year of cash from FY28. Near term, the balance sheet is being used defensively (TalkTalk) and net debt is rising, so buybacks remain mooted rather than actual.

  • Dividend. Interim 2.5p (+2% on 2.45p); policy of low-to-mid single-digit growth until leverage reaches BBB+ metrics; consensus 8.6p, 8.9p, 9.2p for FY27 to FY29; Morgan Stanley expects 11p by FY30.
  • Buybacks. £30m in FY26 (employee plans); enhanced returns deferred until a 0.5x leverage reduction to BBB+ metrics; Bank of America upgraded in May 2026 on expected capital returns.
  • Distribution headroom. The FY27 dividend (about 8.6p on about 9.85bn shares, about £850m) uses a little more than half of the £1.5bn FY27 headroom; the remainder is a theoretical buyback capacity the pension covenant now allows.
  • Pension cash, two readings. (a) If the £2.1bn of zero-coupon bonds maturing in 2033 replace the cash contributions of FY28 to FY31 (about £780m a year in FY27; scheduled to fall to £670m in FY31 and £180m a year from FY32 under the 2023 plan), BT's post-pension cash rises by several hundred million pounds a year from FY28, offset by a debt-like £2.1bn obligation in 2033 that rating agencies may add to leverage. (b) If the bonds mature on "the same maturity profiles" as the contributions they replace, the cash outflow is broadly unchanged and the change is one of form and security. The files support both readings; the second set must resolve it.
  • Balance sheet direction. Net debt rose £1,040m in H1 to £21,006m (pension payments, final dividend, timing) and the TalkTalk cash comes in H2.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–68

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several, and they are concentrated in removing tail risks and in monetising legacy assets.

  • Pension de-risked. Deficit £3.7bn to £2.7bn; the downside stabiliser mechanism (£150m to £300m extra a year if the deficit fell more than £1bn behind plan) is not in prospect; management expects no further cash to reach full funding by 2030.
  • PSTN switch-off (31 January 2027). Ends dual running and the voice drag.
  • Copper and property monetisation. Copper forward sales (£105m in FY24, £99m in FY26) and up to 200,000 tonnes over the 2030s; 5,500 exchanges toward about 1,000 and over 4,000 buildings to close over two decades.
  • AI-led labour reduction. 65,000 to 70,000 target; the bid-preparation example (14 man-weeks to 3 man-days).
  • Structural optionality. The Openreach spin-out option; the Verizon $625m payment; the rural wholesale opportunity if VMO2 rents Openreach.
  • Project Gigabit. Up to £1.2bn of public funding lowers net rural capital expenditure.

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement: strong, broad and well resourced on cost, capital, perimeter, people and balance-sheet de-risking; narrow on growth. The internal change is among the most complete in European telecoms, and in the last three months it added two material de-risking actions (TalkTalk; the pension settlement). Its limitation is unchanged: it raises cash and margin on a flat revenue line, and it does not by itself lift earnings per share by more than low-to-mid single digits a year.

  • Breadth. Chief executive, finance, Openreach, divisional heads, strategy, perimeter, cost programme, capital programme, pension, wholesale counterparty: ten dimensions changed.

  • Limit. No guidance raised; Business and Consumer EBITDA declining; first-half cash weak; net debt rising near term.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

C5916th of 42

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

C5916th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BT sits at the top of a concentrated, rationalising UK industry whose structure became more supportive between July and November 2026. It is number one in retail broadband (about 36% after TalkTalk), SME broadband, mobile revenue and national fixed wholesale, and it is the lowest-cost fibre builder in Europe. The large competitors now put margin first (VMO2 has turned from builder to cost-cutter and possible wholesale customer); the volume-first alt-nets are capital-starved; the most dangerous intermediary (TalkTalk) has been internalised; the competition authority is blocking the challenger consolidation that threatened Openreach; and Ofcom's final decision left the 'fair bet' intact without a margin cap. The remaining fragilities are specific and dated: the CMA's review of the TalkTalk purchase, a possible CityFibre-Netomnia combination, Sky's multi-sourcing, and retail discounting by fixed-wireless, mobile-virtual and alt-net entrants that keeps Consumer broadband ARPU falling. Against the sector's historical pattern, BT fits the outperformers on fundamentals (capital expenditure falling on an owned network, contractual pricing, self-driven cost cuts) and the underperformers on structure and price (leverage and a pension in a rising-yield regime, a multiple near the top of its own range). For the second set: model Openreach volumes on a slowing erosion path with ARPU growth from indexation and mix, model retail ARPU as flat to slightly down, and treat the industry structure as supportive for the forecast horizon, with the TalkTalk regulatory outcome as the main structural uncertainty in the next six months.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C-5818th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market has become materially less fragile since October, because the most dangerous intermediary (TalkTalk) is now inside BT; Sky is the one large remaining intermediary whose sourcing choices can move Openreach volumes in a step.

  • Openreach's channel. Over 700 communications providers but dependence on a few: BT Consumer and Business, TalkTalk (about 2.7m lines, now BT-owned), Sky (about 6m customers, already selling over CityFibre), Vodafone, and potentially VMO2 in rural areas.
  • New fragilities. PlatformX's about 1.0m third-party ISP lines now sit with a BT-owned wholesaler; their ISPs may prefer a neutral wholesaler. Wholesale aggregators (Gamma's FibreXchange; the Broadband Forum's Wholesale Access Project) lower the cost of multi-homing over time.
  • Friction that protects Openreach. Multi-homing requires IT integration, field operations and equipment swaps; experts expect wholesale customers to move no more than 10% to 20% of incremental volume onto alt-nets near term.
  • Retail channel. About 400 EE stores (27 converted to experiential format); EE ID omnichannel; One Touch Switching (425,000 migrations) lowers switching friction.
  • Contract terms. Openreach supply is on one-month rolling terms with no change-of-control clauses (Equivalence of Inputs), which is why ownership, not contract, was the defence.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C-5319th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors' behaviour helps BT at wholesale and hurts it at retail; BT's position is robust against the large players and moderately fragile against local alt-net and fixed-wireless discounting.

  • VMO2. Financially stressed; halting build; cost reset; possibly renting Openreach: helps.
  • Sky. Multi-sourcing via CityFibre and passing on lower wholesale costs: hurts at the margin.
  • VodafoneThree. £11bn network plan; fixed wireless push; challenging EE's network leadership: hurts mobile and entry broadband.
  • Alt-nets. Discounting to fill networks; distressed: hurts locally, but their retrenchment reduces overbuild.
  • Mobile virtual operators. Price down at the low end: hurts mobile ARPU at the edges.
  • Overall effect. Consumer EBITDA -1% and Openreach EBITDA +4% in H1 FY27 express it: the wholesale layer strengthening, the retail layer under pressure.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C+5511th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the disruption forces act most strongly on BT's competitors' balance sheets and on BT's own cost base, both favourably; they act on demand only at the edges. BT's position is moderately robust.

  • Demand. Satellite and fixed wireless take rural and entry-tier households; AI agents could raise switching.
  • Competition. Capital scarcity and high rates are disrupting the alt-nets and VMO2 more than BT.
  • Regulators and politicians. Their concern has moved from Openreach's technology to its pricing tactics and now its retail share.
  • Suppliers. Post-peak build lowers equipment and contractor demand; Open RAN and software-defined networks (Nokia-NVIDIA radio architecture) widen sourcing and reduce vendor power.
  • Workforce. AI enables a 35% to 40% headcount reduction over six years; the risk is industrial-relations friction.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B+804th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: BT is number one in every UK market it serves except mobile subscribers, where it is a close number two to VodafoneThree, and it is the only national fixed wholesaler at scale. The TalkTalk purchase widened its lead in retail broadband.

  • Fixed wholesale. Openreach has about 80% of UK broadband lines (the nexfibre key-question report; Deutsche Bank's measure is 67%), 24.1m FTTP premises passed against CityFibre about 4.7m, nexfibre about 2.6m plus Netomnia's about 3.2m to 3.4m, VMO2's cable footprint and about 20m alt-net premises in aggregate across dozens of players.
  • Retail broadband. About 31% before TalkTalk, about 36% after (Deutsche Bank, calculated); TalkTalk about 9%, Vodafone about 5%; Sky has about 6m broadband customers (about a fifth of 28.6m lines).
  • Small and medium business broadband. About 50%.
  • Mobile. EE about 26% of mobile service revenue (number one by revenue); VodafoneThree about 28m to 29m customers is number one by subscribers.
  • Consumer scale. 13.1m households; 8.2m broadband lines; 15.7m mobile connections (14.1m postpaid in H1 FY27).
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C+7211th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: very concentrated at retail and in mobile, and near-monopolistic but slowly fragmenting at fixed wholesale; the share curve is steep in every layer.

  • Retail broadband. The top five (BT with TalkTalk, Sky, VMO2, Vodafone and the next player) hold the large majority of 28.6m lines; BT alone (about 36%) is several times number five.
  • Mobile. Three network operators (EE, VodafoneThree, VMO2) carry effectively all traffic; mobile virtual network operators ride on them.
  • Fixed wholesale. Openreach about 80% of lines against an alt-net tail where the largest (CityFibre) has about 730k connected customers on 4.6m to 4.7m premises and the sector's average take-up is about 16%.
  • The asymmetry that matters. The concentration BT enjoys at wholesale is the one the regulator exists to reduce (Ofcom's pro-competition stance; the CMA's review of TalkTalk), while the concentration at retail and in mobile is now being tested by the CMA for the first time in BT's case.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F5032nd of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: retail shares are stable; wholesale share is eroding steadily at about 4% of lines a year, and new entrants have taken a meaningful share at wholesale over five years; that erosion is now slowing.

  • Retail. BT's broadband share has been about 31% for years; churn is 1.1% a month.
  • Wholesale. Openreach lost 825k lines in FY26 on a base of roughly 20m (about 4%); 367k in H1 FY27 (about 3.7% annualised). Alt-nets have passed over 20m premises and connected roughly 3m to 4m over three to five years; Openreach estimates about 50% of its 16m fibred premises are overbuilt; 47% of UK premises have more than one FTTP operator.
  • Mobile entrants. Mobile virtual network operators (Monzo, Revolut, Octopus, AO, Lebara, Tesco discussions) are adding at the low end; Sky Mobile is the main winner of mobile churn (Morgan Stanley).
  • Trend. Alt-net construction velocity down at least 40%; the largest wholesale defector (TalkTalk) now internal; line losses down from 203k to 175k a quarter.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

C6417th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: at retail and in mobile, share consolidates toward the largest; at wholesale, toward whoever has capital and the lowest build cost, which is Openreach, though challengers try to consolidate among themselves and regulators decide whether they may. BT is among both the largest and the most advantaged in every layer except the top fibre speed tier.

  • Retail. Toward BT (TalkTalk absorbed; 193k TalkTalk lines previously sold to Utility Warehouse and 130k to Rise Fibre show the other path).
  • Mobile. Toward VodafoneThree by scale (a 4-to-3 merger).
  • Wholesale. Openreach builds at about £300 per premise against £500 to £1,800 for alt-nets and has starved them of capital; alt-nets are consolidating (Truespeed with Freedom Fibre; nexfibre with Netomnia, provisionally blocked; CityFibre possibly with Netomnia). The most advantaged seller of top-tier symmetric speed is the alt-net where it has built.
  • Is BT among them. Yes: largest, lowest-cost builder, and the owner of the most valuable customer relationships after TalkTalk.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F4632nd of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: a two-tier industry. The three large integrated operators and Sky now put margin first (VMO2 more so since its cost reset); the alt-nets put volume first out of financial necessity and will sell below full cost to fill networks; front-book prices fall when volume is contested while back-book prices rise by contract.

  • Margin-first. VMO2 guiding service revenue down 3% to 5%, halting build, cutting £0.8bn of cost, raising its buy-out offer to £300 (BT cut its own to £200 from £300); Sky premium pricing with multi-sourcing; VodafoneThree synergy-led (£700m to £1bn targets), though using fixed wireless for volume.
  • Volume-first. Alt-nets on about £9bn to £10bn of debt with about £500m of revenue and about £1bn of annual interest (Redburn): YouFibre cut 8Gbps from £99.99 to £50; Sky over CityFibre at £35 and £45 against a prior £70 to £80; Community Fibre £20 gigabit; CityFibre wholesale up to 40% below Openreach FTTP; Grain and Brsk Black Friday offers.
  • Mobile. Mobile virtual network operators price down (Lebara from £5, Sky Mobile £1 entry, AO £12 for 500GB, giffgaff unlimited cut from £25 to £14).
  • Prices when volume falls. Front book: yes, they fall. Back book: annual contractual pounds-and-pence rises across all large operators.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4626th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would competitors launch a 'shark attack' on pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: among the large players the attitude is stable and has become more rational over three years; among the alt-nets it is unstable and funding-driven, and a 'shark attack' is likely in local overbuild areas but cannot be sustained nationally. The new asymmetry is regulatory: alt-nets may discount, while Openreach's deepest discount was blocked, so BT's ability to respond to a local attack is constrained.

  • Over three years. From build-and-discount (2022 to 2024) to consolidate-and-fill (2025 to 2026); alt-net construction down at least 40%; VMO2 pivot to cost and wholesale; TalkTalk's collapse.
  • Instability. Alt-net pricing is reactive to refinancing; a restructured, debt-free alt-net could price below Openreach's regulated tariffs (Redburn); competitors attacked TalkTalk's base the moment BT bought it.
  • Shark-attack capacity. Limited by capital scarcity; the most dangerous version is a funded, consolidated wholesaler (CityFibre-Netomnia) offering ISPs 30% to 40% discounts to Openreach's Equinox 2 card to win multi-homing.
  • BT's response capacity. Equinox 2 continuity; the approved £19.32 Frontbook ARPU Share cap; the £50 geographic bounty; selective retail defence (Plusnet £22.99); TalkTalk ownership.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C6016th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: supportive and less fragile than in July 2026. A concentrated retail market, a 3-operator mobile market and a near-monopoly national wholesale network with a five-year regulatory settlement form a supportive structure. Its fragility lies in three places: the regulator's commitment to infrastructure competition, the possibility of a funded second wholesaler, and the CMA's view of BT's enlarged retail share.

  • Support. BT number one in retail broadband, SME broadband, mobile revenue and wholesale; the 'fair bet' to 2031; competitors financially weaker than at any time in five years.
  • Fragility, reduced. TalkTalk internalised; nexfibre-Netomnia provisionally blocked; VMO2's build halted; Ofcom's final decision without a margin cap.
  • Fragility, remaining. CityFibre-Netomnia; Sky's multi-sourcing; the CMA's TalkTalk review; a post-2031 regulatory reset; retail discounting by fixed-wireless and mobile-virtual entrants.

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: substitutes threaten only the edges of BT's demand, and the largest one (cloud IP voice for legacy PSTN) is a migration BT itself is completing.

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D5821st of 428 answers
Judgement58C-4162C-4272C-4332C-4460C-4546C-4640C-4766
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5821st of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BT's competitive advantage is real, physical and widening on cost, and regulated so that it earns about the cost of capital rather than excess returns. The production and scale moats (the lowest-cost national fibre build in Europe, a network rivals cannot replicate, and the balance sheet to consolidate a distressed customer) are strong and, as the build ends, they turn into mechanical cost and cash gains each quarter. Customer utility is leading on network quality and not inferior on service. But the moat does not convert into pricing power: Openreach's price rises come from regulated indexation and mix, retail broadband ARPU falls despite annual rises, and the addressable market is flat. For the second set, this means: model margin expansion and capital-expenditure decline as high-confidence mechanical drivers; model revenue growth as no more than about 0% to 2% a year; do not model real retail price gains; and treat the Openreach regulated return as the ceiling on group returns.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D6221st of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: BT holds two strong, widening production-and-scale moats (the national passive network built at the lowest unit cost in Europe, and the fibre migration that mechanically lowers its operating cost), a two-sided regulatory position, and a moderate retail customer moat; it holds no customer moat at wholesale by design. The moats are real but they protect returns at about the cost of capital rather than generating excess returns, because the regulator shares the benefit with BT's wholesale customers.

  • Production (strong, widening). About £300 per premise passed (now just above the £250 to £350 midpoint) against £500 to £1,800 for alt-nets; 24.1m FTTP premises; fibre fault rates about one third of copper's; repair volumes -18%; the build has starved challengers of capital.
  • Scale (strong, widening as the build completes). Duct and pole infrastructure that cannot be replicated economically at national scale; procurement scale (£9.6bn a year with UK suppliers; priority on fibre cable); the balance sheet to rescue TalkTalk.
  • Regulatory (two-sided, stable). Significant market power with the 'fair bet' (returns above the cost of capital over the fibre cycle) and no cost-based controls in 86% of premises; but open access, Equivalence of Inputs, the anchor product cap and the blocked discount mean the regulator caps how far the moat can be monetised.
  • Customer (moderate, stable to slightly widening). Retail: convergence 27.1%, churn 1.0% to 1.1% a month, EE the best UK network for twelve years (RootMetrics 93.2 against Vodafone 85.8), Group NPS 33.4. Wholesale: none; Openreach contracts are one-month rolling.
  • Narrowing. Top-tier speed (symmetric XGS-PON alt-nets at 5Gbps to 10Gbps); the share of wholesale lines (about 4% a year).
  • Culture and knowledge (moderate). Deep physical-network engineering knowledge; the commercial culture is being rebuilt.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

B+725th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: BT's scale is a multiple of every fixed competitor's, and it confers the lowest unit build cost, procurement power, the ability to absorb regulatory constraints and the capacity to consolidate a distressed rival; it does not confer pricing power at wholesale because the regulator neutralises it.

  • Revenue £19.6bn (FY26), about £17.5bn continuing, against alt-net sector revenue of about £500m in aggregate (Redburn) and CityFibre about 730k connected customers; VMO2 is the only comparable integrated rival and is more indebted.
  • Total labour resource 90,000 to 108,000 (ex- or including International); 13.1m Consumer households; nearly 1m business customers; over 700 wholesale customers; nearly 31m connections.
  • Confers: £300-per-premise economics; Ericsson and Nokia terms; national coverage for public-sector contracts (Home Office Emergency Services Network £1.3bn); resilience to absorb £100m regulatory working-capital hits.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D6026th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: leading on network quality, reliability and coverage; not inferior on service; inferior only at the top fibre speed tier and on front-book price against alt-nets. Inferiority is disproved at the level that matters for the base.

  • Leading: EE best UK mobile network twelve years running; 5G standalone coverage rising (77% at Q1 FY27; 81% cited at H1); the first 5G network slicing service; Openreach's national footprint and fibre reliability.
  • Not inferior: Group NPS a record 33.4 (+5.6 rebased); BT brand satisfaction at a four-year high; service resolution time down to 23.0 minutes from 25.6.
  • Inferior: symmetric multi-gigabit tiers from alt-nets (Sky over CityFibre 5Gbps at £45; YouFibre 8Gbps at £50); price at the entry tier (Community Fibre 1Gbps at £20; Starlink £32 rural).
  • Evidence that the base values the utility: churn stable at 1.0% to 1.1% a month through annual price rises.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D4625th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: BT applies price every year and the rises stick on the base, but the evidence of real pricing power is confined to Openreach's regulated indexation and mix; at retail broadband the applied rises are offset by front-book discounting and voice loss, so measured ARPU falls. Pricing power is used, but its net effect at retail is defensive.

  • Applied and held: annual pounds-and-pence rises across Consumer (Morgan Stanley's survey: rises stuck with fewer departures than expected); mobile ARPU +3%; Openreach ARPU +7% (CPI 3.6% plus fibre-tier mix).
  • Given back: Consumer broadband ARPU -3% to £40.8; Plusnet at £22.99 to defend; connection charges cost volume (-7k broadband net additions); the deepest Openreach discount blocked by Ofcom (which also limits how far Openreach can trade price for volume).
  • Latent: copper price freedom from April 2029 in 90%-fibre exchanges; the Frontbook ARPU Share cap of £19.32 allows front-book ARPU to rise toward it.
  • Not demonstrated: 2%-plus real pricing at group level.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F4033rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: little, and not as a culture; scale benefits are shared de facto through regulated wholesale prices and volume discounts that the regulator requires or allows, and through low-cost flanker brands. This is not a Costco-type flywheel.

  • Equinox volume discounts to committing wholesale customers; the £9.50-a-month offer Ofcom blocked was scale shared too aggressively for the regulator's liking.
  • Plusnet as a value brand; EE as premium; BT absorbing the anchor product price cut.
  • Management messaging emphasises margin, cash and network leadership, not passing savings to customers.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

B668th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: strongly on cost and cash, weakly on revenue. Each quarter the fibre migration lowers fault and maintenance cost, the falling build lowers capital expenditure, rising take-up spreads a sunk network over more paying lines, and capital scarcity keeps challengers from overbuilding; none of these needs heroics. Against them, wholesale share erodes mechanically through overbuild and regulation.

  • Mechanical cost and cash: capital expenditure from £5.1bn toward about £3.6bn (consensus FY30); take-up 38% to 41% in six months on a nearly complete footprint; exchanges 5,500 toward about 1,000; PSTN switch-off January 2027; copper sales.
  • Mechanical competitive: each premise passed at £300 is one an alt-net can no longer profitably overbuild; alt-net build down at least 40%.
  • Mechanical against: about 4% a year of wholesale lines lost; regulated returns bounded at the 'fair bet'.
  • Peers: KPN shows the same capital-expenditure gravity with higher returns; Orange's French capital intensity falls mechanically; Vodafone's German cable loses mechanically to fibre.

Management quality
