Group judgement

Company - Management

C+609th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BT has a strong operator-led management whose actions in the last three months (TalkTalk, the pension settlement, the labour target cut) show it can adapt when a lever is blocked and remove tail risks proactively. The chief executive is proven at this kind of programme, the team has been rebuilt around it, communication of operating drivers is unusually complete, and governance scaffolding is prudent. The limits are equally clear: the ambition is defensive (cash and margin on flat revenue), commercial execution lags, the team is untested in its new seats during the PSTN switch-off and the TalkTalk integration, incentives reward the adjusted measures, insider signals are thin, and shareholders rank behind bondholders and the pension. For the second set: give high weight to management delivering what it guides on cost, capital expenditure and cash (a three-year record), low weight to any revenue or commercial upside it has not guided, and expect conservative, staged capital returns rather than aggressive buybacks.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5228th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: large in execution and modest in outcome. Management attempts more change, faster, than almost any European incumbent (the fastest fibre build in Europe, a 35% to 40% headcount reduction over six years, five disposals and a joint venture in eighteen months, a distressed-competitor rescue), but the outcomes it promises are defensive: margin from 38% to about 40%, cash doubling, revenue growth "from FY28" and low-single-digit earnings growth.

  • Execution ambition: 4.8m premises passed in FY26; 2.2m FTTP net additions in FY26 against 1.2m to 1.5m for Deutsche Telekom, Orange and Telefonica; labour target 65,000 to 70,000; TalkTalk bought within weeks of its administration.

BT guides "low-single-digit" earnings growth and no revenue growth until FY28.
  • Growth is explicitly not the priority; cash is.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C-6217th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: smart, congruent with the asset and adaptive; it widens the cost moat and has just shown it can switch levers when one is blocked; it is not a growth strategy and does not try to be.

  • Smart: build faster and cheaper than anyone can overbuild, starve challengers of capital ("deprived struggling alt-nets of financial oxygen"), migrate the base to fibre where faults are a third of copper's, retire copper and exchanges, take labour out with AI, ring-fence International, deleverage, then distribute.

  • Adaptive: when Ofcom blocked the £9.50 discount, BT bought the largest at-risk customer instead; when the pension was a risk, it settled early.
  • Limits: no answer yet to the top-tier speed gap (XGS-PON is in trial); Business remains in decline; the strategy depends on regulators accepting BT's growing retail share.

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F4239th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BT's economic outputs are weak on returns, growth and earnings quality, and improving on margin and cash; the improvement is mechanical and back-loaded, and most of it is absorbed by depreciation, interest, pension payments and, in FY27, TalkTalk before it reaches the shareholder. Return on capital is about 9%; earnings per share have not grown in six years and consensus has about 3% a year; adjusted earnings are about two-thirds above reported; cash a shareholder receives did not cover the dividend in FY26 and, on our arithmetic, will not in FY27. On the positive side, EBITDA margins rise every year, capital expenditure falls by about 30% over four years, labour by about a third, and from FY28 the shareholder's cash could exceed the dividend by several hundred million to over a billion pounds depending on the pension bond mechanics. Operating leverage is material (each 1% of service revenue is about 4% to 6% of earnings per share) and financial leverage amplifies valuation moves about twofold. For the second set: build earnings per share from EBITDA through depreciation (which falls only slowly, about £4.7bn to £4.6bn), interest (rising slightly on refinancing), an adjusted tax rate drifting toward 25%, TalkTalk's initial losses and a flat share count until buybacks; build cash separately, with the pension under two readings, cash tax from FY28 and working-capital programmes normalising.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F4034th of 42

Judgement: little scope for large high-return deployment; the high-return opportunities are small in capital (take-up on the existing footprint, cost-to-achieve spending, the TalkTalk rescue) and the large one (fibre build) earns about the cost of capital by regulatory design. This caps long-term growth potential and argues for a yield-based, not growth-based, fair multiple.

  • Fair-multiple implication: a utility-like infrastructure owner whose value rests on cash yield and on returning cash, not on compounding.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F3541st of 42

The debt is long-dated and mostly fixed, so the amplification is in valuation and refinancing drift, not in solvency.

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

C508th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price is the largest gross contributor to BT's revenue (about 2% to 2.5% a year of gross uplift) and the most margin-accretive, but its net contribution is about +0.5% to +1% a year, because the retail rises are largely spent defending volume and absorbing voice and front-book leakage. The power is real at wholesale, where CPI indexation and fibre mix lift Openreach ARPU 5% to 7% now and about 3% to 4.5% a year later, and nominal at retail, where fixed money-amount rises (£4.00 a month on broadband and £2.50 on mobile, Deutsche Bank) leave measured broadband ARPU falling. Re-accelerating inflation helps Openreach with a lag (September CPI sets the following April's price) and hurts retail in real terms; the regulatory caps bind on the front book, not the back book.

  • Wholesale (Openreach): real, regulated, lagged.
  • ARPU £17.8, +7% in H1 FY27: about 3.6 points from CPI indexation (FY27; 2.3% a year earlier) and the rest from speed-tier mix and copper-to-fibre migration. The mix component shrinks as take-up passes 50% and the copper base becomes small.
  • Indexation is set off the prior September's CPI: September 2026 was 3.5%, so April 2027 (FY28) indexation is about 3.5%; Morgan Stanley's expected peak of about 4.9% in Q2 2027 means September 2027 CPI (setting FY29) is likely higher than 3.5%. That is an Openreach tailwind in FY29 that consensus revenue growth (+0.9%) may not carry.
  • Constraints: the 80Mbps anchor is £17.17 (from £22.37), costing about £100m of FY27 working capital; Frontbook ARPU Share caps new high-speed connections at £19.32 on average; the Incremental New discount was blocked; up to £1 a month of FTTP rental rises is allowed under Equinox 2 from October 2026 (whether applied is not in the files). Copper pricing freedom from 1 April 2029 (FY30) in exchanges with 90% ultrafast coverage is mostly a migration lever.
  • Competitive gap: CityFibre is up to 30% to 40% cheaper; alt-nets' retail offers are 20% to 30% below BT; this caps front-book wholesale price where overbuilt (about half of Openreach's fibred premises), not the indexed back book.
  • Our Openreach ARPU path: +6% (FY27), +4.5% (FY28), +4% (FY29), +3.5% (FY30), +3% (FY31). About £100m to £130m a year of group EBITDA from external wholesale price, at about 90% drop-through; the internal part is a cost to Consumer and Business.
  • Consumer: nominal, mostly given back.
  • The £4.00 and £2.50 April rises are 7% to 10% of a £40.8 broadband ARPU and about 13% of a £19.8 mobile ARPU, gross. Measured broadband ARPU nevertheless fell 3% (H1 FY27) while mobile rose 3%. The decomposition the files support (direction, not exact split): legacy voice roll-off and front-book discounting (Plusnet at £22.99; alt-nets at £19 to £26) take more than the rise; IFRS 15 spreads the contracted rise across the contract, pulling it forward. The first set noted two sets of figures (£3/£1.50 against £4/£2.50); we take £4/£2.50 as the current rise (Deutsche Bank, November 2025) and the lower set as earlier years, which the files do not confirm by brand.
  • Elasticity: Morgan Stanley's survey found rises "stuck" with fewer departures than feared; but 31% of UK fixed-line users planned to cut spend and 58% of switchers move on price; the out-of-contract premium fell from 21% to 14%; new connection charges turned broadband net additions to -7k. Price therefore holds on the back book and leaks through tier-down and the front book.
  • Inflation: a fixed money rise is the "durable form" in law (Ofcom banned inflation-linked mid-contract rises from January 2025; the European Court of Justice forced Vodafone Germany to reverse a unilateral €5 rise on 2.5m lines in September 2026), but it loses real value when CPI rises toward 4% to 5%. BT can raise the money amount each year; the risk is political (affordability: about 400,000 households priced out, BT's own policy director, November 2026).
  • Our path: Consumer broadband ARPU -2% (FY27), -1% (FY28), flat (FY29 to FY31); mobile ARPU +2% to +3%; Consumer revenue about +0.7% a year and EBITDA flat. The net retail price contribution to group EBITDA is about £50m to £100m a year.
  • Business: weak. Only about 15% of managed-services revenue is indexed and only 25% of new orders in FY24 carried indexation, while its Openreach inputs are indexed; inflation squeezes Business margin. No net price contribution.
  • TalkTalk pricing. A low-ARPU base (about 1.5m retail lines) that cannot be repriced under BT's policy until the CMA review ends; afterwards it is a lever (toward Plusnet's level), but the CMA may require price commitments and competitors are already offering termination-fee credits to take the base. We assume no price upside from TalkTalk.
  • Margin. Price is the only revenue line with near-100% drop-through, so even a net +0.5 points of price is worth about £80m to £90m of EBITDA. Over FY27 to FY31 we estimate price contributes roughly £0.6bn of cumulative EBITDA before leakage and about £0.3bn to £0.4bn after it: that is most of the net EBITDA growth, with cost savings net of inflation covering decapitalisation.
  • Peers. Deutsche Telekom's first back-book rise (€2 a month, April 2026) produced lower churn than expected and positive broadband net additions; KPN's extra-contractual rise brought temporary churn (-7k in Q3 2026); Orange relies on targeted more-for-more; Proximus applied 3% to legacy bundles with limited churn. The precedent is that back-book pricing holds where network quality is superior and fails where the contract term is challenged.

For the model: Openreach ARPU +6% (FY27) easing to +3% (FY31); Consumer broadband ARPU flat to slightly down; no real retail price gain; Business no indexation benefit; watch September 2027 CPI as an FY29 Openreach upside.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7550P-0155
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D5023rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: material, and it works in both directions; each 1% of UK service revenue moves earnings per share by about 4% to 6%, so a plausible service-revenue range of -1% to +1.5% a year creates a range of about ±6% to ±8% a year in earnings per share before cost actions. The cost programme gives management a second lever that has so far absorbed the downside.

  • Structure. Network, IT and property costs are largely fixed; staff are about 70% of EBITDAaL; Openreach access lines carry about 90% gross margin.
  • Sensitivities (our arithmetic on consensus FY27: service revenue about £15.2bn, EBITDA £8,164m, EBIT £3,406m, pre-tax about £2.4bn, earnings per share 18.4p).
  • 1% of service revenue (about £150m) at 60% to 90% drop-through is £90m to £140m of EBITDA, about 3% to 4% of EBIT and about 4% to 6% of earnings per share.
  • 100k Openreach lines: about £21m of revenue and £19m of EBITDA (Redburn), about 0.15p of earnings per share (about 0.8%).
  • 1% of wage inflation on the staff base: roughly £50m, about 2% of earnings per share.
  • Evidence it works both ways. Business EBITDA -5% (FY26) and -7% (H1 FY27) on small revenue declines; Openreach EBITDA +4% on revenue +1%.
  • Range. Over 18 months the operating outcome range is narrow because revenue is contract-backed; over four years the range widens with line losses, retail ARPU and the voice end-date.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C+556th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: BT's cost base is structurally fixed, but over the last six years its operating leverage worked as a stabiliser rather than an amplifier: the cost programme, not revenue, was the variable that set EBITDA, which is why EBITDA was flat in pounds through £1.7bn of gross savings and a revenue decline. Over the next four years the operating leverage turns mildly positive at EBITDA (about +1% a year) because the two high-drop-through revenue losses that absorbed the savings, legacy voice and Openreach line losses, fade from 2027; but the operating leverage that moves earnings per share is in the capital cost line, where a nearly fixed EBITDA meets a falling depreciation charge. The main downside mechanism is decapitalisation: if labour now capitalised into the fibre build returns to operating cost faster than headcount falls, it can absorb the whole EBITDA gain.

  • What the last six years show. FactSet operating EBITDA rose from £7,659m (FY20) to £8,195m (FY26) while revenue fell from £22,811m to £19,646m; on the brokers' basis EBITDA was £8,100m, £8,209m and £8,230m in FY24 to FY26. The margin expansion (33.6% to 41.7% reported; about 46% to 47% on the continuing base now) came from cost falling faster than revenue and from shedding low-margin perimeter (International, disposals), not from profit growth. EBIT was flat at about £3.3bn because depreciation rose from £4.3bn to £4.85bn with the build.
  • Drop-through by division, H1 FY27 against H1 FY26 (our arithmetic from the company's segment figures).
  • Openreach: revenue +£31m (to £3,160m), EBITDA +£86m (to £2,238m, margin 70.8%). Positive leverage above 100%: CPI indexation and fibre mix carry about 90% gross margin, and fibre's fault rate (about one third of copper's) cut repair volumes 16% to 18%, so cost fell £55m while revenue rose.
  • Business: revenue -£26m (to £2,574m), EBITDA -£45m (to £601m). Negative leverage above 100%: high-margin legacy voice runs off (a two-point drag on service revenue) and management reinvested in brand marketing.
  • Consumer: revenue +£94m (to £4,783m), EBITDA -£13m (to £1,255m). Revenue growth was handset-led at near-zero margin, while Openreach input charges rose with CPI and front-book discounting cut broadband ARPU 3%.
  • Why the leverage turns mildly positive from FY28.
  • Legacy voice has taken £150m to £200m a year out of service revenue at a high margin. The switch-off is 31 January 2027; the drag roughly halves in FY28 and ends in FY29. That removes about £60m a year of EBITDA headwind in each of FY28 and FY29 relative to the trend.
  • Openreach line losses fall (our path: about 720k in FY27, 550k in FY28, 480k in FY29), each 100k lines being about £19m of EBITDA (Redburn), so the volume headwind shrinks by about £30m to £50m a year.
  • Gross savings continue at about £450m to £500m a year (£1.7bn delivered by H1 FY27 against £3.5bn by FY30).
  • Against these: wage inflation, National Insurance and the living wage (about £150m to £200m a year on the operating cost base; National Insurance has been in the base since April 2025), energy hedge roll-off (88% hedged for FY27, 52% for FY28; about £20m to £50m in FY28), Business decline (about £35m to £50m a year), and decapitalisation (below).
  • Net: core EBITDA +1.0% in FY28, +1.3% in FY29 and FY30, +1.0% in FY31. That is above consensus (+0.7% and +0.5% in FY28 and FY29) but well below the 2% to 3% that the gross savings alone would imply.
  • Decapitalisation is the line that decides whether the leverage is positive. FY26 staff costs were £4.55bn, of which £1.39bn of direct and £807m of indirect labour was capitalised (48%). Redburn estimates that a £1bn fall in capital expenditure moves about £600m of capitalised labour back into operating cost. Cash capital expenditure falls from £5.17bn (FY26) to about £3.5bn (FY31), about £1.65bn; on Redburn's ratio that is up to about £1bn of decapitalisation, which would erase the whole EBITDA gain. BT's answer is the labour target (90,000 in H1 FY27 to 65,000 to 70,000 by FY30, -25%; Openreach engineering 38,000 toward about 30,000). The Chorus precedent (capitalisation rate 47% to 44% in FY25 while permanent headcount fell 11%) shows the two must move together. We assume decapitalisation absorbs about £100m to £150m a year of the gross savings; this is the assumption most worth testing in the third set.
  • How it shapes the main lines (base case).
  • Revenue: core +0.7% (FY28) to +1.0% a year; group +1.8% in FY28 with the TalkTalk annualisation, then +0.7% to +0.9%. Against the last six years (-2.5% a year, mostly perimeter; about -1% a year continuing), the change is from slow decline to slow growth.
  • EBITDA margin: core about 46.6% (FY27) to 47.1% (FY31), +0.1 to +0.2 points a year, against +1 to +2 points a year in FY20 to FY26. The easy margin (perimeter, International) is gone. Group margin dips to 45.4% in FY28 because TalkTalk's revenue carries no EBITDA, then recovers to 46.8% by FY31.
  • EBIT: +4% (FY28), +7% (FY29), +7% (FY30), +5% (FY31), because depreciation falls under a rising EBITDA: of the roughly £0.88bn EBIT increase from FY27 to FY31, about £0.32bn comes from lower depreciation and about £0.56bn from EBITDA (core about £0.38bn, TalkTalk about £0.18bn).
  • Sensitivity and the cone. One per cent of UK service revenue (about £150m) at 60% to 90% drop-through moves EBITDA by £90m to £135m, EBIT by 3% to 4% and earnings per share by about 0.7p to 1.1p (we agree with the first set's 4% to 6%). One per cent of EBITDA growth a year compounds to about 2.6p of FY31 earnings per share. Over the next 18 months the cone is narrow (FY27 EBITDA within about ±£50m; FY28 within about ±£150m) because revenue is contracted and the cost programme is in hand; by FY31 it widens to about ±£400m, set mainly by decapitalisation, Business and TalkTalk.
  • Peers. Openreach behaves like KPN and Chorus (wholesale fibre with indexation: positive leverage); Business behaves like the enterprise units of Orange and KPN (connectivity price erosion: negative leverage); Vodafone Germany's 21% EBITDAaL fall from March 2023 to March 2026 shows how fast negative leverage runs in a fixed-cost network losing volume, which is the outcome the falling line losses now make less likely for Openreach.

For the model: core EBITDA +1.0% to +1.3% a year; Openreach EBITDA +3% a year, Consumer flat, Business -2% to -3% a year; depreciation falling about £60m to £90m a year; treat decapitalisation as the swing factor (each £100m of unmatched decapitalisation is about 0.8p of earnings per share).

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7635P-0240
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F3541st of 42full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F4022nd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is a fixed drag that does not lift within the horizon, so it amplifies the EBIT gains into earnings per share only modestly (about 1.4 times) and turns cash generation into deleveraging rather than into returns until about FY30. Net finance cost stays at about £1.1bn a year because the refinancing of cheap debt offsets the interest saved on lower net debt; net debt including leases peaks at about £20.3bn in March 2027 and falls to about £17.8bn by March 2030 (about 2.5x to 2.1x EBITDA), which is roughly the BBB+ threshold, so material buybacks are a FY31 event, not an FY28 one. The pension deal changes the security and the permission, not the cash.

  • Interest: the step-up has happened; the next four years are flat. Net interest rose from £748m (FY20) to £1,011m (FY26; adjusted net finance expense £1,060m including about £133m of lease interest), absorbing about £260m of EBIT over six years. Our path: £1,085m (FY27), £1,100m (FY28 and FY29), £1,085m (FY30), £1,070m (FY31). The build:
  • Maturities of £372m (FY27), £1,013m (FY28) and £1,296m (FY29), about £2.68bn in all, refinance about 1.5 to 2 points higher (a May 2026 €850m 3.875% bond cost about 5.4% swapped into sterling, Deutsche Bank; average debt cost 5.2%, coupon-derived 4.5% against 5.3% marked to market, Bernstein): about +£40m to +£55m a year by FY29.
  • 90% of sterling debt is fixed, so the Bank of England rise to 4.00% (reported 5 November 2026) costs about £10m to £20m a year on the floating 10%; lower cash balances (£1.6bn in September 2026 against £2.2bn a year earlier) cut interest income.
  • Net debt falls about £0.6bn a year from FY28, saving about £30m a year at 5%; this only overtakes the refinancing drift from FY30.
  • Bernstein's whole-stack mark-to-market (8% of profit after tax) is the long-run ceiling; to end-2027 the effect is about 1%.
  • How much the gearing amplifies earnings per share. With interest fixed, EPS moves about 1.4 to 1.5 times EBIT: in FY27 EBIT £3.4bn against pre-tax profit £2.3bn. Interest absorbs 32% of EBIT in FY27, falling to 25% by FY31 as EBIT rises; that falling share is itself worth about 1.5 points a year of EPS growth.
  • Balance sheet path (base case; net debt includes about £4.2bn of leases).
  • FY27: normalised free cash flow about £1.95bn, less pension £0.78bn, restructuring £0.30bn, TalkTalk acquisition cash £0.34bn and dividends £0.83bn, gives an outflow of about £0.35bn; net debt rises from £19,966m to about £20.3bn (it was £21,006m in September on seasonal working capital).
  • FY28 to FY30: surpluses of about £0.64bn (including the Verizon equalisation payment of $625m, about £470m), £0.70bn and £1.16bn; net debt about £19.7bn, £19.0bn, £17.8bn; leverage about 2.39x, 2.25x and 2.07x.
  • Including the pension deficit (actuarial £2.7bn in June 2026, falling toward about £0.3bn by 2030 as the bonds are paid), adjusted leverage falls faster, from about 2.8x to about 2.15x, which is what rating agencies weigh.
  • BBB+ needs about 0.5x of deleveraging (management) from about 2.5x to 2.6x; our path reaches it around FY30. Fitch (July 2026) affirmed BBB stable on falling capital expenditure; a one-notch downgrade by two agencies would cost only about £10m a year, so the rating matters for permission to distribute, not for interest.
  • What the pension agreement does to the balance sheet (and where we differ from the first set). The four replacement bonds (par £590m, £590m, £590m, £490m, maturing in FY28 to FY31) and a fifth (par £420m, September 2033) substitute for contributions on the same timetable; cash out is about £770m a year in FY28 to FY30, against £780m before. Their accounting is not stated in the files: if BT's reported net debt includes them, net debt jumps by roughly £2.1bn to £2.4bn at issue while the IAS 19 deficit falls; if not (non-transferable instruments issued to the scheme are normally not plan assets under IAS 19), nothing changes on the face of the balance sheet. We model the second and flag the first as a presentational risk for the third set's leverage monitoring. Refinancing risk arises only if BT refinances a bond rather than repays it. The £4bn charge over EE shares caps the trustee's insolvency claim and is not expected to affect ratings.
  • Equity amplification. Equity (market capitalisation £21.5bn) is about half of enterprise value (£44.1bn including £3.1bn of pensions), so a 5% move in enterprise value is about 10% in equity; by FY30, with net debt £2.5bn lower and the pension deficit largely closed, the same move is about 8% to 9%.
  • Off-balance-sheet and classification risks. Supply-chain finance and factoring contributed £476m to FY26 cash; reclassification as debt would add over £0.5bn to net debt (Deutsche Bank); a vendor-financing programme of about £250m sits outside net debt. Normalisation of these programmes is in our working-capital line (about £0.1bn a year of outflow in FY27 and FY28).
  • Peers. KPN (2.4x, a 2.5x ceiling, returning all free cash flow) is the end-state BT is moving toward; Virgin Media O2 (about £22bn of debt, 5.7x, unsecured bonds in the mid-30s in October 2026) shows the cost of the opposite path and is why BT's competitor set is retrenching.

For the model: net financial items about -£1.085bn to -£1.10bn through FY29, easing to -£1.07bn by FY31 (adjusted basis, excluding IAS 19 interest); cash interest about £130m lower; no buyback beyond employee plans until FY31; carry the pension bonds as pension cash, not debt service.

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

A662nd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: a large share is reducible over four years: labour by about a third, network operating cost materially as copper is retired, IT and property substantially, capital expenditure by about 30%, restructuring cash by three-quarters; the irreducible lines are business rates, pension deficit payments to FY27, interest, leases and spectrum.

  • Labour (the largest). 108,000 (FY26, including International) and 90,000 (H1 FY27, excluding International) toward 65,000 to 70,000 by FY30; Openreach engineering 38,000 toward about 30,000; subcontractors falling as the build ends. Offset: wage inflation and National Insurance.

  • Commercial. Digital channels and AI (virtual assistant; AI bid tool); Business product count from over 300 to 141; reinvestment in Business marketing shows management will spend some savings.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

A621st of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the scope is large (gross £3.5bn by FY30, labour down a quarter, capital expenditure down a third), but the history says that very little of a gross saving reaches EBITDA: £1.7bn of gross savings over two and a half years left EBITDA flat in pounds. We expect the reach to improve from about zero to about a fifth of each year's gross saving (about £80m to £120m a year of net EBITDA from FY28), because three absorbers are fading, while the cost reduction that reaches the shareholder most fully is not in EBITDA at all but in capital expenditure and restructuring cash, where the drop-through is close to 100%. Timing: FY27 H2 small; FY28 limited by energy and the first decapitalisation; most of the EBITDA effect in FY29 to FY31; most of the cash effect already in FY27 to FY29.

  • Why the gross savings did not reach EBITDA before. The absorbers, each sized from the files: labour inflation and payroll taxes (Redburn: at least £400m a year of savings needed to keep EBITDA flat); legacy voice loss (£150m to £200m of revenue a year at a high margin); the Business decline (EBITDA -5% in FY26, -7% in H1 FY27); reinvestment (Business marketing); and the International drag (now discontinued). The FY26 bonus outcome (EBITDA £8,212m against an internal £8,270m) confirms that the internal plan assumed more drop-through than was delivered.
  • Why the reach improves from FY28. Voice loss halves in FY28 and ends in FY29; International is gone; the Business decline slows as voice runs off; Openreach repair volumes keep falling with take-up. Labour inflation and decapitalisation stay. Our net EBITDA effect: about +£80m (FY28), +£110m (FY29 and FY30), +£85m (FY31) on the core, about a fifth of gross savings of about £450m to £500m a year.
  • Composition and plausibility of the remaining £1.8bn gross (FY27 H2 to FY30). BT does not disclose a line split; from the evidence the main components are:
  • Labour: 90,000 (H1 FY27) toward 65,000 to 70,000 by FY30, about 22,500 fewer (-25% in about three and a half years, about -8% a year). That is faster than Deutsche Telekom (about -4% a year), Orange France (-3% a year over six years, expected to slow to -1%) and KPN, and comparable only with Tele2's one-off -15% in 2025. It is the most ambitious line and depends on AI tools (the bid tool cut 14 man-weeks to 3 man-days) and the end of the build (subcontractors already -16%). The CWU agreement avoids compulsory redundancies "where reasonably possible", so exits are voluntary and costly: restructuring charges £448m (FY25) and £336m (FY26).
  • Network operations: fibre faults about one third of copper's; legacy networks consume about half of BT's energy; the PSTN switch-off ends dual running in voice, but exchange closures (5,500 toward about 1,000; about £220m a year spent on redundant property) are a mid-2030s event, so the property saving is mostly beyond the horizon. Emergency Voice Access (a £35-a-month fallback) may prolong some dual running after January 2027.
  • IT and commercial: application retirements past 43%; Business products from over 300 to 141; digital channels (where the FY26 scorecard paid 0%, the weakest delivery area).
  • Where the savings reach, and when (base case, core).
  • EBITDA: net +£80m to +£110m a year from FY28 (above). Cumulative core EBITDA gain FY27 to FY31 about £380m (+4.7%).
  • Capital expenditure: cash £5,169m (FY26) to about £4.35bn (FY27), £3.95bn (FY28), £3.70bn (FY29), £3.55bn (FY30), £3.50bn (FY31); accrued £4.2bn to £4.3bn in FY27 is guided; "more than £1bn below FY26" in the mid-term. This passes almost 1:1 to free cash flow and is the main reason normalised free cash flow rises by about £1.1bn from FY27 to FY30.
  • Restructuring cash (outside normalised free cash flow, inside shareholder cash): about £300m (FY27), £250m, £200m, £100m (FY30), £100m (FY31); a gain of about £200m a year by FY30.
  • Depreciation: slowly, through the lower capital base (P-01).
  • Of the roughly £1.3bn rise in normalised free cash flow from FY27 to FY31: capital expenditure about £0.85bn, EBITDA about £0.56bn, working capital about £0.15bn, cash tax about -£0.21bn, interest about nil.
  • TalkTalk synergies. The CMA's pre-emptive action order (6 October 2026) keeps TalkTalk separate and independently managed during the review, so no synergy is possible until clearance. After it, the sources point to retail cost overlap, migration off TalkTalk's systems (it had sunk cash into consultants and was mid-migration to Kraken) and procurement. BT has said it will quantify synergies only after aligning TalkTalk's accounting later in FY27. We assume TalkTalk EBITDA improves from about -£50m (FY27) to about +£130m (FY31), a swing of about £180m; Telecom Plus's experience with TalkTalk bases (about 20% churn around migration; 9% cross-sell) argues for caution on revenue synergy, so we assume cost synergy only.
  • The risk to the reach. Decapitalisation (P-01) and industrial relations: the CWU has historically forced flat-rate pay awards through strike ballots of over 30,000 Openreach engineers; a breakdown during the AI-led headcount reduction would show first in Openreach repair times and in restructuring cost per leaver.

For the model: do not credit gross savings to EBITDA; use the net path (+1.0% to +1.3% a year); put the large cost gain in capital expenditure (cash, about £4.35bn falling to £3.5bn) and in restructuring cash falling to £100m; date the TalkTalk synergy to FY29 onward and make it conditional on the CMA.

Reinforcing feedback loops

P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B605th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: three reinforcing loops dominate the horizon and they are mostly mechanical (operational fibre migration, the line-loss loop now strengthened by TalkTalk, and the capital-expenditure-to-deleveraging loop), while two balancing loops cap the outcome (retail price elasticity, which holds Consumer at flat EBITDA, and regulatory reaction, which grows stronger the better BT does and is the loop most likely to bite inside 18 months through the CMA's TalkTalk review). The strongest over four years is the financial loop, but it is slower than the market assumes because the pension cash does not fall and cash tax rises; the fastest over 18 months is the line-loss loop, and it is the one consensus least reflects.

  • Reinforcing loop 1, operational (fast, high confidence, self-driven). Take-up rises (41% at H1 FY27; 9.9m connected; 1.1m net additions in the half) → faults fall (fibre incoming fault rate about 60% below copper; storms raise copper faults about 10%, fibre barely) → repair volumes fall (-16% to -18%) → field engineers and subcontractors fall (labour -11% to 90,000) → operating cost falls, and copper stop-sell (1,572 exchanges, 15.4m premises) forces further migration. What limits it: copper wholesale pricing cannot be used to push migration until April 2029; exchange closures are a mid-2030s event; decapitalisation offsets part of the labour saving. Effect: about £50m to £80m a year of the net EBITDA gain, mostly in Openreach (P-01, P-03).
  • Reinforcing loop 2, line losses (fastest over 18 months; medium-to-high confidence). Each premise Openreach passes at about £300 shrinks the overbuild runway (about 80% of current line losses are in areas without Openreach fibre, Redburn) and starves alt-nets of capital (construction velocity down at least 40%; G.Network, Spring Fibre and Trooli failed; toob's equity at zero) → line losses fall (203k, 192k, 175k in the last three quarters) → Openreach revenue stabilises → BT can fund the defence (the TalkTalk purchase). TalkTalk was more than half of line losses (Morgan Stanley) and paid about £40m a month in rentals; under BT ownership the deliberate migration off Openreach stops, though its retail customers still churn to alt-net ISPs (CityFibre says under 5% of its 2026 connections came from TalkTalk customers, which suggests the remaining leakage is modest).
  • Our line-loss path: about 720k (FY27, against the 800k guide; H1 367k), 550k (FY28), 480k (FY29), 450k (FY30), 430k (FY31). Against a flat-loss world that is about +£50m of EBITDA in FY28 and about +£70m by FY30, and it lets Openreach revenue grow about 2% to 2.5% a year.
  • Balancing forces inside this loop: CityFibre's £2.3bn refinancing (including £500m of equity) and the CMA's counterfactual of CityFibre buying Netomnia would create a funded wholesaler; debt-free restructured alt-nets can price below Openreach; Sky's multi-sourcing on CityFibre; the about 1.0m third-party lines on PlatformX may prefer a neutral wholesaler. Migration friction (a year or more to integrate a second network, Morgan Stanley; KPN saw no active wholesale migration) means these forces act on incremental volume first.
  • Reinforcing loop 3, financial (strongest over four years; slower than assumed). Capital expenditure falls → normalised free cash flow rises (£1.95bn to £3.05bn by FY30) → net debt falls → BBB+ metrics → distributions within the headroom → re-rating → cheaper capital. What slows it: pension cash stays at £770m a year to FY30 (no release, see Read this first); cash tax rises from £90m to £250m in FY28; restructuring and TalkTalk cash in FY27 and FY28; dividends grow. Result: shareholder cash after dividends is negative in FY27 and only about £0.6bn to £0.7bn a year in FY28 and FY29, so BBB+ metrics arrive about FY30 and a buyback about FY31. KPN's precedent shows the end-state (full free cash flow returned at a 2.5x ceiling) but also that distribution targets slow deleveraging once begun.
  • Balancing loop 1, retail price elasticity (medium confidence; caps Consumer). Annual money-amount rises → tier-down, MVNOs (Monzo from £8, AO £12 for 500GB, Lebara from £5), fixed wireless (VodafoneThree targeting over 4m), Starlink £32 rurally → front-book discounting to hold volume → broadband ARPU flat to down. It is why Consumer EBITDA stays about £2.5bn in our model despite annual rises, and why price contributes net only about +0.5% to +1% a year (P-04).
  • Balancing loop 2, regulatory reaction (the loop that grows with BT's success). A higher retail share (about 31% to 36% with TalkTalk), falling line losses and rising Openreach returns (Bernstein sees regulated returns above 15% by about 2031 before re-basing to 8% to 10% by 2035) invite intervention: Ofcom blocked the deepest discount; the CMA's review of TalkTalk could impose behavioural undertakings or a sale of PlatformX (VMO2 has demanded divestment); the post-2031 review could re-base returns. Inside four years its effect is concentrated in the TalkTalk outcome: a forced sale of PlatformX would remove about 1.0m wholesale lines from BT's control and leave the £400m spent with less benefit, worth about -£20m to -£40m of FY29 EBITDA against our base and more line-loss risk.
  • The VMO2 wholesale loop (ambiguous). VMO2 halting its build and discussing Openreach access in rural areas (about 40% of the UK) adds no lines (those homes are already on Openreach) but adds a retail competitor in BT's best areas; we treat it as neutral to Openreach revenue and mildly negative for Consumer.
  • Net effect on the line items. Revenue: Openreach +2% to +2.5% a year (loop 2) against Consumer +0.7% (balancing loop 1). EBITDA: +£100m to +£150m a year from loops 1 and 2 against flat Consumer. Cash and balance sheet: loop 3 delivers about £2.5bn of deleveraging by FY30, but no buyback before FY31. The skew over 18 months is to the upside on line losses and to the downside on the regulatory loop (a binary CMA outcome on TalkTalk).

For the model: line losses well below guidance (about 720k, 550k, 480k); Openreach revenue +2% to +2.5% a year from FY28; Consumer EBITDA flat; no shareholder-return acceleration before FY31; a TalkTalk downside case with PlatformX divested.

Risks

C+592nd of 5

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BT's forecasts for the next 18 months are more predictable than most equity forecasts, and the investment case built on them is less predictable than the forecasts. The evidence for the first half of that sentence: the change from the past has one cause, the end of the build, which is physical and in management's hands (D-03); the forecast for FY27 and FY28 leans on strengths BT has shown, namely the capital programme, cost and labour reduction, Openreach's profit and landing inside guided ranges (D-02); no external driver moves FY28 earnings per share by more than about 2% (D-06); the operating drivers are published every quarter, with two prints and three dated events inside six months (D-05); and the cone is narrow, about ±2% for EBITDA and ±8% for earnings per share (D-04).

Three qualifications matter for the memo.

  • The forecasts themselves needed correcting. One input (the FY27 revenue growth rate) was stated against the wrong base and made the model's earnings about 3.5p too low in every year; it is corrected. One judgement (a fast fall in depreciation) does not survive the reported first half or a like-for-like comparison with capital expenditure; I have moved it to the consensus path. After both, earnings per share are 18.3p, 19.0p, 20.6p, 22.0p and about 23.5p, growing about 6% a year, within 3% of consensus throughout. The second set's claim of earnings 5% to 7% above consensus in FY29 and FY30 becomes about 3% in FY29 and nothing in FY30. The cash forecast is unchanged and remains above consensus for FY29 and FY30.
  • The least predictable figure is the most watched one. FY27 normalised free cash flow needs about £1.76bn in the second half; I put about 65% on a clean print of £1.95bn or more. Its composition (working capital and financing lines) will say more than its total.
  • Three items below EBITDA will change the reported picture without changing the economics, and none is in consensus in a consistent way. The pension notes add about £2.4bn to reported net debt at March 2027 and may add up to 0.7p of finance cost to adjusted earnings in FY28; TalkTalk will take about 0.6p to 0.7p off earnings per share when it enters the adjusted figures; the EE amortisation has flattered FY27 by about 1.1p and cannot do so again. The company's disclosure does not allow any of the three to be tracked before May 2027 (D-05, D-06).

Where I differ from the earlier sets, in one list. Revenue-growth input (arithmetic; corrected). Depreciation path (changed). Pension notes inside net debt (the second set assumed outside). TalkTalk probably not consolidated before clearance (the second set consolidated from October; inputs kept, core figures given). Line losses of 550k in FY28 (I think about 650k is as likely; immaterial to earnings). A first statement on distributions in May 2027 as a positive step (the first set; I expect no buyback). The TNT Sports guarantee as an unverified upside (it ends real payments of about £190m a year that were outside both normalised free cash flow and our bridge, and leaves a £184m receipt due).

The steer. Treat the operating and cash forecast as dependable to within the ranges in D-04, and treat the multiple as the uncertain term: it is about half sector-driven (D-07), it stands at a company-specific premium to its own history, and at the guideline multiple the model's return stays negative after the corrections. The case is predictable enough to hold through noise, and not predictable enough at this price to make the noise harmless.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D613rd of 5

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the forecast for the next 18 months relies almost entirely on strengths BT has demonstrated: delivering a capital programme, taking cost and labour out, growing Openreach's profit, and landing inside a guided EBITDA range and on a guided cash figure. The forecast beyond 18 months, and every part of it that sits above consensus, relies on things BT has not yet demonstrated: turning gross savings into group EBITDA growth, growing revenue, turning round a distressed retail business, and letting depreciation fall. The cash thesis is therefore plausible on the record; an earnings-growth thesis above about 5% a year is not.

  • Demonstrated, and what demonstrates it.
  • The capital programme. The build peak passed on schedule; accrued capital expenditure fell 5% in the first half; premises passed fell from 4.8m in FY26 to 1.2m in the half; unit cost stayed within £250 to £350. Royal KPN (capital expenditure from €1.2bn to under €1.0bn in 2027) and Chorus (gross capital expenditure down NZ$375m in the year to June 2026) show that a post-build fall of this size is ordinary, and the AlphaSense plausibility report rates this part "high confidence". Two small slips (the 25m target moved three months; unit cost "just above the midpoint") do not change it.
  • Cost and labour out, without service damage, for one year at the required pace. Total labour resource fell 11% to 90,000 in the year to September 2026 while the group Net Promoter Score rose 5.6 points and Openreach repair volumes fell 16%. The target (65,000 to 70,000 by FY30) needs about 8% a year for three and a half more years. The research found no incumbent that cut a quarter of its labour in under four years with stable service (Telia and Tele2 managed 15% in one year; Deutsche Telekom and Orange run at 3% to 4% a year). BT has done one such year; repeating it three times is not yet demonstrated, and the route matters: attrition and subcontractors are cheap, paid exits now cost about £159k each.
  • Openreach profit growth. EBITDA +4% on revenue +1% in the first half, from indexation, fibre mix and fewer faults. This is the one division where savings visibly reach profit.
  • Meeting guided ranges. EBITDA inside the range for three years; the cash floor met for three years (£1,508m against £1,500m in FY26), though with £476m of factoring and supply-chain finance in FY26.
  • Removing tail risks by action. TalkTalk bought within weeks; the pension settled early.
  • Not demonstrated, and what the forecast asks.
  • Group EBITDA growth from savings. £1.7bn of gross savings in two and a half years left EBITDA flat (£8,100m, £8,209m, £8,230m). The forecast asks for +1.0% to +1.3% a year from FY28. The mechanism is credible (the legacy voice drag of £150m to £200m a year halves in FY28; International has gone), but it is a forecast of something that has not happened under this management. The FY26 bonus outcome (EBITDA at 77% of the internal target) says internal plans have run ahead of delivery.
  • Labour leaving as fast as capitalised work falls. BT capitalised £1,388m of direct and £807m of indirect labour in FY26, 48% of staff cost. Redburn's ratio is £600m back into operating cost for each £1bn of capital expenditure cut. My reading of the evidence is more favourable than that ratio for two reasons: capitalised labour barely moved (£1,412m to £1,388m) while the build ran at its record, which means the marginal build was done by subcontractors whose cost simply ends; and the first half showed no sign of it (capital expenditure -5%, labour -11%, EBITDA +1%). But the real test is the half now under way: accrued capital expenditure must fall about 28% in the second half of FY27 against a year earlier (about £1.97bn against £2.73bn) for the guidance to hold. Management reconfirmed EBITDA guidance five weeks into that half, which is the best available evidence; the proof is the May 2027 result and the own-work-capitalised figure in the June 2027 annual report. No comparable disclosure was found for Chorus or Royal KPN, so there is no peer base rate.
  • Revenue growth. Service revenue fell 1% in the first half; Consumer broadband revenue per user fell 3%; the revenue outlook was cut in November 2024. Growth "from FY28" rests on a dated event (the switch-off) rather than on commercial execution, which is why I give it more credit than the commercial record alone would deserve; +0.7% for the core in FY28 is a fair figure, with the risk on the retail side.
  • A distressed retail turnaround. BT has no record of integrating a loss-making, low-price base, cannot start while the order stands, and the one precedent in the files (Telecom Plus's purchase of TalkTalk customers) showed churn of 10% to 20% around migration. The second set's swing from -£50m to +£130m of EBITDA is 1.2p of the roughly 5p of earnings growth to FY31.
  • Depreciation falling. It has not started; the first half says so (D-01).
  • The mid-term targets themselves. About £3.0bn of free cash flow and £3.5bn of gross savings by FY30 are the first long-dated targets this team has set. The cash target depends on capital expenditure (demonstrated) far more than on EBITDA (not), which is why the research rates it "highly likely" while rating the savings conversion "low confidence".
  • What the EE roll-off says about the quality of the near-term earnings. About 1.1p of FY27 earnings per share and about 0.3p of FY28 come from an amortisation charge ending. Without it, core earnings per share would fall about 2% to 3% in FY27. Consensus growth of 6% in FY28 needs about £90m more EBIT from operations with interest flat. That is achievable, but it shows that the first two years of earnings growth owe more to accounting roll-off than to the transformation.
  • Peers, as a test of plausibility. Post-build incumbents do grow profit modestly: Royal KPN's EBITDA after leases is up 0.8% in 2026 to date; Chorus grew EBITDA 3% in the year to June 2026 on lower maintenance cost. Neither faces BT's retail discounting, and Chorus has no infrastructure competitor. Vodafone Germany (EBITDA after leases down €1.1bn in three years as cable volumes fell) is the reminder of what a fixed-cost network does when volume leaves; Openreach's falling line losses are what keeps BT away from that path. The precedents support +1% a year, not more.
  • The balance. Of the 0.7p of earnings growth from FY27 to FY28, about 0.4p is the depreciation line (mostly EE) and about 0.6p is core EBITDA, less 0.3p for TalkTalk, interest, tax and share count. Of the £277m rise in normalised free cash flow, capital expenditure contributes £400m. So the 18-month forecast rests on the capital programme (demonstrated), a 1% rise in core EBITDA (plausible, half-demonstrated in the first half's +0.9%) and an accounting roll-off (certain).

  • What sets the width of the FY28 earnings cone. Core EBITDA ±£150m is ±1.2p; depreciation ±£60m is ±0.5p; TalkTalk ±£40m is ±0.3p; tax ±0.1p. If these were the only sources and independent, the range would be about ±1.3p. Two one-sided items widen the lower side: the accretion on the pension notes if it is charged within adjusted finance costs (up to -0.7p), and a harsher TalkTalk outcome.
  • Why the skew is mildly negative.
  • Management meets and does not exceed. The cash floor was met by £8m in FY26; guidance was reconfirmed, not raised, after a 3.2% EBITDA beat. The upper side of each guided line is therefore capped by behaviour, while the lower side is not.
  • The items not yet in anyone's numbers are mostly costs: note accretion, TalkTalk's losses, restructuring cost per leaver (tripled in FY26), cash capital expenditure lagging the booked figure.
  • The upside items are real but small in earnings: 100k fewer line losses is 0.15p; the top of the EBITDA range against its middle is 0.4p; a larger copper roll-off in depreciation is unknown; the £184m TNT Sports receipt and the Verizon payment are cash, not earnings.
  • FY27 normalised free cash flow, the weakest link. The first half delivered £236m. The second half must deliver about £1.76bn, against £939m a year earlier.
  • The arithmetic. Second-half EBITDA of about £4.07bn, less leases, cash interest and cash tax of about £0.88bn, leaves about £3.2bn before capital expenditure and working capital. To reach £1.76bn, cash capital expenditure less the working-capital inflow must be about £1.43bn. With cash capital expenditure of £1.83bn (the second set), working capital must return about £0.4bn; with £2.05bn (Morgan Stanley's full-year figure), about £0.6bn. The first half absorbed about £0.47bn of working capital (the anchor-product price change, about £245m of bills of exchange repaid, capital creditors paid).
  • The judgement. About 65% that BT prints £1.95bn or more; about 20% for £1.85bn to £1.95bn, presented as "about £2.0bn" with a timing explanation; about 15% below £1.85bn. Management has the levers (supply-chain finance and bills of exchange can be redrawn, creditor timing, copper sales), a record of landing on the figure, and 27.5% of the bonus on it. Deutsche Bank calls the required reversal "extreme and unprecedented"; Redburn expects it to reverse; Vodafone's first half shows that back-ended cash is ordinary in the sector.
  • Quality matters as much as the figure. If the target is met by redrawing the financing repaid in the first half, the headline holds and the bears' case on cash quality is strengthened. The memo should judge the print on the working-capital and financing lines, not on the total.
  • Dated events inside the window that can move the cone.
  • 15 December 2026: the CMA's final decision on nexfibre and Netomnia. A block is likely (about 80%), since the buyer offered no remedies. It is largely priced and it leaves the alternative path of CityFibre as consolidator open.
  • The TalkTalk public interest decision: no date in the files. My probabilities: clearance with behavioural undertakings about 55%; a required sale of PlatformX about 30%; a required sale of the retail base or an unwinding about 15%. The financial range in FY28 is small (±0.3p); the range for line losses and for sentiment is larger.
  • 31 January 2027: the switch-off of the old telephone network, with a fallback service from April 2027. A delay shifts when the voice drag ends, not whether.
  • Early February 2027: the third-quarter trading update. 13 May 2027: FY27 results and FY28 guidance. Late July 2027, early November 2027, early February 2028 and May 2028: the four further prints inside 18 months.
  • Second half of 2027: completion of the Verizon joint venture and receipt of $625m (about £470m), which is about three quarters of FY28's surplus after dividends on the second set's bridge (£639m).
  • Checks on the width from outside.
  • Broker forecasts for FY28 earnings per share run from 11.8p to 22.0p, but most of that is definition (Oddo BHF and Barclays on a reported-type basis; two brokers still including International). On a like-for-like adjusted basis the range is about 18.0p to 22.0p, with UBS alone lower at 15.2p on falling EBITDA. That is about ±10% around 20p: a similar width to mine, centred about 1p higher because the bullish brokers assume lower depreciation.
  • For EBITDA the like-for-like broker range for FY28 is £7.95bn (UBS) to £8.47bn (Goldman Sachs), ±3%.
  • Refinancing is a second-order risk inside 18 months: £372m and £1,013m mature in FY27 and FY28 against £16bn of fixed-rate debt; a one-point rise in rates lowers net finance cost by about £107m through hedges and cash (BT's own sensitivity).
  • Why a narrow cone does not make a predictable case. The model values the shares at a guideline multiple of 7.25 times two-year-forward earnings at the 18-month column, against about 10.9 times today on our earnings (FactSet's 11.34 times appears to rest on the mean of brokers, which the reported-basis forecasts pull down; it remains the pinned figure). After my two input changes, my arithmetic gives a return at that multiple of about -19% to March 2028 and about -6% to September 2029, against the model's present -27% and -15%: the multiple takes about -33%, earnings add about +11% and dividends about +7% over the 18-month column. The return is nil at about 9.1 times. An ±8% error in earnings is worth 8 points of return; the difference between 7.25 times and today's multiple is worth 33. The predictability of the forecasts is therefore necessary and not sufficient.
D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

B+671st of 5

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: very helpful for the operating drivers and for cash, with two company prints inside the window (early February and 13 May 2027) and three dated external events; unhelpful for exactly the three items on which our assessment differs from what consensus carries (depreciation, the treatment of the pension notes, and TalkTalk), none of which will be visible before May and one of which needs the annual report in June. The case can be monitored well for whether it is on track, and poorly for whether it is ahead of or behind estimates below EBITDA.

  • What BT gives every quarter (the first set lists these). Premises passed, fibre net additions and take-up; Openreach revenue per line and broadband line losses; revenue and EBITDA by division; revenue per user and churn by product; convergence; labour resource; savings and cost to achieve; capital expenditure and net debt. This is better than the sector norm and covers every operating driver in the forecast.
  • What to read at the third-quarter update, with thresholds (my judgement).
  • Openreach line losses. At or below about 175k supports our 720k for the year and the claim that owning TalkTalk helps; above about 200k says it does not, and puts the guide of about 800k back in play. Any change of wording on the 800k guide matters more than the figure.
  • EBITDA. A third quarter at or above the first-half quarterly average of about £2.05bn keeps the top half of the range in reach. Openreach growth (+4% in the first half) is the line to watch for the first sign of labour returning from capitalised work to operating cost as the build slows.
  • Capital expenditure. The second-half step-down has to be visible: about £1.0bn accrued in the quarter, against a first-half quarterly average of £1.14bn.
  • Cash. If the nine-month normalised free cash flow is given, as in earlier updates, a figure below about £0.8bn would leave the fourth quarter needing more than £1.2bn, which is more than the whole second half of FY26. Net debt should be falling from £21.0bn on the old basis; on the new basis it will include the notes, and the update is the first chance to see how BT presents them.
  • Consumer. Broadband revenue per user (-3% in the first half) and net additions (-7k) after a discount season that the research describes as a price war among alternative networks.
  • TalkTalk. Whether BT gives any figures, and how it describes the accounting.
  • What the May results add. The cash print and its composition; FY28 guidance (revenue growth, EBITDA, capital expenditure, free cash flow); the depreciation charge for the year (the test of my change and of Morgan Stanley's path); net finance cost with the first months of note accretion; TalkTalk's treatment; the dividend; any words on distributions.
  • What is not disclosed inside the window.
  • Depreciation, interest and earnings per share are half-yearly; nothing below EBITDA appears in February.
  • Own work capitalised and the asset register by class are annual (June 2027); the carrying value and remaining lives of copper are not disclosed at all.
  • The bridge from gross savings to EBITDA is never given.
  • TalkTalk's revenue, profit and churn since October are not yet published; BT has said figures follow once its accounting is aligned.
  • The terms after the TNT Sports guarantee, and the timing of the £184m.
  • External markers inside six months.
  • The CMA's nexfibre decision (15 December) and any step on TalkTalk.
  • CityFibre's quarterly net additions (108k, then 118k): the best outside gauge of Openreach's losses.
  • Virgin Media O2's quarterly figures and any signed agreement to use Openreach.
  • UK inflation each month: it sets the size of the April retail price rise in real terms and, in September 2027, Openreach's indexation for FY29.
  • The ten-year gilt yield, for the multiple rather than for the forecast.
  • Peers. Royal KPN and Deutsche Telekom give a comparable quarterly set; Royal KPN adds an explicit free-cash-flow bridge that BT does not. BT's omission is not the operating data but the reconciliation from its preferred cash measure to the cash a shareholder receives.
D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

D563rd of 5

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the chain from what BT promises to cash is clear and can be followed with published measures; the chain from what BT promises to earnings per share breaks in two places. The first break is between gross cost savings and EBITDA, where the company publishes both ends and nothing in between. The second is between EBITDA and earnings per share, where depreciation, interest and tax are unguided and where three accounting changes are in motion at once. The external transmission from the value chain to earnings per share is clear in mechanism and small in size: over 18 months the value chain moves BT's multiple far more than its earnings.

  • Chains that trace cleanly.
  • Build to capital expenditure to free cash flow. Premises passed a year (4.8m in FY26, about 2.1m in FY27, about 1m to 1.5m a year after) times a unit cost of £250 to £350 gives the build spend; the guide is "more than £1bn below FY26"; capital expenditure passes one for one into normalised free cash flow. Each step has a published measure.
  • Fibre take-up to faults to Openreach cost. Take-up 41%; fibre fault rate about a third of copper's; repair volumes -16%; Openreach cost down £55m in the half while revenue rose £31m.
  • The voice drag to service revenue. £150m to £200m a year, ending after 31 January 2027; a dated event with a stated size.
  • Openreach price. September inflation sets April indexation; revenue per line is published quarterly.
  • The first break: gross savings to EBITDA. BT reports £1.7bn of gross annualised savings and a £3.5bn target, and EBITDA that has not moved. It does not publish the absorbers (pay, the voice loss, labour returning from capitalised work, reinvestment). The second set's working assumption that about a fifth of each year's gross saving reaches EBITDA is an inference from outcomes, not a disclosed relationship. This is the main reason the largest stated ambition of the transformation cannot be tracked through to profit.
  • The second break: EBITDA to earnings per share.
  • Earnings per share is guided only as "low-single-digit growth". Depreciation (£4.7bn, larger than EBIT), net finance cost and the tax rate are not guided.
  • Three changes are under way without stated effects: the end of the EE amortisation (worth more than the guided growth in FY27); the pension notes (a new accretion charge of uncertain classification, and a matching fall in pension interest, which BT already treats as a specific item); and TalkTalk (inside or outside the adjusted figures, from an unstated date).
  • The data carry four definitions of earnings per share (reported 11.0p, FactSet adjusted 15.4p, the Viewforth definition 17.5p and the brokers' adjusted 18.3p for FY26), and published broker forecasts mix at least two of them.
  • A third difficulty: three measures of cash. For FY27, normalised free cash flow is about £1.95bn; the cash left for shareholders after pension, restructuring and TalkTalk is about £0.5bn, less than the dividend of about £0.83bn; and reported net debt will rise by about £2.8bn because the notes are counted. All three are correct. The company guides and pays bonuses on the first.
  • External transmission, driver by driver (my arithmetic on the second set's sensitivities).
External driver Route Size Clarity
Alternative-network and cable competition at wholesale Openreach line losses 100k lines: about £19m of EBITDA, about 0.15p Clear, small, quarterly measure
UK inflation Openreach indexation the following April; wage settlements One point on external Openreach revenue: about £25m to £30m, about 0.2p, offset by about £25m of expensed pay Clear, lagged up to 18 months
Retail discounting Consumer broadband revenue per user 1%: about £40m of revenue at a high margin, about 0.3p Clear in mechanism, unpredictable in size
Gilt yields Refinancing of £2.7bn over three years; the pension deficit; the multiple About 0.3p to 0.4p by FY29 through interest; the multiple is the main route Clear for earnings (small); dominant for valuation
Regulators Discrete decisions (anchor price, TalkTalk remedies) About £100m of working capital in FY27; ±0.3p for TalkTalk in FY28 Dated or undated events, sizes mostly stated
  • What follows for predictability. No single external driver moves FY28 earnings per share by more than about 2%; the business is insulated by contracts, indexation and fixed-rate debt. That supports the narrow cone in D-04. But the things an investor most needs to trace to earnings (the transformation and the three accounting changes) are those the company describes least, so the earnings line will be learned from results, not anticipated from disclosure.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

F3432nd of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the perception risks are larger than the fundamental ones at today's price, because the stock sits near the top of its own valuation range in a sector the market is actively de-rating as a leveraged yield group.

  • Rates. A further rise in UK or US 10-year yields (gilts about 5.3%; US near 5%) hits telecoms as a group; the Fitness for Fighting digest's own trigger for lifting Telecoms is a US 10-year below 4.5%.
  • Sector narratives. The AI-agent switching narrative (Barclays, 20 October 2026) caps multiples until churn data disprove it; satellite headlines (Starlink pricing, a Starlink listing); peer results (Vodafone, Telefonica) read across.
  • Supply. A block sale by Bharti (24.95%) or Deutsche Telekom (12.06%); the register is tight (about 40.8% in three hands), which also enables sharp upward moves.
  • Positioning. Hedge-fund net exposure fell from 6.8% to 4.6% by mid-2026 (lower crowding risk); options in mid-2026 priced a 4.4% probability of the shares below 100p.
  • Sell-side split. Six of the eleven visible brokers rate Sell or Underweight (targets 143p to 180p) against four Buy (219p to 340p); a downgrade wave after a weak H2 cash print would move a stock already pinned F on earnings momentum.
  • Post-results behaviour. The shares fell from about 231p before the H1 results to about 216p despite a beat, a sign that de-risking news is already priced.

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

F4232nd of 42

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: a meeter, not an upgrader. This management sets cash and EBITDA ranges it reliably lands inside, raises cost targets and lowers headcount targets, but has not raised profit or cash guidance; the one change to top-line guidance was a cut.

  • Reconfirmed rather than raised after the Q1 beat (23 July 2026) and the H1 beat (5 November 2026, Q2 EBITDA +3.2% against consensus).
  • Raised: the gross savings target (£3.0bn to £3.7bn in May 2026); the labour target (to 65,000 to 70,000 in November 2026).
  • Cut: the revenue outlook in November 2024 on weak non-UK trading.
  • Narrow beats: FY26 normalised free cash flow £1,508m against a £1,500m floor; FY26 EBITDA at 77% of the internal bonus target.
  • Style: combative toward short-sellers rather than conservative-then-beat; contrast Deutsche Telekom (raised three times in fiscal 2025) and Orange (an early 2026 raise; "classic sandbagger").
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

D5622nd of 42

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: over one year, small beats against consensus and guidance met; over three years, guidance met on EBITDA and cash with one revenue cut; over five years, a mixed record that includes the dividend suspension and the profit warnings of the previous management. In comparable conditions (inflation and rising rates, 2022 to 2024), BT held EBITDA through CPI-plus pricing and cost.

  • One year. Q1 FY27: revenue +0.6% and EBITDA +0.2% against consensus; line losses 192k against 198k. Q2 FY27: revenue £4,428m against £4,311m (+2.5%), EBITDA £2,084m against £2,020m (+3.2%), UK service revenue +0.6%, line losses 175k against 198k (12% better); Openreach EBITDA £1,145m against £1,106m. Business EBITDA missed by 0.7% in Q1. Deutsche Bank: Q2 adjusted earnings per share 8.2p against its 9.2p. FY26: adjusted EBIT 2.5% ahead of consensus (Deutsche Bank).
  • Three years. EBITDA within guided ranges FY24 to FY26; normalised free cash flow met or beat (£1,598m in FY25 "comfortably" above expectations; £1,508m against a £1,500m floor in FY26); revenue outlook cut in November 2024; FY26 EBITDA 77% of internal target.
  • Five years. The 2020 dividend suspension and 2022 rebase; Business revenue -21% and EBITDA -45% from 2020 to 2024; earlier International profit warnings (Morgan Stanley: "a historical source of multiple profit warnings").
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

C-5511th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the forecasts deserve high weight for the next 18 months on the lines management controls and guides (cost, capital expenditure, EBITDA range, normalised free cash flow), moderate weight for FY29 to FY31, and low weight on any revenue or commercial improvement management has not guided. The right lean is asymmetric by line: lean EBITDA and line losses up against guidance and consensus, lean FY27 cash slightly down within the guided "about £2.0bn", lean Business and TalkTalk down, and lean depreciation down against consensus. This management meets what it chooses to promise and chooses promises it can meet; its record says nothing yet about the mid-term targets, which no part of the current team has had to deliver.

  • What the record supports (three years, current management).
  • EBITDA inside the guided range FY24 to FY26; FY27 range (£8.1bn to £8.2bn) reconfirmed after a 3.2% Q2 EBITDA beat; Openreach beat by 3.5%.
  • Normalised free cash flow at or above the floor: £1,598m (FY25, "comfortably"), £1,508m (FY26) against £1,500m.
  • Capital expenditure peak passed on schedule; phase-one transformation finished a year early (2020); £1.7bn of savings by H1 FY27; labour -11% in a year; five disposals and the Verizon joint venture agreed.
  • What the record does not support.
  • Revenue: the outlook was cut in November 2024; Business EBITDA -5% (FY26) and -7% (H1 FY27); digital channel share paid 0% of target in FY26.
  • Internal plans run ahead of delivery: FY26 EBITDA was £8,212m against an internal bonus target of £8,270m (77% payout).
  • Small slips: 25m premises moved from December 2026 to March 2027; unit build cost drifted to "just above the midpoint" of £250 to £350.
  • Quality of the cash beats: FY26's £8m margin over the floor came with £476m of factoring and supply-chain finance and £269m of bills of exchange (about 30% of normalised free cash flow, Morgan Stanley). Meeting a cash floor exactly while drawing on financing levers is the pattern to expect again in FY27, when H2 must deliver about £1.76bn (H2 FY26: £939m), mainly from a cash capital expenditure step-down of about £0.8bn against H2 FY26.
  • The previous era (2016 to 2023): the Italian accounting scandal, International profit warnings, the 2020 dividend suspension and rebase. The current team is not that team, but the FY30 targets (£3.0bn of free cash flow, £3.5bn of savings, 65,000 to 70,000 labour) are the first long-dated targets this team has set.
  • Weights we apply.
  • FY27 and H1 FY28: about 80% confidence that EBITDA lands within ±1% of our figure; about 70% that core normalised free cash flow is £1.9bn to £2.1bn.
  • FY29 to FY31: about 50% to 60% confidence on EBITDA within ±3%; depreciation and capital expenditure higher confidence (mechanical); TalkTalk and decapitalisation lower.
  • Direction of lean, line by line (relative to guidance and consensus).
  • EBITDA FY27: up (top half of the range, core £8.19bn), because H1 was +0.9% on the continuing base and the Q2 beat was in the highest-margin division.
  • Line losses: up (better than 800k), because the guide implies H2 worse than H1 (367k) just as TalkTalk's migration stops.
  • FY27 normalised free cash flow: slightly down within "about £2.0bn" (core about £2.0bn, group about £1.95bn with TalkTalk's losses), because the floor is met with no buffer and may use working-capital levers again.
  • Revenue FY28: up slightly (core +0.7% against consensus +0.1%), because the voice drag halves.
  • Business: down against consensus segment EBITDA (we have £1,195m in FY27 falling to about £1,125m; consensus mean £1,229m, £1,218m, £1,224m).
  • TalkTalk: down against management's "value accretive over time", on the Telecom Plus precedent and the CMA constraint.
  • Depreciation: down against consensus (P-01; P-08).
  • Capital expenditure: in line with guidance and consensus; the team has delivered the capital programme.
  • Peers as a check. Deutsche Telekom (raised three times in 2025) and Orange ("classic sandbagger") beat and raise; BT reconfirms. The implication for the fourth set: guidance raises are unlikely even when the numbers run ahead; the upside, where it comes, arrives as delivery at the May results rather than as a raised guide.

For the model: carry FY27 at the top half of EBITDA guidance and at the guided cash, not above it; carry FY28 to FY31 between consensus and the company's ambition, leaning up on Openreach and depreciation, down on Business and TalkTalk.
