Forecasts and upgrades

7

Forecasts with good growth rates and higher growth/margins than past

–not yet rated

Memo topic 6, : the Rater's score

7a

Growth rate

4 answers
Judgement57C-4928P-0845P-1058
Group judgement

Financial forecasts

B+574th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BT's next four years are an inflection below EBITDA in a business whose operations change slowly: revenue moves from slow decline to slow growth, core EBITDA from flat to about +1% a year, but depreciation, capital expenditure and interest all turn from headwinds to neutral or tailwinds together, so adjusted earnings per share grow about 8% a year after a flat FY27 and normalised free cash flow rises from about £1.95bn to about £3.2bn. That inflection is mostly mechanical (the build is past its peak; the fibre base lowers faults; the depreciable base shrinks), which makes it more predictable than an operating recovery, and it rests on a management that delivers cost, capital expenditure and cash ranges it sets. Its limits are equally specific: the gross savings reach EBITDA only about a fifth at a time because decapitalisation and wage inflation absorb them; retail pricing is nominal and is spent defending volume; financial leverage keeps interest at about £1.1bn and turns cash into deleveraging rather than returns until about FY30; and the pension agreement, contrary to the hopeful reading in the first set, releases no cash before FY32. Against consensus, the base case is in line for FY27 and FY28 (TalkTalk dilution offsetting a stronger core) and about 5% to 7% above for FY29 and FY30, the difference sitting in depreciation and in line losses rather than in any heroic operating assumption. The three swing items for the third and fourth sets are the CMA's TalkTalk outcome, the pace of decapitalisation against headcount, and whether the FY27 cash target is met without leaning again on working-capital financing.

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?F2841st of 42full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F4519th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: over the last six years the below-EBIT lines worked against the shareholder every year (interest up about £260m, cash tax deferred but accumulating, pension cash at about £800m a year, specific items of £0.4bn to £0.9bn a year), so adjusted earnings per share stayed at about 18p to 19p and reported fell from 17.5p to 11.0p. Over the next four years they turn broadly neutral for adjusted earnings (interest flat, tax rate drifting up about a point, no minorities, share count flat), they turn positive for reported earnings (specific items and pension interest shrink, so reported earnings per share grow faster than adjusted), and they remain a heavy but stable drag on shareholder cash (pension £770m a year to FY30, cash tax rising to £250m, restructuring falling to £100m). The share count does no work until FY31.

  • Interest (P-02). £1,085m (FY27) to £1,070m (FY31), against £748m (FY20) to £1,011m (FY26) on FactSet's line. The six-year headwind of about £260m becomes roughly zero. Cash interest runs about £130m below the profit and loss charge (£932m paid in FY26 including lease interest, Deutsche Bank). IAS 19 net interest (£191m in FY26; £100m in H1 FY27) is a specific item: excluded from adjusted, included in reported; it falls as the deficit closes.
  • Tax. Adjusted effective rate 22.1% (FY26) and 22.8% (H1 FY27), with a stable patent box benefit (£54m in FY26, £55m in FY25); we model 22.8% (FY27), 23.0% (FY28 and FY29), 23.5% (FY30 and FY31) as profits rise faster than the patent box benefit. Cash tax: about £90m (FY27), £250m a year (FY28 to FY30, Morgan Stanley), about £300m (FY31). BT carries about £11.3bn of tax losses, but loss relief is restricted to part of taxable profit, and full expensing tapers as capital expenditure falls, so cash tax resumes; the step of £160m in FY28 alone takes about 7% off that year's free cash flow growth.
  • Specific items and the reported-to-adjusted gap.
  • Restructuring: charges £448m (FY25), £336m (FY26), £85m in H1 FY27; cash £423m (FY25), £311m (FY26); falling to about £100m a year by FY30 (company).
  • TalkTalk: deal and administration costs and the about £100m of wholesale receivables never paid to Openreach in FY27; integration costs in FY28 and FY29 (not disclosed; we assume about £80m and £50m).
  • The gap between adjusted and reported earnings per share was 7.3p in FY26 (18.3p against 11.0p). On our path it narrows to about 5p in FY27 (reported about 13.5p) and about 1.2p to 1.5p by FY30 (reported about 22p), so reported earnings per share grow about 15% a year against about 8% for adjusted. This matters for the fourth set: the brokers forecasting on a reported or near-reported basis (Barclays 13.1p, Oddo BHF 11.9p for FY27) will show the steepest upgrades as the specific items fade, and part of the dispersion in published forecasts (FY29 12.6p to 25.5p) is this definitional gap, not a disagreement about the business.
  • Joint ventures and minorities.
  • TNT Sports: BT's ordinary equity is carried at nil (£218m impairment in FY26), so no further share of losses is recognised; the C preference shares stand at £184m; Warner Bros. Discovery holds a call option at 50% of fair value. Redburn (September 2024) expected the expiry of the minimum revenue guarantee in October 2026 to benefit BT's real cash flow by £200m to £250m a year; that is not in our numbers or, as far as the files show, in consensus, and it is the most material unverified upside below EBITDA.
  • Verizon joint venture: completion in the second half of calendar 2027 (FY28); $625m (about £470m) equalisation cash to BT at closing; BT's 50% share of earnings will be equity-accounted and small (International contributed only 1% to 2% of EBITDA); we include nil in adjusted earnings.
  • Minorities: none.
  • Share count and buybacks. FY26 shares outstanding 9,853.6m; employee plans add about 30m shares a year (Saveshare, Restricted Share Plan); purchases for employee plans run about £45m to £50m a year (£30m in FY26; £43m in H1 FY27). The pension covenant requires trustee consultation for any buyback other than for employee awards, for distributions above 120% of the threshold, or if normalised free cash flow falls below £1bn; management will not start enhanced returns before a 0.5x leverage reduction. We therefore model a first buyback of about £700m in FY31 (within £2.0bn of headroom less about £0.95bn of dividends), reducing the share count by about 1.4% in that year. Morgan Stanley forecasts no substantial buyback through FY30.
  • Dividend. Interim 2.5p (+2%); policy low-to-mid single-digit growth until BBB+ metrics; the covenant's headroom assumes 10% a year growth in FY28 and FY29 as a ceiling, not a plan. We model 8.55p (FY27), 8.85p, 9.2p, 9.6p and 10.1p (FY31), a payout of about 45% to 40% of adjusted earnings.
  • Pension cash (the resolved question). About £780m (FY27), £770m (FY28 to FY30), £670m (FY31), then about £180m (FY32 and FY33) and about £600m (FY34). The real release comes in FY32 (about £490m a year lower), just beyond the horizon; it is worth flagging to the fourth set as an 18-months-to-three-years narrative point that the market may misdate.
  • How the lines move against the last six years (base case).
  • Adjusted net income: about £1.8bn (FY26) to £1.81bn (FY27), £1.90bn, £2.11bn, £2.31bn, £2.47bn (FY31).
  • Adjusted earnings per share: flat at 18.1p to 18.8p in FY24 to FY26, then 18.3p, 19.3p, 21.4p, 23.3p, 25.3p (about +8% a year from FY27 to FY31). FY27 is flat because TalkTalk takes about 0.6p off a core figure of about 18.9p (core +3%, matching "low-single-digit growth").
  • Normalised free cash flow: £1.3bn to £1.6bn in FY22 to FY26, then £1.95bn to £3.24bn.
  • Shareholder cash before dividends (after pension, restructuring, TalkTalk, spectrum and the Verizon receipt): about £0.42bn in FY26 against £807m of dividends; about £0.48bn (FY27), £1.49bn (FY28, including about £0.47bn from Verizon), £1.59bn (FY29), £2.08bn (FY30), £2.42bn (FY31). Dividend cover by shareholder cash moves from about 0.5x (FY26 and FY27) to about 1.8x (FY29) and about 2.3x (FY30).

For the model: net financial items about -£1.1bn flat; tax rate 22.8% rising to 23.5%; share count +30m a year with buybacks only for employee plans until FY31; dividend 8.55p rising to 10.1p; carry pension, restructuring and TalkTalk cash outside the model's free cash flow but inside the net debt bridge.

P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B584th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: our base case is a business that meets its FY27 guidance at the top of the EBITDA range and at, not above, its cash target; that starts growing revenue slowly in FY28 as the voice drag ends and line losses fall; that grows core EBITDA about 1% a year because decapitalisation and inflation absorb most of the gross savings; and whose earnings per share and cash inflect mainly through depreciation and capital expenditure. TalkTalk is dilutive for two years and modestly accretive after, conditional on the CMA. The pension deal releases permission, not cash, so shareholder returns beyond the progressive dividend wait until BBB+ metrics are reached around FY30. Relative to the first set's steer, we are more confident on the line-loss and depreciation upside and less optimistic on FY28 shareholder cash.

  • The CMA's TalkTalk decision (public interest review; a forced sale of PlatformX is the downside case).

  • The FY27 results (13 May 2027): FY28 guidance (revenue growth; EBITDA; capital expenditure), the cash print against about £2.0bn, the TalkTalk synergy plan, the pension bonds' accounting, and the first statement on distributions under the new headroom.
  • Evidence on decapitalisation: own-work capitalised and Openreach engineering headcount in the FY27 annual report (June 2027).
  • September 2027 CPI, which sets FY29 Openreach indexation.
  • Probabilities (our judgement). Base 60%; bear (flat core EBITDA, flat depreciation, TalkTalk losses persist) 20%; bull (EBITDA +2% a year, faster depreciation fall, TalkTalk synergies) 20%. Expected FY29 earnings per share about 21.2p against consensus 20.0p; expected FY28 about 19.3p against 19.5p.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

C-6215th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: meaningful scope beyond cost, concentrated in a few big areas: the end of the voice drag, Openreach volume stabilisation now that TalkTalk is internal, the TalkTalk integration itself, the pension settlement, and from FY28 cash returns to shareholders. It is congruent with strategy, and peer precedents (KPN's fibre monetisation; Deutsche Telekom's German back-book price rise) show parts of it are achievable.

  • Few big areas.
  • Voice drag ending: £150m to £200m a year of service revenue stops falling from FY28.
  • Openreach volume: line losses below the 800k guide (H1 FY27 run-rate about 735k a year) with TalkTalk's deliberate migration off Openreach removed.
  • TalkTalk: retail cost overlap, migration of its lines to BT systems, better ARPU management (its scale and synergy plan are not in the files).
  • Copper pricing freedom from April 2029; copper and exchange monetisation.
  • Pension: lower deficit and possibly lower cash from FY28.
  • Capital returns: distribution headroom of up to £1.5bn in FY27 and £2.0bn by FY30 permits buybacks once leverage allows.
  • Many small areas. Business new products; network slicing; convergence upsell; energy.
  • Peer precedents. KPN: 15% return on invested capital through cost transformation and wholesale fibre monetisation; Deutsche Telekom: a €2 back-book broadband rise on 40% to 50% of the base with low churn; Orange: 10% annual adjusted earnings per share target 2025 to 2028 on 3% EBITDAaL growth via lower capital intensity and consolidation.

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: no deceleration; the revenue growth rate has improved from its trough and the input change forces are accelerating. The risk would be a renewed fall in service revenue once the handset-led H1 boost fades, so the measure to watch is service revenue, not total revenue.

  • Revenue growth: -2.2% (FY25), -3.6% (FY26, H2 -3.8%), +0.6% to +1% in H1 FY27 on the continuing base (the first growth in two and a half years, handset-led); service revenue -1% (voice); Q2 revenue £4,428m beat consensus by 2.5%.

C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

D5026th of 42

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: consensus forecasts a break from the past in capital expenditure, margin and cash, and continuity of the past in revenue, EBITDA and earnings per share; the break is credible because it is mechanical and in management's control, and the continuity is credible but probably slightly too cautious on revenue and line losses and too generous on cash quality.

  • Revenue. History about -2% a year (FY20 to FY26, including disposals); consensus continuing base flat to +1% a year (FY27 to FY29 after the -10.6% perimeter step). Credible; probably slightly low on FY28 given the voice drag ending and slowing line losses, and excludes TalkTalk.
  • EBITDA. History £8,100m, £8,209m, £8,230m (FY24 to FY26, brokers' basis); consensus £8,164m, £8,221m, £8,260m on the smaller continuing base: flat in pounds, so margin rises to about 46.5% to 46.8%. Credible; the cost programme against wage inflation, voice loss and Business decline.
  • Depreciation and amortisation. History rising from £4.3bn to about £4.9bn; consensus £4,694m to £4,592m (FY27 to FY30): only about £100m lower over three years despite capital expenditure falling about £1bn. Plausible because fibre assets are long-lived; it is the line where a broker could find upside if copper assets fully depreciate.
  • EBIT. History flat at about £3.3bn; consensus £3,406m, £3,502m, £3,631m (+3% a year). Credible.
  • Interest, tax and share count. Net interest about £1.0bn in history; consensus implies about £1.0bn to £1.16bn between EBIT and pre-tax (pre-tax £2,395m, £2,339m, £2,598m; the FY28 dip suggests some brokers include specific items). Tax drifting toward 25%. Shares flat at about 9.85bn.
  • Earnings per share. History flat to falling (18.5p, 18.8p, 18.3p); consensus 18.4p, 19.5p, 20.0p. Credible; the FY28 step of +6.1% is the one to test.
  • Capital expenditure. History £4.1bn to £5.2bn (24% to 26% of revenue); consensus £4,552m, £4,038m, £3,766m (26%, 23%, 21%). Credible; the build is physically past peak.
  • Free cash flow. History (normalised) £1.3bn to £1.6bn; consensus £1,972m, £2,189m, £2,503m. Credible on the company's definition; the shareholder-cash bridge (pension, restructuring, TalkTalk, cash tax) is where the uncertainty lies.
  • Dividend. 8.0p, 8.16p, 8.32p in history; consensus 8.6p, 8.9p, 9.2p. Credible.
  • Dispersion. FY29 earnings per share from about 12.6p (Oddo BHF) and 13.6p (UBS) to 25.5p (Morgan Stanley); part definitional (some brokers still include International or use a reported basis).

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B+584th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the break from the last six years is not at the top line or at EBITDA, which move only from slight decline to slight growth, but below EBITDA, where three lines that all went against the shareholder in FY20 to FY26 turn at once: depreciation stops rising and starts falling, capital expenditure falls by about a third, and interest stops rising. That makes the inflection mechanical and therefore more predictable than an operating recovery, but also slower: adjusted earnings per share grow about 8% a year from a flat FY27, normalised free cash flow rises about 65% from FY27 to FY31, and the balance sheet turns from rising net debt to about £0.6bn to £1.2bn a year of deleveraging. The drivers are concentrated: capital expenditure, depreciation and the net EBITDA effect of the cost programme explain almost all of the change, and TalkTalk is the one discretionary swing item.

  • Where the change comes from (FY27 to FY31). EBIT rises about £0.88bn: about £0.32bn from lower depreciation, about £0.38bn from core EBITDA (net cost savings and the end of voice, net of decapitalisation, Business and inflation) and about £0.18bn from TalkTalk turning from loss to profit. Interest contributes nothing, tax takes about 0.7 points. Normalised free cash flow rises about £1.3bn: capital expenditure about £0.85bn, EBITDA about £0.56bn, working capital about £0.15bn, cash tax about -£0.21bn.
  • Concentration (for the third set's D-03). Four drivers carry about 90% of the earnings-per-share change: depreciation (about 2.5p of the 7p gain), core EBITDA growth (about 3p), TalkTalk (about 1.2p swing from -0.6p to +0.6p), and the share count (about 0.3p in FY31). For cash, capital expenditure alone is about two thirds. A single driver that disappoints (decapitalisation absorbing the core EBITDA gain; depreciation flat as consensus assumes) removes about 2.5p to 3p from FY31 earnings per share.
  • Against consensus (median, 16 November 2026). FY27 group EBITDA £8,140m against £8,164m (core £8,190m above it; consensus excludes TalkTalk); earnings per share 18.3p against 18.4p (core 18.9p). FY28 19.3p against 19.5p. FY30 23.3p against 22.4p (one broker), where the depreciation gap (about £100m) is as large as the EBITDA gap (about £120m). The disagreement is back-ended and below EBITDA.
  • Skew and the cone. Bear case (core EBITDA flat, depreciation flat as consensus has it, interest about £50m to £90m higher, TalkTalk losing about £60m a year, tax 24% to 24.5%): earnings per share about 17.6p (FY27), 17.2p (FY28), 17.3p (FY29), 17.6p (FY30); normalised free cash flow about £2.0bn to £2.5bn; leverage still about 2.35x in FY30, so no buyback. Bull case (core EBITDA +2% a year, depreciation falling as Morgan Stanley has it toward about £4.25bn by FY30, TalkTalk +£160m by FY30): 18.7p, 21.4p, 24.7p, 27.7p; free cash flow about £3.4bn by FY30. Over 18 months the cone is narrow (FY28 earnings per share about 17.2p to 21.4p, base 19.3p) and roughly symmetric; beyond it the cone widens and skews modestly upward on depreciation and downward on TalkTalk and decapitalisation.

Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

–not yet rated

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

C+492nd of 5

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Our assessment is, for the next 18 months, very largely what guidance, consensus and the bullish narrative already say; the gap is small, and its sign changes with the horizon. The second set believed it had found earnings 5% to 7% above consensus in FY29 and FY30. After correcting the model's revenue base and bringing depreciation into line with the reported first half, the earnings forecast is within 3% of consensus in every year. What is left of the gap is specific.

  • In our favour. Operations at the favourable end of guidance (EBITDA at the top of the FY27 range, line losses about 80k better than the guide, Openreach above and Consumer below consensus in a slightly higher total); the mid-term capital-expenditure target met two years early; free cash flow for FY29 and FY30 about £0.2bn and £0.5bn above consensus; and, from FY28, reported net debt and reported earnings improving faster than the adjusted figures suggest.
  • Against us. A management that will not convert being ahead into raised guidance; a cash target for FY27 that is met, if it is met, without margin and probably with help from working-capital financing; and a run of presentational negatives due between February and May 2027 that estimates do not carry consistently: TalkTalk's losses entering earnings per share (about -3.5% in FY28), about £2.4bn of pension notes entering reported net debt, depreciation that disappoints the most bullish forecasts, and possibly note accretion in adjusted finance costs.

The order in which these arrive matters for the memo. Over the next six months the balance of surprises against published expectations is neutral for operations and slightly negative for earnings per share and balance-sheet headlines; the pinned F for sell-side momentum is likely to persist until mid-2027. Over months 6 to 18 the balance turns neutral to favourable: a first year of revenue growth, free cash flow of about £2.2bn, reported net debt falling by more than £1bn, and FY29 estimates drifting up. Beyond 18 months the favourable gap is in cash, not earnings.

What this means against the price. The shares stand at 215.8p, about 11 times two-year-forward earnings, the 91st percentile of five years, after rising 18% on the de-risking news and falling on the results that confirmed it. The model values them at the guideline multiple of 7.25 times; after my corrections the return at that multiple is, on my arithmetic, about -19% to March 2028 and about -6% to September 2029, and it is nil only at about 9.1 times. Nothing in guidance, estimates or the narrative is mispriced in our favour by enough to offset a fall in the multiple of that size: the favourable gaps are each worth a few per cent of earnings or a few hundred million pounds of cash. The case for the shares therefore has to rest on the multiple holding, which is half a sector question (D-07), and not on expectations being too low.

Signposts that would change this judgement. Favourably: a third-quarter line-loss figure well under 175k with the guide lowered; a May cash print at £2.0bn without recourse to the financing lines; FY28 guidance for EBITDA above £8.25bn and capital expenditure below £3.9bn; note accretion kept outside adjusted earnings; TalkTalk cleared on behavioural terms. Unfavourably: a cash print below £1.85bn; depreciation for FY27 above £4.72bn; a required sale of PlatformX; Consumer EBITDA falling; gilt yields higher.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

C+532nd of 5

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: the most likely path, at about 55% to 60%, is that every financial guidance line is met or reconfirmed at all six events in the next 18 months. A raise of a profit or cash line at any of them is about 20% likely, because this management meets and does not raise even when ahead. A reduction or a clear miss at any of them is about 20% to 25% likely, and nearly all of that sits in one place: FY27 normalised free cash flow at the May 2027 results. The good news will arrive in softer forms than a raised guide: a lower line-loss guide, the words "top end", a higher cost or labour ambition, and a capital-expenditure target met two years early. Our assessment sits at or just above guidance on operations and exactly on it for cash, so the gap in our favour is small.

  • The six events and my probabilities for the financial lines.
Event What is tested Raised Met or reconfirmed Reduced or missed
Third-quarter update, early February 2027 FY27 guidance 10% 85% 5%
FY27 results, 13 May 2027: EBITDA Core £8.1bn to £8.2bn 10% above the range 85% (65% in the top half) 5%
FY27 results: normalised free cash flow About £2.0bn under 5% above £2.05bn 65% at £1.95bn or more; 20% at £1.85bn to £1.95bn 15% below £1.85bn
FY27 results: line losses About 800k 80% better than 800k 15% 5%
First-quarter (late July 2027), half-year (early November 2027) and third-quarter (early February 2028) prints FY28 guidance 15% at any of the three 75% 10% at any of the three
FY28 results, May 2028 FY28 outcome 10% 80% 10%
  • What FY28 guidance is likely to contain when it is set on 13 May 2027, against consensus.
  • Revenue and UK service revenue: growth, the first such guide, as management has pre-announced "from FY28". Our core figure is +0.7%; consensus has +0.1%. Slightly above consensus, about 60% likely.
  • EBITDA: a range that brackets consensus (£8,221m), probably £8.2bn to £8.3bn for the core. Our core figure is £8,272m. A midpoint above consensus is about 45% likely; in line 45%; below 10%.
  • Capital expenditure: about £3.8bn to £3.9bn accrued (ours £3.85bn; FactSet's £4.04bn mixes cash, accrued and perimeters). This is the line most likely to read better than estimates.
  • Normalised free cash flow: about £2.2bn (ours £2.22bn; FactSet £2.19bn), after cash tax rising from about £90m to about £250m. In line.
  • Line losses: a lower figure than 800k.
  • TalkTalk: inside guidance only if the review has ended; otherwise excluded again. If included, it lowers the EBITDA and free-cash-flow guide by about £40m against the core figures above, before integration costs that BT would treat as specific items.
  • Distributions: a dividend rise of 2% to 4% and the unchanged policy. A buyback announcement by May 2028 is about 10% likely: management requires a 0.5 times fall in leverage first, net debt rises in FY27, and the pension agreement requires consultation with the trustee for any buyback beyond employee plans.
  • What would drive each outcome.
  • A raise (about 20% across the period). Third and fourth quarters holding the second quarter's EBITDA rate (3.2% above consensus); Openreach continuing at +4%; labour leaving faster than the build slows. Even then the record (reconfirmation after two beats in FY27; one top-line change in two years, a cut) says the outperformance is shown in the result, not in the guide. Deutsche Telekom, Orange, Telia, Telefonica and Vodafone all raised guidance in autumn 2026; BT did not, on a beat.
  • Met (the default). Contracted revenue; a cost programme in hand; a capital programme that is physically slowing; a team paid 55% of its bonus on adjusted EBITDA and normalised free cash flow.
  • A reduction or miss (about 20% to 25%). In order of likelihood: working capital not returning in the second half of FY27 (D-04); TalkTalk brought into guidance with its losses, which will read as a cut even though it is a change of perimeter; a retail price war through the winter (Consumer broadband revenue per user is already -3%); an above-plan pay settlement; cost from a difficult switch-off; a CMA remedy that removes PlatformX.
  • Two presentational changes that could be mistaken for guidance changes.
  • Leverage. The pension notes add about £2.4bn to reported net debt. If BT restates its leverage measure and its "0.5 times" path on the new basis, the starting point moves from about 2.5 times to about 2.8 times. The rating agencies already counted the pension deficit, so the economic path is unchanged (D-01).
  • Earnings per share. If the accretion on the notes is charged within adjusted finance costs, a guide of "growth" in FY28 becomes harder to meet (up to 0.7p, about 3.5%). I think it slightly more likely than not that BT presents the accretion as a specific item, as it does pension interest, but nothing in the files settles it.
  • Secondarily, 18 months to three years out (to late 2029).
  • Normalised free cash flow of about £3.0bn by FY30: held about 70%; raised or brought forward about 15%; reduced about 15%. Our FY30 figure is £3.05bn, on the target with no margin, in the same way that FY26 and FY27 sit on theirs. Morgan Stanley has £3.2bn. A cut would come from capital expenditure having to stay higher (faster fibre technology, mobile, the costlier premises beyond 25m), not from EBITDA.
  • Capital expenditure "more than £1bn below FY26": on our figures this is met in FY28 (£3.85bn accrued against £5.1bn), two years ahead of the period it was set for. A tighter target is about 40% likely within three years and is the most probable genuine raise.
  • Gross savings of £3.5bn and labour of 65,000 to 70,000: another increase in ambition is about 35% likely (both were raised during 2026). These are raises of input, not of profit.
  • Revenue growth from FY28: about 70% that FY28 shows growth on the core. If TalkTalk is first consolidated during FY28, reported revenue will grow about 2% to 3% for that reason alone.
  • Credit metrics consistent with BBB+ and enhanced distributions: reached about FY30 on our path; no change of target expected; the first buyback is an FY31 event in our model.
  • How conservative is the guidance against our assessment. EBITDA: we are at the top of the FY27 range (£8.19bn) and about £50m above the likely FY28 midpoint. Line losses: about 80k better than the FY27 guide. Capital expenditure: on the guide. Free cash flow: on the guide in FY27, FY28 and FY30, with nothing to spare in any of them. Earnings per share: the core, at 18.9p, is at the upper end of "low-single-digit growth", but more than all of that growth is the EE roll-off. The guidance is modestly conservative on operations and not conservative on cash.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

C+492nd of 5

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: over the next two events (February and May 2027) consensus EBITDA should edge up and consensus free cash flow should hold, but consensus earnings per share for FY28 is more likely to fall than to rise, for reasons of presentation and not of trading: TalkTalk entering the estimates, the bullish brokers' depreciation assumptions meeting the reported run-rate, and possibly the accretion on the pension notes. The breadth of revisions, now negative and pinned F, is therefore unlikely to turn clearly positive before the middle of 2027. Over months 6 to 18 the direction improves: FY29 earnings per share should drift up by about 2% to 3% and free-cash-flow estimates for FY29 and FY30 by more. Our assessment is within 3% of consensus earnings in every year, so there is no large upgrade to wait for.

  • Where consensus and its momentum stand (FactSet, 13 to 16 November 2026). Median earnings per share 18.4p, 19.5p and 20.0p for FY27 to FY29; EBITDA £8,164m, £8,221m and £8,260m; free cash flow £1,972m, £2,189m and £2,503m. Mean earnings per share for FY28 down 2% in one month and 3% in three; EBITDA flat and down 1%; target price down 1% and flat; share of buy ratings 40%, down 3 points; revision-momentum score -9; sell-side momentum pinned F. Six of the eleven visible brokers rate the shares Sell or Underweight. The mean target price is 229p and the median 219p against a price of 215.8p, so targets offer no pull either.
  • Our assessment against consensus, like for like.

  • Why EBITDA estimates should edge up. The first half was +0.9% on the continuing base and the second quarter beat by 3.2%. A revision screen counts this as a cut; it is a change of perimeter.

If its depreciation moves to the run-rate, its FY27 figure falls by about 1.6p and its later years by more. The same applies in smaller measure to the other brokers above 21p for FY28. The cuts most likely to come are from the bulls.
  • Note accretion (negative if adjusted, up to 3.5% in FY28). Unsettled; see I-01.
  • Specific items falling (positive for the mean). Specific operating costs were £68m in the first half against £177m. Brokers who publish on a reported-type basis (Barclays at 13.1p and Oddo BHF at 11.9p for FY27) will rise as these fall; our path has reported earnings per share growing about 15% a year. FactSet's mean, and so the revision counts, will register these as upgrades.

BT's estimate momentum is weak against a sector whose own momentum is unremarkable.

11d

Current multiple

–not yet ratedMemo topic 11, : the Rater's score1 answer
D-0744
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D443rd of 5

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: there is no measured answer, because the co-movement table holds no price history for BT. On the indirect evidence, the direction of BT's multiple over the last six months was set about half by the sector and half by the company, in two distinct legs: the sector's de-rating took the shares down from May to September, and a burst of company-specific news brought them back in October. The level of the multiple against BT's own history, by contrast, is company-driven: BT sits at the 82nd percentile of its ten-year range while the median telecom sits at about the 34th. A company-specific premium inside a sector the market is selling needs a continuing supply of company-specific good news, and that supply is now thinner and more two-sided.

  • The data gap. The table reads "no price history found for BT.A-GB". I have not estimated a correlation or a beta. What follows uses returns and valuation figures from the dashboard block (13 to 16 November 2026) and the sector digest.
  • The two legs of the last six months.
  • Sector leg, May to September. The 32 telecom stocks fell 14% relative to the market from 19 May; the median two-year-forward multiple fell 12% to the end of September while 2027 estimates held; the two shocks were satellite (June) and bond yields (11 September to 9 October). BT's price fell about 15% from mid-May to 196.55p on 27 September (my arithmetic from the six-month return of -7.1% to 215.8p), with one company-specific blow inside it (Ofcom's proposed block of 28 July).
  • Company leg, October to early November. BT rose about 18% in the month to 4 November (231.4p) on Ofcom's final decision, the TalkTalk purchase, the CMA's provisional finding and Virgin Media O2's retreat, while the sector was at the bottom grade of relative strength. It then fell about 7% after a results beat.
  • Net. Over six months, -7.1% with FY28 estimates up 3%: a fall in the multiple of about 10%, close to the sector's 12%, reached by a sector fall and a company recovery that roughly offset. The September memo's answer of "about half each" still describes it.
  • Level against peers (dashboard sector of 32 peers, my counts).
  • Price-to-earnings percentile against own ten-year history: BT 82; sector median about 34 (27 peers with data); Deutsche Telekom 5, Telefonica 9, Royal KPN 43, Vodafone 47, Orange 82.
  • Price-to-earnings now: BT 10.6 times; sector median about 12.4 times. BT is expensive against itself and still below the sector, which is what a re-rated but leveraged, pension-carrying, low-growth incumbent looks like.
  • Twelve-month return: BT +22.3%; sector median about +14%. Technical momentum: BT is pinned D; of the 32 peers, 21 are D or F and ten are A, B or C.
  • Why the sector share of the risk is larger from here.
  • BT is more sensitive than the sector to the factor that is driving the sector: equity is about half of enterprise value, and the sector digest's own description is that a leveraged defensive de-rates first when yields rise. The gilt yield is about 5.3%.
  • The company-specific news of October has been absorbed (the shares fell on a beat). What is left is delivery (the cash print in May) and two-sided events (the TalkTalk decision, the presentation of net debt).
  • So over the next six months the sector factor is the larger source of change in the multiple, and it points whichever way yields go; the company factor can add to a fall more easily than it can offset one.
  • What this means for predictability. Nothing for the forecasts, which are domestic and contract-backed. For the case, it means the multiple that turns forecasts into returns is not in the company's hands for about half of its movement, and the half that is company-driven already stands at a premium to history.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D5027th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: BT fits the sector's outperformance pattern on its fundamentals (capital expenditure falling on an owned network, contractual pricing, self-driven cost cuts, a now-closed in-market consolidation) and fits the underperformance pattern on its financial structure and price (leverage plus a pension in a rising-yield regime, a multiple near the top of its own range, earnings per share growing slowly). The evidence says the first set of attributes makes the cash case right and the second set decides the share price, so the precedent that best fits is "right on the business, flat on the shares until earnings or the discount rate move".

  • What has paid in this sector (Sector and Industry Digests, 15 October 2026). Cash gains already in management's hands: closed-merger synergies (MasOrange, VodafoneThree), capital expenditure falling on an owned network, owner-forced cost cuts, price rises written into contracts. Cash waiting on a regulator, a court or a partner is marked down. Vodafone +37.9% and NOS +52.2% over twelve months on cash in hand.
  • What has hurt. The group's 24-month-forward multiple fell 12% from May to September 2026 while 2027 estimates held; leveraged defensives de-rate first when yields rise (Wisdom Library, 6 November 2026); "slow growth is punished twice" (multiple and earnings); telecoms read the lowest relative-strength grade on 13 November 2026.
  • Peer precedents.
  • KPN: the closest capital-expenditure-cliff precedent (capex from €1.25bn to below €1bn in 2027); it trades at a premium (15.8x 2026 enterprise value to operating free cash flow against a 12.7x peer average) with 2.4x leverage, 45% margins and 14% return on capital employed; the premium needs broadband losses to stabilise (Bernstein). BT has the cliff without KPN's returns or balance sheet.
  • Deutsche Telekom and Orange: "beat and raise" or sandbagging guiders that compound; BT meets rather than raises.
  • Vodafone: re-rated about a third on portfolio reset and closed consolidation, yet its risk work scores 'false friend' on cash quality; the same debate exists at BT.
  • Orange 2012 and Telia: dividend-cover failures in leveraged incumbents; BT's own 2016 to 2024 history (accounting scandal, Business revenue -21% and EBITDA -45% between 2020 and 2024, dividend suspended in 2020 and rebased to 7.7p from 15.4p) is the negative archetype.
  • Base rates. Of 113 past group cases in telecoms' current technical position, 25% made a new relative high within 26 weeks, median relative return +2% (Sector Digest).
  • Fit. Positive pattern on capex, ownership, pricing form, cost; negative pattern on leverage, pension, valuation percentile (82nd ten-year; 91st five-year) and earnings growth. The Sector Digest itself names BT as the stock that "needs the cash itself" to justify its price.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F2441st of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: about 35% to 45% with earnings unchanged, to about 120p to 140p; about 25% just to the stock's own median multiple. The market has rarely sustained BT above 11x 24-month-forward earnings, so the asymmetry at 216p is to the downside on valuation alone.

  • Today. 215.8p on 24-month-forward consensus earnings of about 19.2p: 11.2x (FactSet 11.34x; the 91st percentile of five years; the 77th of two years; ten-year bands 'mania' 11.60x, 'defendable' 9.90x, 'prudent' median 8.30x, 'conservative' 7.50x, 'crash' 6.00x).
  • Arithmetic (earnings unchanged at 19.2p; our calculation). Two-year median 10.36x: 199p (-8%). Ten-year median 8.30x: 159p (-26%). Five-year 25th percentile 6.98x: 134p (-38%). Ten-year 'crash' 6.00x: 115p (-47%). Five-year low 5.66x: 109p (-50%).
  • Fair trough. About 7x on about 18p to 19p (a bear case in which FY29 earnings stay at Deutsche Bank's 17.8p) gives about 125p to 135p; Deutsche Bank's sum-of-the-parts target is 150p; Morgan Stanley's bear case 100p; the risks report's simultaneous downside 100p at 5x FY27 earnings.
  • Floor arguments. A dividend of about 8.6p is a 6% yield at about 143p and 7% at about 123p; a normalised free-cash-flow yield above 10% at today's price (about £2.0bn on £21.5bn); peers' median current P/E about 11.9x (the memo's count).
  • Amplifier. Equity is about half of enterprise value, so de-ratings in enterprise-value terms double in equity.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C-5815th of 42

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead of BT has dated, credible positive steps in every window, and the next 18 months carry the most important ones: the FY27 cash print, the first revenue growth, and the first capital-return statement under the new distribution headroom. The October 2026 rally already absorbed the de-risking steps (TalkTalk, Ofcom, the provisional CMA finding), so the novelty left is in delivery rather than in news. For the second and later sets: the narrative supports the cash case and can carry the multiple only if the May 2027 results deliver £2.0bn of cash, a revenue-growth guide for FY28 and a first buyback; without those, the staircase becomes a set of known events.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–58

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two earlier sets are congruent in direction and in their main conclusion (an inflection that is mechanical, below EBITDA and already priced), and the second set corrected the first set's one large open question (the pension). They jar in seven specific places. One is an arithmetic error that made the model's forecasts wrong; one is a line of reasoning on depreciation that does not hold; the rest are assumptions that the newer research contradicts. After the corrections, the two sets fit each other better than before: the second set's earnings growth of about 8% a year sat uneasily with the first set's "low-to-mid single digits"; about 5% to 6% does not.

  • Where the sets reinforce each other.
  • Both find that the change from the past is in the capital cycle (capital expenditure falling from £5.2bn to about £3.5bn to £4.0bn), not in revenue or EBITDA.
  • Both give high weight to what management guides and controls (cost, capital expenditure, EBITDA range, the cash floor) and low weight to revenue or commercial improvement.
  • Both treat the price, not the business, as the main risk: the first set's trough of 120p to 140p and the model's guideline multiple of 7.25 times say the same thing.
  • The second set's resolution of the pension (no cash release before FY32; the agreement gave permission to distribute, not cash) is confirmed by the newer research, which gives the same schedule: about £770m a year from FY28 against £780m before, and about £600m in FY34 against £180m. (Its table also shows £770m for FY31, where the note maturing in April 2030 implies the second set's £670m; I keep the second set's figure.)
  • The gap between the Analyst's cross-check and the model, explained year by year. The earnings gap is wholly the revenue-base error. The free-cash-flow gap is the same error plus a difference of definition that is not an error.
FY27 FY28 FY29 FY30 FY31
Net income: the second set's / the model's (£m) 1,810 / 1,460 1,900 / 1,554 2,110 / 1,750 2,310 / 1,940 2,470 / 2,104
Earnings per share: the second set's / the model's (p) 18.3 / 15 19.3 / 16 21.4 / 18 23.3 / 20 25.3 / 22
Free cash flow: the second set's / the model's (£m) 1,945 / 808 2,222 / 1,437 2,709 / 1,845 3,053 / 2,104 3,243 / 2,285
Free-cash-flow gap (£m) 1,137 785 864 949 958
of which the revenue base (£m) 517 351 364 369 374
of which definition (£m) 620 434 500 580 584
  • The revenue base (an input error, corrected). Revenue about £1,000m too low at a 45% to 47% margin removes about £450m to £490m of EBITDA a year, about £350m to £375m of net income and about 3.5p of earnings per share. In FY27 it also inflates the working-capital outflow: the input sets the year-end balance as a percentage of revenue, so lower revenue made the model release £318m of working capital instead of the intended £154m.
  • Definition (not an error; no input changed). The model's "equity free cash flow" deducts the tax and interest charged in the profit and loss account. The second set's figure is the company's normalised free cash flow, which deducts tax and interest paid. Cash tax is about £90m in FY27 and £250m a year in FY28 to FY30, against a charge of about £535m in FY27 rising to about £675m in FY30; cash interest is about £130m below the charge; FY27 also has about £50m of copper proceeds. The second set gave these same amounts (£0.57bn plus copper, £0.45bn, £0.51bn, £0.59bn, £0.59bn). After the revenue correction the model's figure will read about £1.3bn, £1.8bn, £2.2bn, £2.5bn and £2.7bn. The memo should quote normalised free cash flow from the second set (unchanged) when comparing with guidance, and should not read the model's line as a miss against the £2.0bn target.
  • The net debt line is not usable as it stands. The model shows net debt of £19.6bn at March 2027 falling to £16.0bn at March 2031. It carries neither the pension cash (about £0.8bn a year), nor restructuring cash (£0.3bn falling to £0.1bn), nor the £2.4bn of pension notes now inside reported borrowings; against that it deducts more tax and interest than is paid. My path on the company's definition including leases and the notes is about £22.7bn (March 2027), £21.6bn, £20.4bn, £18.6bn and £17.4bn (March 2031), or 2.8, 2.6, 2.4, 2.2 and 2.0 times EBITDA. It is the second set's bridge plus the notes outstanding, valued at a discount rate of about 4.5% that I infer from the stated £2.1bn present value of the first four notes.
  • A note for the model. The Financial Analyst should, first, confirm that the model applies a year's revenue growth to the sum of FactSet's half-years for the prior year (£18,536m for FY26), which the corrected input now assumes; and second, either carry in the net-debt bridge the items the inputs form has no line for (pension cash, restructuring cash and the pension notes issued on 13 November 2026) or mark the net-debt line as excluding them.
  • A basis point for anyone quoting growth. The model's earnings history is on the Viewforth definition (17.5p in FY26), while the forecast is BT's adjusted figure (18.3p in FY26 on the brokers' basis). After the correction the model will show FY27 growth of about +5%; on the company's basis FY27 is flat for the group and +3% for the core.
  • Where the sets, or the sets and the newer evidence, jar. 1. Depreciation (I disagree with the second set; input changed). The second set argued that depreciation must fall £60m to £90m a year because capital expenditure runs £0.4bn to £1bn below it. Three things are wrong with that.
    • The comparison is not like for like. The £4.7bn charge includes about £610m of depreciation on leased assets, which is matched by lease payments, not by capital expenditure. Capital expenditure of £4.35bn in FY27 is above the charge on owned assets (about £4.1bn); it is about £0.1bn below in FY28 and £0.3bn to £0.4bn below from FY29.
    • Depreciation follows capital expenditure with a lag of the asset life. The years now leaving the depreciation base are those before 2020, when capital expenditure was £3.4bn to £4.1bn; the years of £4.6bn to £5.3bn stay in the base into the 2030s. In Openreach, where the build is, network depreciation rose £99m in FY26 to £1,653m, and Openreach's capital expenditure stays at or above its depreciation even after the build slows.
    • The first half of FY27 tests the three views of FY27. Depreciation and amortisation was £2,312m (EBITDA £4,097m less adjusted EBIT £1,785m, FactSet), against £2,307m a year earlier, even though the EE amortisation fell by about £70m in the half. That run-rate supports consensus (£4,694m for the year) and the second set (£4,715m with TalkTalk) and contradicts Morgan Stanley's £4,473m, the only broker path that falls steeply (to £3,973m by FY30).
    • The falls that are dated and real are small: the EE intangibles (£136m in FY27, £44m in FY28), a slowly shrinking lease charge, and whatever copper and telephone-exchange assets become fully depreciated after the switch-off on 31 January 2027, which BT does not disclose. Chorus, the cleanest precedent, saw its charge fall only when the copper in its fibre areas was fully written off, and then by under 10%; Orange had to add a dismantling provision of €1.7bn that raised its charge.
    • My path is therefore consensus plus TalkTalk's own charge. The upside case (a larger copper roll-off after January 2027) is real but unevidenced; the property, plant and equipment note in the FY27 annual report (June 2027) will show it. 2. The pension notes and net debt (the second set's assumption is contradicted). Covered in "Read this first", point 4. It changes no cash flow and no adjusted earnings figure unless the accretion is taken into adjusted finance costs. 3. TalkTalk's accounting (the second set's assumption is probably wrong on timing). Covered in "Read this first", point 4. The second set's remark that its FY27 and FY28 figures are "in line with consensus because TalkTalk dilution offsets a stronger core" compares a consolidated figure with a consensus that excludes TalkTalk; like for like, the core is 2.7% above consensus in FY27 and 1.2% above in FY28. 4. Shareholder returns (the first set's staircase against the second set's model). The first set listed "the first statement of distributions under the new headroom" at the May 2027 results as one of three novel positive steps. The second set has no buyback until FY31, Morgan Stanley has none of substance to FY30, and management ties enhanced returns to a 0.5 times fall in leverage that has not begun (net debt rises in FY27). I side with the second set: May 2027 can bring a dividend rise of 2% to 4% and words about the framework, not a buyback. 5. Openreach line losses in FY28 (I think the second set is too optimistic; no input changed). The second set has 720k in FY27 and 550k in FY28. The first is reasonable: 367k in the first half, 175k in the second quarter. The second assumes that owning TalkTalk removes most of TalkTalk's share of losses. The evidence is weaker than that: management kept its guide of about 800k on 5 November, a month after taking ownership; CityFibre alone is adding 108k to 118k customers a quarter and rising; alternative networks have about 20m premises passed at about 16% take-up, so they can keep taking lines for years without building; TalkTalk's retail base is being targeted (Fibrus offers up to £400 to leave) and BT cannot manage it while it is held separate; and CityFibre says under 5% of its 2026 connections came from TalkTalk customers, which suggests TalkTalk's "more than half of line losses" was partly lines that moved to other providers still on Openreach. A path of about 650k in FY28 is as likely as 550k. The financial effect is small (100k lines is about £19m of EBITDA, about 0.15p of earnings per share), so I leave the EBITDA inputs alone; the effect on the narrative is larger than the effect on the numbers. 6. FY27 cash capital expenditure (a composition risk inside the guided free cash flow). The second set has £4,350m of cash capital expenditure against £4,250m accrued. In the first half, cash (£2,518m) ran £236m above accrued (£2,282m) as creditors from the build were paid; Royal KPN's experience is that cash falls more slowly than booked expenditure; Morgan Stanley has £4,572m. If cash capital expenditure is nearer £4.5bn, the £2.0bn of free cash flow needs about £150m to £200m more from working capital than the second set assumed. The total is guided, so I have not changed the input, but it is why the cash figure is the least predictable guided line (D-04). 7. TNT Sports (the second set's "unverified upside" is clearer now, and smaller for the forecast). BT paid £187m (FY25), £191m (FY26) and £102m (first half of FY27) to settle the minimum revenue guarantee, outside normalised free cash flow. These payments end with the guarantee. They were never in the second set's forward bridge, so their end does not lift its figures; it explains why net debt rose faster than the bridge implied (by £1.04bn in the first half). The new item is a receipt: BT carries £184m of preference shares in the joint venture at the value of cash it expects in the second half of FY27. It is outside guidance and outside our bridge, and it depends on a counterparty (Warner Bros. Discovery, now rated BB after its acquisition) whose incentive is to conserve cash.
  • Smaller points of agreement worth recording. The second set's FY27 pension cash of £780m is about £810m on Morgan Stanley's figure (a £30m payment was brought forward). Restructuring cost per paid leaver rose from about £67k (FY25: £448m for 6,661 leavers) to about £159k (FY26: £336m for 2,119), which puts the second set's restructuring cash path (£300m falling to £100m) at risk of running £50m to £100m a year higher if the labour target is met through paid exits rather than through attrition (voluntary turnover is about 10% a year) and fewer subcontractors.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

D463rd of 5

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: both sides of the main debate are fully articulated. The bulls (Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, Goldman Sachs) tell the story of capital expenditure falling, cash doubling and wholesale risk receding; the bears (Deutsche Bank, UBS, Barclays) tell the story of competition, cash quality, the pension and valuation. The price reflects the bulls' operating story: the shares fell on a results beat. What neither side articulates well is the set of mechanics below EBITDA, and on those our assessment cuts both ways. In the next six months the unarticulated items lean negative for headlines (net debt including the notes, TalkTalk in the estimates, depreciation that does not fall). From FY28 they lean positive (reported net debt falling by more than £1bn a year, reported earnings growing about 15% a year, free cash flow above estimates). There is no direct evidence of investor commentary in the files.

  • Each dynamic: how well known, and where our assessment differs.

  • What dominates the sector's attention instead. Bond yields (the spread between a 6% sector dividend yield and gilts has narrowed), switching agents built on artificial intelligence (Barclays, Morgan Stanley) and satellite. These act on the multiple of every telecom and crowd out company detail; Morgan Stanley itself says the market looks at the headline price-to-earnings ratio and passes over the cash mechanics. That is why the items in the table can still move the shares when they appear in a result.
  • The shape of the sell-side. Split and entrenched: four Buy (targets 219p to 340p) against six Sell or Underweight (143p to 180p). The bulls' targets rest on the cash and, in Morgan Stanley's case, on depreciation falling; the bears' on competition and the sum of the parts. BT is Bernstein's "best idea" and Morgan Stanley's "core Overweight", so among sector specialists the long case is a well-owned idea, not a contrarian one. The research could not confirm what share of covering brokers it captured; most dynamic-specific analysis comes from two houses (Morgan Stanley and Deutsche Bank), so "no one discusses it" may in places mean "neither of those two does".
  • Investor commentary: what can and cannot be said. No fund letters, interviews or social-media commentary were retrieved. The indirect evidence:
  • Price behaviour. +18% in the month before results on the de-risking news, then -7% on a beat with guidance reconfirmed: holders treat the de-risking as known and wait for delivery.
  • Positioning (mid-2026 data cited in the first set). Hedge-fund net exposure down from 6.8% to 4.6% and the long-short ratio from 2.7 to 1.7 times: less crowded than a year ago. Options then priced a 4.4% chance of the shares below 100p.
  • The register. About 41% in three strategic hands; a potential overhang from Bharti or Deutsche Telekom, and thin free float in a squeeze.
  • Sector stance. Telecoms at the bottom grade of relative strength; the market has chosen other defensives.
  • My inference, to be tested by the research request: generalist investors hold the simple version (a leveraged UK yield stock whose capital expenditure is falling), and the items in the table are known, if at all, to specialists.
  • Secondarily, 18 months to three years out.
  • Known and priced: about £3bn of free cash flow by FY30; lower capital expenditure; copper pricing freedom from April 2029.
  • Known but possibly misdated: the fall in pension cash, which comes in FY32 (about £490m a year lower), not FY28; buybacks, which follow BBB+ metrics around FY30.
  • Little articulated and favourable: reported net debt falling by about £1.1bn to £1.7bn a year from FY28 once the notes are inside it; free cash flow in FY29 and FY30 above consensus; reported earnings converging on adjusted.
  • Little articulated and unfavourable: depreciation staying near £4.6bn through FY30, so that earnings per share grow about 5% to 6% a year and not the 11% a year of the most bullish forecast (Morgan Stanley: 18.8p in FY26 to 28.5p in FY30); the regulatory reaction that higher Openreach returns invite before the 2031 review (Bernstein sees returns above 15% by about 2031).

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 16 November 2026, 15:11 to 15:28 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
BT Group plc (BT.A-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 16 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years). Money in pounds sterling; per-share figures in pence (the FactSet block quotes pounds, so 0.184 GBP is 18.4p). Fiscal year ends 31 March; FY27 is the year to March 2027.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read this first: what the reader needs before the answers

1. The latest Earnings Review is confirmed as covering the half to 30 September 2026. It reports H1 FY27 results announced on 5 November 2026 (adjusted revenue £8,750m, adjusted EBITDA £4,097m, net debt £21,006m at 30 September 2026). These match the FactSet block's H1-FY2027 column (revenue 8,750; EBITDA 4,097), so it can be relied on as the latest period.

2. Four things changed between the 27 September 2026 memo and today, and together they change the shape of the case. The memo parked BT at 196.55p on the view that the cash inflection was real but did not reach earnings per share and that three external risks (Ofcom, a scaled wholesale challenger, TalkTalk) could break it. Since then:

  • TalkTalk is now BT's. BT bought TalkTalk and its wholesale unit PlatformX out of administration on 5 October 2026 (pre-pack, described as debt-free; about £400m total FY27 cash impact including consideration, working capital and operating losses; excluded from guidance; a Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) public interest review is open). TalkTalk hosted about 2.7m lines, about 95% on Openreach, worth about £1bn a year of Openreach revenue, and Morgan Stanley says it had contributed more than half of Openreach's line losses. The largest single wholesale counterparty risk has been internalised, at the price of a low-revenue-per-user, loss-making retail base, an integration and a regulatory review.
  • The pension triennial was agreed early. The June 2026 actuarial deficit fell to £2.7bn from £3.7bn (funding level 92.3% from 91.0%). BT is issuing £2.1bn of zero-coupon bonds to the scheme, maturing 2033, which "replace scheduled cash contributions from FY28 to FY31"; management "expects no further cash contributions to be required to reach full funding by 2030"; Deutsche Bank notes total future payments are £282m higher than previously expected; and the agreement allows shareholder distributions of up to £1.5bn in FY27, rising to up to £2.0bn by FY30. The cash mechanics are not unambiguous in the files (see C-19 and C-52); they are the most important below-EBITDA modelling question in the case.
  • Two of the three external threats moved in BT's favour. Ofcom's final decision (28 September 2026) withdrew the one blocked Openreach offer but approved the Frontbook ARPU Share offer (capped at £19.32 a month) and the £50 geographic bounty, and imposed no margin-squeeze constraint; the CMA provisionally found (October 2026) that nexfibre's £2bn purchase of Netomnia would substantially lessen wholesale competition, and nexfibre declined to offer remedies (statutory deadline 15 December 2026); Virgin Media O2 (VMO2) has halted its fibre upgrade beyond existing commitments and is discussing renting Openreach in rural areas.
  • The share price re-rated ahead of the evidence. The shares ran to 231.4p before results (+18% in the month to 4 November, Bernstein) and have since drifted to 215.8p (13 November). The 24-month-forward price-to-earnings ratio is 11.34x, the 91st percentile of five years, just below the ten-year 'mania' band of 11.60x; consensus FY28 earnings per share fell 2% in the last month and 3% in three months; the dashboard pins technical momentum D, sell-side earnings momentum F and valuation F.

3. The single most important judgement for the model. The fundamental risks to the cash plan fell materially in October and November 2026, but the earnings line did not move and the market has already paid for the de-risking. For the operating model this means: lower line-loss and lower pension-cash assumptions are now defensible; the FY27 free-cash-flow target leans heavily on the second half; TalkTalk consolidation must be modelled explicitly because guidance and probably most of consensus exclude it; and earnings per share still grow only low-to-mid single digits because depreciation (about £4.6bn to £4.7bn a year) and interest (about £1.0bn) absorb most of the EBITDA.

4. Base-year anchors (company or FactSet, bases named).

  • FY26 (continuing and discontinued, brokers' basis): revenue £19,646m; adjusted EBITDA £8,230m; adjusted earnings per share 18.3p (reported 11.0p); normalised free cash flow £1,508m; cash capital expenditure £5,169m (accrued £5,127m); dividend 8.32p; net debt £19,966m including £4,184m of leases.
  • H1 FY27 (continuing, International discontinued): revenue £8,750m (+1%); service revenue £7,685m (-1%, entirely legacy voice); EBITDA £4,097m (+1%); Openreach EBITDA £2,238m (+4%); Business EBITDA -7%; Consumer EBITDA -1% (£1,255m); normalised free cash flow £236m (H1 FY26 £569m); accrued capital expenditure £2,282m (-5%); cash capital expenditure £2,518m (-1%); net debt £21,006m; interim dividend 2.5p.
  • FY27 guidance (continuing, reconfirmed 5 November 2026, excluding TalkTalk): revenue £17.1bn to £17.6bn; UK service revenue £15.1bn to £15.4bn; adjusted EBITDA £8.1bn to £8.2bn; accrued capital expenditure excluding spectrum £4.2bn to £4.3bn; normalised free cash flow about £2.0bn; Openreach line losses about 800k; adjusted earnings per share low-single-digit growth. Mid-term: revenue growth from FY28; EBITDA ahead of UK service revenue; capital expenditure more than £1bn below FY26; normalised free cash flow about £3.0bn by FY30; gross annualised savings £3.5bn by FY30 on a continuing basis (previously £3.7bn including International), cost to achieve £1.2bn; total labour resource 65,000 to 70,000 by FY30 excluding International (previously 75,000 to 80,000).
  • FactSet consensus (median, 16 November 2026): revenue £17,554m, £17,564m, £17,718m for FY27 to FY29; EBITDA £8,164m, £8,221m, £8,260m; depreciation and amortisation £4,694m, £4,633m, £4,623m; EBIT £3,406m, £3,502m, £3,631m; earnings per share 18.4p, 19.5p, 20.0p (+0.4%, +6.1%, +2.5%); dividend 8.6p, 8.9p, 9.2p; capital expenditure £4,552m, £4,038m, £3,766m; FactSet free cash flow £1,972m, £2,189m, £2,503m. FY30 rests on one broker.

5. Data cautions the reader must carry.

  • Consensus mixes bases. Three visible brokers forecast FY27 revenue of about £19.3bn (Oddo BHF and a restricted house), which still includes International, while the median is £17.6bn; Barclays (13.1p) and Oddo BHF (11.9p) forecast FY27 earnings per share far below the median, probably on a reported or different basis. The spread of FY29 earnings per share (12.6p to 25.5p) is partly definitional. Use the median and say so.
  • FactSet's free-cash-flow line is not the company's normalised free cash flow; the FY26 FactSet figure was £137m against the company's £1,508m.
  • The latest Earnings Review dates the employer National Insurance increase "from April 2027" and sizes it at £80m; the cited sources are Deutsche Bank notes of October 2024 and March 2025, and the earlier Earnings Review dates the same change from April 2025 at about £100m a year, about 70% in operating cost. We read it as already in the cost base since April 2025, not a new FY28 headwind. The Bank of England rise to 4.00% on 5 November 2026 is reported by Morgan Stanley; the Geography Digest of 13 November says its outcome was not read by any dated source.
  • Two sets of retail price-rise figures exist (£3 a month broadband and £1.50 mobile in the Earnings Review; £4.00 and £2.50 in the business foundations report); they are not merged here.
  • The cost target change (£3.7bn to £3.5bn) appears to be a restatement for the International perimeter, not a cut; the labour target change (75,000 to 80,000, to 65,000 to 70,000) appears to be on the same excluding-International basis and therefore a genuine raise in ambition, but the files do not state the old basis unambiguously.

Scores: 1 to 100, where a higher number is more positive for an investment case in BT; 50 is neither.

Inside the group External change forces (Layer 1)

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, against the last 12 to 18 months.
