Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, against the last 12 to 18 months.

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Overall steer to the second set

BT is a regulated, ex-growth, leveraged UK infrastructure incumbent whose cash flow is about to inflect for mechanical reasons, whose tail risks fell sharply in October and November 2026, and whose earnings per share will grow only low-to-mid single digits. The model should be built in two layers: an operating layer (Openreach lines and ARPU, Consumer and Business revenue and EBITDA, the end of the voice drag, the cost programme net of wage inflation and own-work decapitalisation, capital expenditure falling to about £3.6bn to £3.8bn by FY29 to FY30), and a cash layer that bridges normalised free cash flow to the shareholder's cash (pension under two readings, restructuring, TalkTalk, cash tax from FY28, working-capital programmes, then dividends and buybacks within the distribution headroom). The predictability of the operating layer over 18 months is high; the predictability of the cash layer is moderate and timing-sensitive (H2 FY27), and the TalkTalk consolidation and the pension mechanics are the two inputs most likely to move the outcome and least covered in consensus.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: BT Group plc (BT.A-GB), the UK's integrated fixed and mobile telecoms incumbent, reporting Openreach (the regulated national wholesale fixed-access network, passing about 24.1m premises with full fibre at 30 September 2026), Consumer (BT, EE and Plusnet broadband, mobile and TV, plus TalkTalk and its wholesale unit PlatformX, acquired out of administration on 5 October 2026) and Business (UK enterprise, public-sector and small-business connectivity and IT), with its International enterprise unit being contributed to a 50/50 joint venture with Verizon (completion expected in the second half of 2027).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Royal KPN (Dutch fibre incumbent reaching its capital-expenditure cliff in 2027 with a €100m net opex programme; the closest post-build precedent); Chorus (New Zealand wholesale fibre network after its build ended; precedent for opex and depreciation after a national fibre build); Deutsche Telekom (German fibre incumbent with an €800m non-US cost target and union wage inflation); Orange (French copper decommissioning, a completed €600m net opex programme and the MasOrange integration); Vodafone and VodafoneThree (UK mobile rival with £700m to £1bn of merger synergies and a €1bn net European opex target); Telia and Tele2 (cost programmes run by BT's chief executive at prior employers, and Tele2's owner-led programme); Virgin Media O2 (UK cable and mobile rival with a £0.8bn cost reset announced in 2026); Swisscom with Fastweb and Vodafone Italia (in-market synergy delivery); Proximus (Belgian incumbent cutting costs under fibre overbuild); CityFibre and TalkTalk (private UK alt-net and retailer; TalkTalk now owned by BT). Inputs: What share of BT's group and Openreach operating cost base is fixed (network maintenance, staff, energy, IT, property, business rates of about £500m a year, leases) and what share varies with lines or revenue, and how has group and divisional EBITDA moved with revenue in FY20 to H1 FY27? Of the gross annualised savings BT has reported (£1.6bn phase one completed in 2020; £1.7bn cumulative under the current programme at H1 FY27, BT, 5 November 2026), how much reached reported EBITDA after wage inflation, the April 2025 employer National Insurance increase, legacy voice loss and reinvestment (for example the Business marketing reinvestment noted in Q1 FY27), and how does that compare with the drop-through of comparable programmes at the named peers? What is the composition of the current target of £3.5bn of gross annualised savings by FY30 on a continuing basis with a £1.2bn cost to achieve (BT, 5 November 2026), restated from £3.7bn and £1.4bn including International (May 2026), by labour, network, IT, property and commercial lines, and how is it phased? Is the total labour resource target of 65,000 to 70,000 by FY30 excluding International (BT, 5 November 2026) on the same basis as the earlier 75,000 to 80,000 target, what is the restructuring cost per leaver, what do the Communication Workers Union and Prospect agreements allow, and which European incumbents have cut headcount by a third or more over six years, with what effect on service quality and churn? How much labour cost does BT capitalise into fibre capital expenditure (own-work capitalised has been reported as up more than £600m since the build accelerated, and Rothschild & Co Redburn has estimated that a £1bn capital-expenditure reduction triggers about £600m of decapitalisation), and what happened to operating cost at Chorus, KPN, Orange and Deutsche Telekom when their fibre builds slowed? What savings and dual-running costs attach to the PSTN switch-off on 31 January 2027, copper retirement, the reduction from about 5,500 exchanges toward about 1,000, and fibre fault rates reported at about one third of copper's? How does BT's energy cost (about £500m a year; 88% hedged for FY27 and 52% for FY28, BT annual report 2026) move as hedges roll off? What cost overlap, integration cost, operating losses and synergies attach to the TalkTalk acquisition (a total FY27 cash impact of about £400m, BT, 5 November 2026), and what did comparable in-market retail consolidations deliver against plan? Which costs in the Business division (EBITDA down 5% in FY26 and 7% in H1 FY27) can be removed as legacy voice runs off? Focus: How much of BT's operating cost base can be removed between FY27 and FY30 net of wage inflation, capitalised-labour reversal, reinvestment and TalkTalk, and how much of each pound of service revenue gained or lost reaches EBITDA in Openreach, Consumer and Business, so that operating leverage can be modelled in both directions.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Deutsche Telekom (first-ever German back-book broadband rise of €2 a month in April 2026 on 40% to 50% of the base); Royal KPN (contractual CPI indexation in a three-player Dutch market); Orange (French price rises limited to targeted front-book and low-end tariffs); Vodafone and VodafoneThree (UK mobile pricing under Ofcom's 2025 ban on inflation-linked mid-contract rises and CMA tariff caps); Virgin Media O2 and Sky (UK fixed and mobile rivals with fixed money-amount rises and multi-network sourcing); Chorus (regulated wholesale fibre pricing after a national build); Proximus and Telenet (fixed operators facing fibre and cable competition); CityFibre, Community Fibre and YouFibre (private UK alt-nets setting front-book and wholesale price benchmarks). Operating conditions: UK consumer-price inflation re-accelerating (3.5% in September 2026) while retail contracts carry fixed pounds-and-pence annual rises; Ofcom's ban on inflation-linked mid-contract rises (from January 2025); Ofcom's Telecoms Access Review 2026-31 with the 80Mbps anchor product capped at £17.17 a month and the 'fair bet' on fibre; Ofcom's blocking of Openreach's 'Incremental New to Openreach' discount (final decision 28 September 2026) alongside approval of the Frontbook ARPU Share offer (£19.32 cap) and a £50 geographic bounty; low-cost entrants and substitutes (alt-net gigabit offers, mobile virtual network operators such as Monzo, Revolut, AO Mobile and Lebara, Starlink at £32 a month, VodafoneThree 5G fixed wireless); in-market consolidation (VodafoneThree in mobile; BT's purchase of TalkTalk in fixed retail); copper wholesale price deregulation from 1 April 2029 in exchanges with 90% ultrafast coverage; a squeezed UK consumer with rising energy bills. Inputs: What annual retail price rises has BT applied by brand and product since moving to pounds-and-pence pricing (sources report £3 a month for broadband and £1.50 for mobile, and also £4.00 and £2.50; which applies to which brand and year?), and why did Consumer broadband ARPU fall 3% to £40.8 in H1 FY27 while postpaid mobile ARPU rose 3% to £19.8 (BT, 5 November 2026): decompose into the price rise, legacy voice roll-off, front-book discounting (for example Plusnet full fibre at £22.99), IFRS 15 timing and mix. What happened to volumes and churn after the 2026 introduction of new connection or activation charges (Consumer broadband net additions of -7k in H1 FY27), and after each annual rise (Morgan Stanley's survey found rises stuck with fewer departures than expected)? Decompose Openreach broadband ARPU growth of 7% to £17.8 into CPI indexation (3.6% for FY27), fibre speed-tier mix and Equinox 2 terms, and set out what the Frontbook ARPU Share cap, the option to raise FTTP rentals by up to £1 a month from October 2026 under Equinox 2, planned copper price increases and the 2029 copper deregulation allow. How large is the wholesale price gap to alt-nets (CityFibre reported up to 40% cheaper than Openreach FTTP; alt-nets offering 30% to 40% discounts to Equinox 2) and how has it moved? How will TalkTalk's lower-ARPU base be priced under BT ownership, and what constraints might the CMA's public interest review impose? What share of Business contracts carries indexation (15% of managed-services revenue reported)? How did pricing behave at the named peers when inflation re-accelerated with fixed-amount clauses, when regulators blocked discounts, and when low-cost entrants arrived, with dated figures? Focus: Whether BT can raise prices in real terms at retail and at wholesale between FY27 and FY30 given fixed money-amount retail rises, Ofcom's caps and blocks, and front-book competition, and how much volume and ARPU it gives back when it does.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: BT's outcome over four years depends on whether several interacting dynamics now under way (TalkTalk's internalisation, alt-net capital scarcity, challenger consolidation, regulatory reaction, the capital-expenditure fall and capital returns) reinforce or offset each other. Peers: Royal KPN (capital-expenditure cliff feeding distributions; wholesale customer migration by Odido); Chorus (wholesale fibre after the build, competing with fixed wireless); Deutsche Telekom (German fibre with alt-net overbuild and back-book pricing); Vodafone Germany (a cable network losing share to fibre, a negative loop); Virgin Media O2 (a cable operator halting its fibre build and turning to wholesale); Orange (French co-financing and copper switch-off); CityFibre, nexfibre and Netomnia (private UK alt-nets consolidating under CMA review); Telia and Tele2 (cost savings returned to shareholders). Inputs: Openreach's build (24.1m premises at 30 September 2026; about £300 per premise against £500 to £1,800 reported for alt-nets) and alt-net capital scarcity (construction velocity reported down at least 40% year on year; toob's equity written to zero; G.Network in administration): does each premise built reduce the overbuild that drives line losses, and how have line losses responded (203k in Q4 FY26, 192k in Q1 FY27, 175k in Q2 FY27, BT)? With TalkTalk owned by BT since 5 October 2026 (Morgan Stanley reported TalkTalk had contributed more than half of Openreach line losses), how much does deliberate migration off Openreach stop, how much retail churn to alt-net ISPs continues (competitors such as Fibrus offering termination-fee credits), and do the about 1.0m third-party ISP lines on PlatformX move to a neutral wholesaler? Does challenger consolidation (the CMA's provisional finding against nexfibre-Netomnia, decision due by 15 December 2026; reports of CityFibre as the counterfactual buyer of Netomnia) create a stronger balancing competitor? Does a higher BT retail share (reported as rising from about 31% to about 36%) trigger regulatory remedies that balance the gain? Does rising fibre take-up (41% at H1 FY27) lower fault rates and labour cost in a reinforcing loop, and where does it stop? Does the capital-expenditure fall feed deleveraging toward BBB+ metrics, distributions within the headroom set by the November 2026 pension agreement (up to £1.5bn in FY27 and £2.0bn by FY30) and a re-rating, and what stopped similar loops at peers? Does Virgin Media O2's retreat from building (and reported talks to wholesale Openreach in rural areas) add volume to Openreach? Do annual retail price rises push customers toward mobile virtual network operators, fixed wireless and cheaper tiers, balancing ARPU? Focus: Which of these loops is strongest and fastest over the next 18 months to four years, in particular whether Openreach's wholesale line losses are entering a self-reinforcing decline now that TalkTalk is inside BT and challengers are capital-starved, or whether consolidation among challengers and regulatory reaction will balance it, and what evidence would show each loop strengthening, stalling or reversing.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: BT's earnings per share and shareholder cash depend on below-EBITDA lines and transactions the company does not guide (depreciation, interest, tax, the new pension bonds, TalkTalk, the Verizon joint venture), and the published broker forecasts are widely dispersed partly for definitional reasons. Peers: Royal KPN (depreciation and earnings after a capital-expenditure cliff; Glaspoort consolidation); Orange (MasOrange consolidation and a 10% a year adjusted earnings per share target for 2025 to 2028); Vodafone (adjusted earnings per share and free-cash-flow bridges, minority leakage, hybrid bonds); Deutsche Telekom (minority and stake-creep effects on earnings per share); Telefonica (interest cost and refinancing); Chorus (depreciation after a national fibre build); Virgin Media O2 (financing costs at a leveraged UK rival); Telecom Plus (TalkTalk customer acquisitions). Inputs: Depreciation and amortisation as capital expenditure falls from its £5.1bn FY26 peak (FactSet consensus about £4.7bn in FY27 falling only slightly to FY30, 16 November 2026): fibre and copper asset lives, accelerated copper depreciation and the effect of the copper switch-off. Net finance costs: the maturity schedule (£372m in FY27, £1,013m in FY28, £1,296m in FY29, BT annual report 2026), coupons on maturing bonds against current BBB sterling and euro refinancing yields, the fixed-rate share, lease interest and IAS 19 pension interest. Tax: the adjusted effective rate (22.1% in FY26 with patent box) against reported (25.0%), the durability of the patent box benefit, and the resumption of UK cash tax from FY28 as full expensing tapers (Morgan Stanley estimated about £90m in FY27 rising to about £250m a year in FY28 to FY30). The pension agreement announced on 5 November 2026: how the £2.1bn of zero-coupon bonds maturing in 2033 replace the scheduled FY28 to FY31 contributions, what cash BT pays in each year FY27 to FY34 under the new arrangement against the 2023 schedule (£780m a year to FY30, £670m in FY31, £180m a year from FY32), how the bonds are accounted for and treated by rating agencies, what Deutsche Bank's estimate of £282m higher future payments comprises, and how the distribution covenant (up to £1.5bn in FY27, up to £2.0bn by FY30) works. TalkTalk: revenue, EBITDA, operating losses, the intra-group elimination of its Openreach charges, the about £400m FY27 cash impact (consideration, working capital, losses and any debt), and whether brokers' forecasts include it. The Verizon joint venture: equity-accounted earnings, the $625m equalisation payment and its use. The TNT Sports joint venture (a £218m share of losses in FY26). Specific items and restructuring cash (expected to fall from about £400m to about £100m a year by FY30) and how each broker treats them. Share count: buyback capacity within the distribution headroom and employee-plan dilution. The bridge from normalised free cash flow (£1,508m in FY26; about £2.0bn guided for FY27; about £3.0bn by FY30) to statutory free cash flow and to cash after pension and restructuring, including factoring and supply-chain finance (£476m contribution in FY26), copper forward sales and spectrum. The basis behind the dispersion of published FY27 to FY29 earnings per share forecasts (FactSet shows FY29 from about 12.6p to about 25.5p on 16 November 2026), including brokers still modelling International. Focus: Which earnings-per-share definition the market prices for BT and how each below-EBITDA input, the pension bonds and the TalkTalk consolidation move between FY27 and FY30, so that a reliable bridge from EBITDA to earnings per share and from normalised free cash flow to shareholder cash per share can be built.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 17 November 2026, 20:26 to 20:44 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
BT Group plc (BT.A-GB): second set of questions, the operating and financial model (18 months to four years)

Written 17 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Money in pounds sterling; per-share figures in pence in the text (the forecast-inputs form and the FactSet block quote pounds, so 0.183 GBP is 18.3p). Fiscal year ends 31 March; FY27 is the year to March 2027. "Core" means the continuing group excluding TalkTalk; "group" includes TalkTalk from 5 October 2026.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read this first: what the reader needs before the answers

1. The base case in one paragraph. BT is a flat-revenue, slowly-rising-EBITDA business whose earnings and cash inflect below EBITDA, not at it. Our base case has core EBITDA growing about 1% to 1.3% a year (from £8.19bn in FY27 to £8.57bn in FY31), TalkTalk diluting FY27 and FY28 and turning modestly positive from FY29, depreciation falling about £60m to £90m a year as capital expenditure runs about £1bn a year below it, net finance cost flat at about £1.1bn, and the adjusted tax rate drifting from 22.8% to 23.5%. Adjusted earnings per share: 18.3p (FY27), 19.3p, 21.4p, 23.3p, 25.3p (FY31), against consensus of 18.4p, 19.5p, 20.0p and 22.4p (FY30, one broker). Normalised free cash flow (company definition): £1.95bn, £2.22bn, £2.71bn, £3.05bn, £3.24bn. Shareholder cash after pension, restructuring, TalkTalk, spectrum and dividends: about -£0.35bn (FY27), +£0.64bn, +£0.70bn, +£1.16bn, and +£1.47bn before a first buyback in FY31. Net debt including leases peaks at about £20.3bn in March 2027 and falls to about £17.8bn by March 2030 (2.1x EBITDA), which is roughly where BBB+ metrics sit.

2. Three places where we differ from the first set, and why it matters.

  • The pension question is resolved, and against the optimistic reading. The first set left open whether the £2.1bn of zero-coupon bonds release several hundred million pounds a year of cash from FY28. The AlphaSense EPS-bridge report (5 November 2026 company presentation and press release) gives the schedule: the four replacement bonds mature at £590m in each of FY28, FY29 and FY30 and £490m in FY31, alongside the unchanged £180m a year of asset-backed funding, so cash out is £770m a year in FY28 to FY30 and £670m in FY31, against £780m and £670m under the 2023 plan; a fifth bond (par £420m, maturing September 2033, present value £282m) adds about £420m in FY34. Deutsche Bank's "£4.0bn against £3.5bn" is exactly this FY28 to FY34 window (£3.94bn against £3.55bn). There is no cash release in the forecast horizon. What the deal released is permission: distribution headroom of up to £1.5bn in FY27 rising to £2.0bn by FY30 (from a £1.0bn limit in FY26), and the removal of contribution triggers on non-core disposals. The first set's FY28 scenario of shareholder cash exceeding the dividend by more than £1bn should be discarded; our FY28 surplus is about £0.64bn, and part of that is the one-off Verizon payment.
  • Depreciation will fall faster than consensus from FY29. Consensus holds depreciation and amortisation almost flat (£4,694m in FY27 to £4,592m in FY30). With cash capital expenditure about £0.4bn below depreciation in FY27 and about £1bn below it from FY29, the depreciable base shrinks every year; we model £4.72bn falling to £4.40bn by FY31. Together with slightly faster EBITDA growth, this is why our FY29 to FY31 earnings per share sit above consensus; by FY30 the depreciation gap (about £100m) is as large as the EBITDA gap.
  • TalkTalk should be in the model, and it is dilutive before it is accretive. Consensus and guidance exclude it. We consolidate it from 5 October 2026: about £0.45bn a year of net revenue after eliminating its Openreach charges, EBITDA of about -£50m in FY27 and -£40m in FY28, turning to +£40m, +£100m and +£130m in FY29 to FY31 if the CMA review ends in behavioural undertakings around mid-2027. It costs about 0.6p of earnings per share in FY27 and 0.7p in FY28 and adds about 0.6p by FY31.

3. Basis cautions for anyone using the numbers.

  • FY26 base: FactSet's FY26 revenue of £19,646m includes International; the continuing FY26 base is about £17,536m (H1 £8,696m restated; H2 about £8,840m), continuing EBITDA about £8,105m. FactSet's H2-FY2026 half-year figures (£9,840m revenue; £4,104m EBITDA) include International, while its H1-FY2026 figures are restated. The forecast-inputs form states growth against FactSet's own bases so that the model's arithmetic lands on our absolute figures; Part 4 of the form gives the like-for-like rates.
  • Earnings per share are BT's adjusted basic definition (the brokers' basis), which excludes specific items (restructuring, IAS 19 pension interest of about £200m a year, TalkTalk deal costs) but does not exclude the amortisation of acquired intangibles.
  • Our free cash flow is the company's normalised measure: after capital expenditure, lease payments, interest paid and tax paid, before pension deficit payments, specific items and spectrum. It uses cash tax (about £90m in FY27 and £250m a year in FY28 to FY30) and cash interest (about £130m below the profit and loss charge), so a model that deducts the profit and loss tax and interest will show free cash flow about £0.3bn to £0.5bn a year lower than ours from FY28.
  • The FY27 pension figure in the new schedule (£210m in the presentation's table against £780m in the 2023 plan) is read here as the payments after the June 2026 valuation date; net debt rose in H1 FY27 partly on pension payments, so we keep about £780m for FY27. If the true FY27 figure is lower, FY27 shareholder cash is better by up to about £0.5bn.

Scores: 1 to 100; a higher number means the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is more positive for an investment case; 50 is neither.

Openreach revenue includes its internal sales to Consumer and Business (about 3.5bn a year) and, from October 2026, to TalkTalk (about 0.45bn a year), all eliminated on consolidation, which is why eliminations step up in FY2028. Consensus segment EBITDA (mean, FY2027 to FY2029): Openreach 4,340, 4,411, 4,486; Consumer 2,581, 2,641, 2,701; Business 1,229, 1,218, 1,224. We are above consensus on Openreach (falling line losses, indexation) and below it on Consumer and Business (front-book leakage, legacy voice, low indexation in Business).

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Openreach: FTTP footprint 25.0m premises (March 2027), about 29m by FY2031; take-up 41% (H1 FY2027) rising to about 44%, 49%, 53%, 57%, 60% by year end; broadband line losses about 720k (FY2027, against the about 800k guide; H1 367k), 550k, 480k, 450k, 430k, because TalkTalk's deliberate migration off Openreach stops under BT ownership and alt-net build has slowed; broadband ARPU +6%, +4.5%, +4%, +3.5%, +3% (CPI indexation off the prior September, 3.5% for FY2028, plus a shrinking fibre-mix effect); EBITDA +3% a year from lower fault and repair cost (fibre faults about one third of copper's).
  • Consumer: broadband ARPU -2% (FY2027), -1%, then flat, as fixed money-amount rises (4.00 a month broadband, 2.50 mobile) are offset by front-book discounting, tier-down and voice roll-off; postpaid mobile ARPU +2% to +3%; EBITDA flat at about 2.5bn.
  • Business: legacy voice runs off (a two-point drag on service revenue), only about 15% of managed services indexed; EBITDA -6% (FY2027) easing to -0.5% a year by FY2031.
  • Group cost: gross annualised savings reach 3.5bn by FY2030 (1.7bn by H1 FY2027); total labour resource 90,000 (H1 FY2027) to about 68,000 by FY2030; only about a fifth of each year's gross saving reaches EBITDA, because wage inflation, energy hedge roll-off (88% hedged FY2027, 52% FY2028) and decapitalisation (labour capitalised into the build returning to operating cost as capital expenditure falls; about 100 to 150 a year) absorb the rest. Core EBITDA +1.0% (FY2028), +1.3%, +1.3%, +1.0%.
  • UK service revenue (core): about 15.27bn (FY2027, -1.1%), then +0.6%, +0.9%, +0.9%, +0.8% as the legacy-voice drag (150 to 200 a year) halves in FY2028 after the PSTN switch-off on 31-Jan-27 and ends in FY2029.
  • TalkTalk and PlatformX: about 1.5m retail and 1.0m wholesale lines; external revenue about 1.0bn a year falling about 4% a year; held separate under the CMA's pre-emptive action order, so no synergy until the public interest review ends (assumed mid-2027, with behavioural undertakings); EBITDA -50, -40, +40, +100, +130 on cost synergy only.
  • Depreciation: falls about 60 to 90 a year from FY2028 because cash capital expenditure (4.35bn falling to 3.5bn) runs about 0.4bn to 0.9bn below it; right-of-use depreciation and shorter-lived electronics and IT slow the fall.
  • Interest: about 2.7bn of bond maturities in FY2027 to FY2029 refinance about 1.5 to 2 points higher (average cost of debt 5.2%; 90% fixed), offset from FY2029 by about 0.6bn a year of net debt reduction.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Perimeter. International is a discontinued operation from June 2026 and goes into the 50/50 Verizon joint venture in the second half of calendar 2027; company guidance and the consensus median are on the continuing basis. FactSet's FY2026 history (revenue 19,646; EBITDA 8,195 operating, 8,230 brokers' basis) and its H2-FY2026 half (9,840; 4,104) include International; the continuing FY2026 base is about 17,536 revenue and 8,105 EBITDA. Three visible brokers (revenue about 19.3bn for FY2027) still include International, which is why the consensus mean (17,945) is above the median (17,554).
  • TalkTalk. Consolidated in our inputs from 5-Oct-26 (net of about 0.45bn a year of eliminated Openreach charges once annualised); excluded by guidance, by the consensus median and by Morgan Stanley, which keeps it separate. To compare our figures with consensus, remove TalkTalk: revenue -250 (FY2027) and about -450 a year after; EBITDA +50 (FY2027), +40 (FY2028), -40, -100, -130; EPS +0.6p (FY2027), +0.7p (FY2028), about nil (FY2029), -0.4p (FY2030), -0.6p (FY2031).
  • Earnings per share. Company adjusted basic EPS (the brokers' basis) excludes specific items: restructuring (charges 336 in FY2026, about 250 in FY2027, falling to about 100 by FY2030), IAS 19 net pension interest (191 in FY2026; 100 in H1 FY2027), TalkTalk transaction and integration costs, and impairments (218 on TNT Sports in FY2026). Reported basic EPS was 11.0p against 18.3p adjusted in FY2026; on our path reported is about 13.5p in FY2027 and about 22p in FY2030. Barclays (13.1p) and Oddo BHF (11.9p) appear to forecast on a reported or different basis; the median is the like-for-like reference.
  • EBITDA. Company adjusted EBITDA (before specific items) matches the brokers' basis (FY2026 8,230); FactSet's operating EBITDA (8,195) differs slightly.
  • Free cash flow. Company normalised free cash flow is after capital expenditure, lease payments, interest paid and tax paid, before pension deficit payments, specific items (including restructuring cash) and spectrum, and includes copper forward-sale proceeds and working-capital financing movements (FY2026: 1,508). FactSet's free cash flow line is operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure after pension and specific items (FY2026: 137) and is not comparable; its consensus (1,972, 2,189, 2,503) is closer to the normalised measure but on mixed definitions.
  • Capital expenditure. Guidance is accrued, excluding spectrum; FactSet consensus (4,552, 4,038, 3,766, 3,606) mixes cash and accrued definitions and some brokers include International.
  • Net debt. The company's net debt (21,006 at September 2026) includes about 4.2bn of leases, as FactSet's debt does; the company's leverage measure (about 2.4x) is below FactSet's trailing 2.61x. Whether the pension zero-coupon bonds will be included in reported net debt is not stated; we assume not.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Earnings per share are BT's adjusted basic EPS in GBP (18.3p, 19.3p, 21.4p, 23.3p, 25.3p), including TalkTalk; excluding TalkTalk they are 18.9p, 20.0p, 21.4p, 22.9p, 24.7p. Free cash flow is the company's normalised free cash flow, including TalkTalk's trading losses (core FY2027 about 1,995 against the about 2.0bn guide). It is built from EBITDA, cash capital expenditure, lease principal, cash interest (net financial items plus about 130 of non-cash charges), cash tax (about 90, 250, 250, 250, 300) and the working-capital flows above, plus about 50 of copper proceeds in FY2027. A model that deducts the profit and loss tax charge and the full net financial items will show free cash flow lower than ours by about 0.57bn (FY2027: tax about 0.44bn above cash tax, interest 0.13bn), about 0.45bn (FY2028), 0.51bn (FY2029), 0.59bn (FY2030) and 0.59bn (FY2031); that difference is definitional, not a disagreement. Shareholder cash after pension, restructuring, TalkTalk, spectrum and the Verizon receipt is far lower than normalised free cash flow: about 0.48bn (FY2027), 1.49bn, 1.59bn, 2.08bn, 2.42bn before dividends of about 0.83bn, 0.85bn, 0.88bn, 0.92bn, 0.95bn. Net debt including leases: about 20.3bn (March 2027), 19.7bn, 19.0bn, 17.8bn, 17.0bn; net debt to EBITDA 2.50x, 2.39x, 2.25x, 2.07x, 1.96x.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 23 November 2026, 15:09 to 15:35 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
BT Group plc (BT.A-GB): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and how far they are reflected in guidance, estimates and the narrative

Written 23 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Money in pounds sterling; per-share figures in pence. Fiscal year ends 31 March; FY27 is the year to March 2027. "Core" means the continuing group excluding TalkTalk; "group" includes TalkTalk from 5 October 2026, as the second set modelled it. Probabilities are my judgement unless a source is named.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read this first: what the reader needs before the answers

1. The model's forecasts of record are too low in every year because of one input, which I have corrected. The one-page model summary shows earnings per share of 15p to 22p and revenue of £16.8bn to £17.6bn, against the second set's own 18.3p to 25.3p and £17.8bn to £18.6bn. The cause is arithmetic, not judgement. The second set stated FY27 revenue growth of -9.2% against FactSet's annual FY26 revenue of £19,646m. The model applies the year's growth to the sum of FactSet's two FY26 half-years, £18,536m (the first half restated without International, £8,696m, plus the second half still including it, £9,840m). So -9.2% produced £16,831m, about £1,000m (5.6%) below the intended £17,830m, and the error carried into every later year. The correct input on the model's base is -3.8%. It is in inputs-changed.md. With it, the model reproduces the second set's earnings per share.

2. I have also changed one judgement: depreciation and amortisation falls more slowly than the second set assumed. The second set had the charge falling from £4,715m (FY27) to £4,400m (FY31). I have £4,715m, £4,690m, £4,670m, £4,640m and £4,600m, which is the consensus path plus TalkTalk. The reasons are in D-01 and D-02; in short, the reported first half shows the charge still flat to rising, the second set compared capital expenditure with a depreciation figure that includes about £610m a year of lease depreciation, and the build-era assets stay in the depreciation base into the 2030s.

3. The forecasts after both changes (my arithmetic; the Financial Analyst's updated model is the figure of record).

FY27 FY28 FY29 FY30 FY31
Revenue, group (£m) 17,832 18,153 18,316 18,481 18,610
Adjusted EBITDA, group (£m) 8,149 8,241 8,425 8,594 8,710
Depreciation and amortisation (£m) 4,715 4,690 4,670 4,640 4,600
Adjusted earnings per share, group (p) 18.3 19.0 20.6 22.0 about 23.5
Adjusted earnings per share, core (p) 18.9 19.7 20.6 21.6 about 22.9
Consensus earnings per share (median, excludes TalkTalk) (p) 18.4 19.5 20.0 22.4 (one broker) none
Group against consensus -0.4% -2.4% +2.8% -1.8%
Second set's earnings per share, for reference (p) 18.3 19.3 21.4 23.3 25.3
Normalised free cash flow, company definition, unchanged (£m) 1,945 2,222 2,709 3,053 3,243

Earnings per share now grow about 6% a year from FY27 to FY31 for the group and about 5% a year for the core, not about 8%. The earnings forecast is within 3% of consensus in every year. The remaining differences against consensus are in free cash flow for FY29 and FY30 (about £0.2bn and £0.5bn above), in Openreach against Consumer and Business within a similar EBITDA total, and in TalkTalk, which consensus leaves out.

4. Three facts the earlier sets did not have, from the research written for this work.

  • The pension bonds are inside reported net debt. The zero-coupon notes were issued on 13 November 2026 by BT Finance plc under its listed note programme, are transferable, and will be reported as loans and borrowings; they count as scheme assets, so the pension deficit falls by the same amount (AlphaSense pension report, citing BT's interim documents). The second set assumed the opposite. On my arithmetic reported net debt including leases will be about £22.7bn at March 2027 (about 2.8 times EBITDA), not about £20.3bn (2.5 times), and will then fall by about £1.1bn to £1.7bn a year, because redeeming the notes no longer adds to net debt. The economics and the cash are unchanged; the headline at the May 2027 results is not. The accretion on the notes (about £40m in FY27, £85m in FY28, £65m in FY29, £40m in FY30, my arithmetic) will be a finance expense; whether BT puts it inside adjusted earnings or treats it as a specific item is not disclosed. If inside, it costs about 0.3p, 0.7p, 0.5p and 0.3p of adjusted earnings per share in FY27 to FY30. I have not changed the input; see D-04.
  • TalkTalk is probably not consolidated while the hold-separate order stands. Four of the AlphaSense reports, citing BT's interim report, its prospectus supplement of 6 November 2026 and the order itself, say BT does not yet control TalkTalk in the accounting sense and carries it as an investment, with a separate segment to follow after clearance; one of those reports also calls the signals conflicting. I have kept the second set's consolidation in the inputs, because the shareholder bears TalkTalk's losses either way. But the figures BT reports in May 2027 are likely to be the core: about £17.6bn of revenue, £8.19bn of EBITDA and 18.9p of earnings per share, which is the basis of guidance and of consensus.
  • The acquired intangibles from the EE purchase are almost fully amortised. Their carrying value fell from £224m to £44m in FY26. The charge was £180m in FY26, is at most £44m in FY27 and nil from FY28. BT does not exclude this charge from adjusted earnings. So about 1.1p of FY27 earnings per share, more than the whole of the guided "low-single-digit growth", is this roll-off, and about 0.3p of FY28. It is in the reported first half already and it cannot repeat.

5. The steer in one paragraph. The next 18 months of BT's financial forecasts are predictable at the level of EBITDA, capital expenditure and the dividend, moderately predictable for earnings per share (about ±8% for FY28) and least predictable for the one figure the market most wants, FY27 normalised free cash flow, which must be earned almost entirely in the second half. Our assessment is close to guidance and to consensus. The gap in our favour is narrow and sits in operations (line losses, Openreach, the top of the EBITDA range, FY29 and FY30 free cash flow). The gap against us sits in presentation (TalkTalk dilution when it enters estimates, the notes entering net debt, depreciation stickier than the most bullish broker has it, no buyback before about FY31). At 215.8p and about 11 times two-year-forward earnings, the market has paid for the de-risking of October and November; what remains is delivery.

6. Cautions on the sources.

  • The AlphaSense reports contain errors and stale figures; I have used a figure only where a second source or the arithmetic supports it. Examples: a labour target of 75,000 to 80,000 (superseded by 65,000 to 70,000); FY27 EBITDA guidance of £8.2bn to £8.3bn (the range before International was discontinued); first-half free cash flow given as both £236m and £247m (the company figure is £236m); the end of the TNT Sports minimum guarantee given as both July and October 2026.
  • The co-movement table is empty ("no price history found for BT.A-GB"), so D-07 has no measured correlation.
  • No investor commentary (fund letters, social media) was retrieved by the narrative report; I-03 rests on sell-side commentary, positioning data and price behaviour.

Scores: 1 to 100. For the third set, a higher number means the thing asked about is more positive for the predictability of the forecasts over the next 18 months. For the fourth set, a higher number means the gap between our assessment and what guidance, estimates and the narrative already reflect is more favourable.

Research requests for the next set

Company: BT Group plc (BT.A-GB), the UK's integrated fixed and mobile telecoms incumbent, reporting Openreach (the regulated national wholesale fixed-access network), Consumer (BT, EE and Plusnet broadband, mobile and TV), Business (UK enterprise, public-sector and small-business connectivity and IT), and TalkTalk with its wholesale unit PlatformX (acquired out of administration on 5 October 2026 and held separate under a Competition and Markets Authority pre-emptive action order); its International unit is a discontinued operation being contributed to a 50/50 joint venture with Verizon (completion expected in the second half of 2027).

Request: OWN-1

Question: For BT Group plc (BT.A-GB), following the issue on 13 November 2026 by BT Finance plc, under its Euro Medium Term Note programme (prospectus supplement dated 6 November 2026), of zero-coupon notes to the BT Pension Scheme co-investment vehicle under the triennial funding agreement announced on 5 November 2026 (company presentation of 5 November 2026: notes maturing at £590m in each of April 2027, April 2028 and April 2029, £490m in April 2030 and £420m in September 2033): how will BT present these notes in its reported net debt, in its net debt to EBITDA measure and against its stated aim of credit metrics consistent with a BBB+ rating, and has the company restated or redefined any leverage measure or target since the issue; will the annual accretion on the notes be charged within adjusted net finance expense and adjusted earnings per share or be treated as a specific item alongside IAS 19 pension interest, and how large is the accretion in each of FY27 to FY31; how have Fitch, Moody's and S&P treated the notes and the reduced pension deficit in their adjusted leverage and in any rating commentary since 5 November 2026; what leverage level and what date do the company and each covering broker associate with reaching BBB+ metrics and with the start of share buybacks under the distribution thresholds and trustee-consultation terms of the pension agreement; and which brokers have changed their net debt, finance cost, earnings per share or shareholder distribution forecasts for BT as a result, and by how much? Name each source with its date, separate company statements from broker inference, and say what remains undisclosed and which publication would resolve it.

Request: OWN-2

Question: For BT Group plc (BT.A-GB), ahead of its trading update for the nine months to 31 December 2026 (expected in early February 2027) and its results for the year to 31 March 2027 (13 May 2027): what is the latest company-compiled consensus, or failing that each covering broker's own forecast, on the company's own definitions for FY27, FY28 and FY29, covering adjusted revenue, UK service revenue, adjusted EBITDA for the group and for Openreach, Consumer and Business, depreciation and amortisation, adjusted net finance expense, adjusted earnings per share, accrued and cash capital expenditure, normalised free cash flow, net debt, dividend per share, and Openreach broadband line losses by quarter and by year; for each broker, are the figures on the continuing basis without International, do they include TalkTalk and PlatformX, and is earnings per share adjusted or reported; what do brokers expect BT's guidance for FY28 to contain and at what levels, including whether TalkTalk will be inside it; how and when has BT said it will account for and report TalkTalk while the Competition and Markets Authority's order stands (consolidated in adjusted revenue and EBITDA, as a separate segment, or as an investment) and what has it disclosed about TalkTalk's revenue, profit and customer numbers since 5 October 2026; and what has each broker written since BT's half-year results of 5 November 2026 about the direction of its own estimates and the reasons for any change? Name every covering house identified and say which are included.

Request: OWN-3

Question: For BT Group plc (BT.A-GB): over the last six, twelve and twenty-four months, how closely have BT's share price and its forward price-to-earnings multiple moved with the European telecoms sector index, with named peers (Vodafone, Orange, Deutsche Telekom, Royal KPN, Telefonica) and with the UK ten-year gilt yield, and how much of BT's movement came on company-specific news, stating the share-price reaction on and after each of Ofcom's decision on Openreach's offers of 28 September 2026, the TalkTalk acquisition of 5 October 2026, the Competition and Markets Authority's provisional findings on nexfibre's purchase of Netomnia in October 2026, the reports of 30 October 2026 that Virgin Media O2 may use Openreach's network, and the half-year results and pension agreement of 5 November 2026; what do brokers and strategists say about BT's sensitivity to bond yields relative to its peers; and what do investors, as distinct from sell-side analysts, currently say and do about BT, including fund manager letters and interviews, statements by the main holders (Bharti, Deutsche Telekom), changes in the shareholder register, short interest and stock lending, hedge-fund positioning data, options pricing, and commentary on social media and investor forums; which of the debates on BT (the pension funding notes and net debt, TalkTalk and its regulatory review, Openreach line losses, the fall in capital expenditure and the rise in free cash flow, the timing of shareholder returns) do investors raise and which do they not? Date every piece of evidence and say where the evidence is thin.
